# Nobody Fools Archie Fairweather

### An Archie Fairweather Mystery

---


---

# Chapter 1

## Upside Down

Hanging upside down, Archie Fairweather had discovered, improved almost everything.

It improved the Merrow Opera House, for a start. Right side up, the old hall was a cold, high, honest room above the town office — three hundred folding wooden chairs, a pressed-tin ceiling, iron radiators that knocked like someone wanted to be let in, and tall arched windows full of gray October light off the harbor. Upside down, it became a place where anything could happen. The painted curtain behind him, an 1899 canvas of Merrow Harbor in high summer, hung the wrong way up in his eyes, so that its schooners sailed along the bottom of a blue sky and its gulls flew underneath the water.

It also improved the straitjacket. Right side up, a straitjacket was a canvas coat with very long sleeves and opinions about your elbows. Upside down, with his ankles locked into a padded harness and twelve feet of air between his head and the stage, it became a conversation, and Archie had always been good at conversations.

"One minute," said Lenny, from the rail.

"I heard you the first time."

"I didn't say it the first time. That was the first time."

Archie rolled his left shoulder, found the slack he'd stolen when Lenny buckled him in, and walked his right arm up across his own chest by about an inch. The blood was pooling in his face. His hair, which was too long and going silver at the temples and which he refused on principle to cut, hung toward the stage like the bristles of a broom.

The record was two minutes and twelve seconds. His grandfather had set it on this stage in 1961, in a jacket borrowed from the state hospital in Augusta, and there was a poster about it in the stairwell — THE GREAT FAIRWEATHER DEFIES THE ASYLUM! — that Archie touched for luck every time he climbed up here. In eight days, on Halloween night, he intended to break it in front of the whole town. Today was the first day he'd gotten anywhere close.

"One-fifteen," Lenny said.

Lenny Szabo was seventy, five foot six in his boots, and built like a fire hydrant that had given up on the world. He wore three sweaters because the opera house was cold, and a fourth one around his shoulders in case the other three let him down. He had built every illusion Archie had ever performed, in thirty years and on four continents, and he had never once said a word of praise about any of them, because, as he'd once explained, they were hinges. You didn't praise a hinge. You oiled it.

Archie got his right hand over his left shoulder and seized the strap at the back of his neck with two fingers.

"One-thirty."

"Lenny, I love you, but I need you to stop telling me the time."

"You told me to tell you the time."

"I've grown as a person."

That was when the doors at the back of the hall banged open, and a woman's voice said, "— and here's the hall itself. Mind the floorboard by the door, it's been loose since Carter was president."

Archie knew the voice. Hollis Tibbetts had run the Merrow Players for thirty years and taught English at the regional high school for thirty-five, and she spoke to everyone, including the select board and the weather, as if they had not done the reading. She had been the first person in Merrow to knock on his door in August. She'd brought him a blueberry pie and the opera house keys, in that order, and told him he looked like he needed both.

Behind her, upside down, came three people Archie had never seen before.

"Oh, honey," said the one in front. "Look at you."

She was a tall woman in a long velvet coat the color of red wine, with jet-black hair and a single streak of pure white running back from one temple. Her mouth was a dark, glossy red. There were rings on most of her fingers, big pale stones that caught the window light, and she had the kind of walk that turned a drafty town hall into a red carpet whether the town hall wanted it or not.

Archie had seen her face on the sides of buses, under the words *SHE TALKS TO THEM. THEY TALK BACK.* He'd also seen it on a folder on his desk in Las Vegas labeled CHALLENGE — NO RESPONSE.

"Mrs. Crane," he said. "You're early."

"Lorelei, please." She came down the center aisle toward the stage and stopped below him, tipping her head to look into his face. "And you're upside down. I'd say we're both doing what we do."

"One-forty-five," said Lenny.

"Is he all right?" asked the man behind her. He was in his forties, in a fleece vest and very white sneakers, with a phone in each hand, and he was already raising one of the phones to film. "Can we get this? Can I just — Lorelei, this is incredible, this is the cold open."

"No," said Lenny.

The man looked around for whoever had spoken. Lenny did not help him.

"Teddy Marchand," the man said to the room in general. "Marchand Media. We produce *The Other Side*." He said it with the confidence of a man who knew the ratings.

The third visitor said nothing at all. She was younger, late twenties, in an oversized gray sweater with a silk scarf knotted at the throat, her hair scraped back so tightly it looked like a decision. She stopped halfway down the aisle and didn't come any closer. Archie noticed her because she was the only one of the three who wasn't looking at his face. She was watching his hands.

Nobody watched his hands. That was the whole point of the face.

"My daughter," said Lorelei. "Ivy. She doesn't do small talk, so don't take it personally."

"I never take anything personally upside down," said Archie. "It all runs to my head."

"Two minutes," said Lenny.

Archie got the neck strap open. He felt the left sleeve go slack. Twelve seconds — he could still do it in twelve seconds, if he concentrated.

"Hollis," he said, without looking away from the medium. "Why are the dead people's representatives in my theater on a Thursday?"

"Because they're doing a walkthrough on Thursday," said Hollis, "and you were supposed to be at the hardware store. I told you on Tuesday." She was a short, sturdy woman with a white crop of hair, a men's cardigan, reading glasses on a beaded chain and fingerless gray wool gloves she wore from September to May. Her ring of keys, the size of a dinner plate, clanked at her hip. She did not look sorry.

"You told me they were *coming*. You didn't say they were coming *in*."

"How else would they get here?"

"And who," said Archie, "invited them?"

"I did." Hollis folded her arms. "Back in May, Archie. Before you'd ever set foot in Merrow, when this hall was on the list to be condemned and the town didn't have two nickels for the roof. I wrote to Mr. Marchand's company and told them we had the most haunted theater in New England and the thirty-seventh anniversary of the Great Fairweather's vanishing coming up on Halloween. Forty thousand dollars for the location. That's a roof." She looked up at the pressed-tin ceiling, where a long brown stain mapped the leaks of several decades. "You can be cross with me under a dry ceiling."

There was no arguing with a roof. Archie had tried, in his head, for about a month.

"Two-oh-five," said Lenny.

Lorelei Crane stepped up onto the first stair at the side of the stage so that her face was level with his. Up close she smelled of woodsmoke and something expensive. Her eyes were dark and amused and very steady.

"I know about your challenge, Mr. Fairweather," she said. "A million dollars to any medium who'll sit under your controls. You sent me the letter three times."

"Four."

"Four. I'm sorry. My people lose things." She smiled. "Bring your controls on Saturday. Bring all the controls you like."

That was the first moment Archie felt the floor of the conversation move. Nobody said yes to the controls. Not in thirty years, not one. They said the energy would be wrong. They said skeptics interfered with the spirits. They said, if they were honest, *no*.

"All of them?" he said.

"Why not?" Lorelei put one ringed hand flat against the canvas over his chest, lightly, the way you might touch a horse. "But you should know something first, honey. Your grandfather's here. He's been standing behind you this whole time." She tilted her head, listening to the air beside his ear. "He says you still haven't opened his trunk."

Archie stopped moving.

It wasn't a decision. His hands simply quit, in the middle of the left sleeve, with his elbow halfway across his own throat.

There was a trunk. It was in the turret room of his grandfather's house on Gull Point, a black steamer trunk with gold stars painted on it and seven brass letter-dials on the lock, and on its lid, under a square of old celluloid, a card in Gus Fairweather's handwriting: *To be opened when I'm gone. Not before.* Archie had tried eleven thousand and some words on those dials since August. He kept them in a notebook. He had not told anyone in Merrow about the notebook. He had barely told himself.

"Two-ten," said Lenny, in a slightly different voice.

Lorelei took her hand away. "He's proud of you," she said gently. "But he thinks you're looking in the wrong place."

Afterward, Archie could never say exactly how he got out. He knew the moves; he'd done them a thousand times. But there was a gap in his memory between the medium's hand leaving his chest and the moment the jacket fell past his face to the stage with a canvas slap, and in that gap something in him had simply refused to hang there and be read like a menu. He got the left arm free, the right arm, the crotch strap, and the jacket was gone.

"Time," he said, upside down and coatless.

Lenny looked at the stopwatch. He looked at it again.

"Two-eleven," he said.

Hollis Tibbetts made a short barking sound, which was how she laughed, and began to clap. After a moment Teddy Marchand joined in, one phone still raised. Ivy Crane did not. She was still looking at his hands.

"Let me down, Lenny."

Lenny let him down. Archie came out of the ankle harness on the stage boards in a crouch, stood up too fast, and had to wait for the blood to finish relocating before he could trust his face. When he could, he walked to the lip of the stage and looked down at Lorelei Crane, who had stepped back into the aisle and was waiting for him with every appearance of enjoying herself.

"That was beautiful," Archie said. "Truly. The trunk. You must have a very good researcher."

"I have a very good grandfather of yours."

"Here's my offer. The Fairweather Challenge, on Saturday, on this stage. My controls. My cameras next to yours. If one single thing happens at your séance that I can't show the audience how you did, I'll write a check for a million dollars to the charity of your choice. On the air."

Teddy Marchand made a small, happy noise.

Lorelei considered him, her head on one side. "Make it two," she said.

"Two million."

"Two million. And you don't stand in the corner with a flashlight, honey. You sit in the circle. Hands held, lights out, like everybody else. If you want to catch me, you have to be in the dark with me." She smiled her dark red smile. "Unless you're afraid of what you'll hear."

Archie had been afraid of a great many things in his life — sharks, opening nights, his mother — and had made a very good living doing every one of them anyway in front of a live studio audience. He felt the old grin arrive before his brain had finished voting.

"Saturday," he said. "Bring your grandfather. I'll bring mine."

He held out his hand. She shook it. Her rings were cold.

"Wonderful," said Teddy Marchand, typing with both thumbs. "That's — honestly, that's the special. That's the whole special."

They left the way they'd come, Hollis leading, talking about fire exits. At the doors, Ivy Crane looked back once — not at Archie's face, but at his right hand, the one that had shaken her mother's — and then she was gone down the stairs after the others, and the hall was cold and quiet again except for the knocking of the radiators.

Lenny began to coil the rope.

"You agreed to sit in the dark with her," he said.

"I did."

"For two million dollars."

"For charity."

"You agreed," Lenny said, "to sit in the dark with a woman who knows about the trunk."

Archie looked out at the hall. Up at the back, behind the balcony rail, the little plywood light booth stood dark. Behind him the painted harbor hung right side up again, its gulls back in the sky where they belonged. It didn't look like a place where anything could happen anymore. It looked like a room where someone had just told him a secret.

"Everybody in Merrow knows about the trunk," he said. "It's a small town."

Lenny finished coiling the rope and hung it on its cleat. "Then why'd you stop?"

Archie didn't have an answer for that. He went to pick the straitjacket up off the boards instead, and found, when he did, that his hands weren't entirely steady, and that he very much wanted to go home and look at a black steamer trunk with gold stars on it, and try one more word.


---

# Chapter 2

## The Trunk

The lobster's name was Harold, and that was the problem.

He had not been Harold when Archie bought him. At the co-op on the wharf that afternoon he had been one of forty lobsters in a tank, mottled green-black and anonymous, and Archie had pointed at the biggest one with all the confidence of a man who intended to make a triumphant dinner for his family. Somewhere on the drive home along Shore Road — the paper sack on the passenger seat of the hearse, rustling — he had started talking to it. By the time he set the sack on the kitchen counter at Fairweather House, the lobster had a name, a personality, and what Archie privately believed was a sense of humor.

"You can't name it," said June, without looking up from her homework. "That's the one rule."

"I didn't name him. He introduced himself."

"Dad."

The kitchen at Fairweather House was the only room in the place that had never been asked to do anything clever. Gus had built trapdoors into the pantry and a revolving bookcase into the library and a staircase off the second-floor landing that climbed eleven steps and stopped against the ceiling, but the kitchen was just a kitchen: big and square and warm, with a scarred farm table in the middle, a cast-iron range nobody could work, a modern range nobody could work either, and tall windows looking down the lawn to the rocks and the harbor. In the middle of the table, in a white enamel jug, stood a fistful of tall blue flowers Vivienne had cut from the garden that afternoon. At six o'clock on a late-October evening the windows were already black. Out past the point, every six seconds, Merrow Light swept a green flash across the water and was gone.

June sat at the end of the table in her school cardigan with three textbooks open and a pencil stuck through the bun of her dark hair. She was sixteen, and a junior at the regional high school twenty minutes inland, and she had been, for most of her life, the most sensible person in any room her father was in. She had his hands and her mother's freckles and a way of saying *Dad* that could mean nine different things depending on the weather.

Archie lifted Harold out of the sack. Harold raised both claws, which were banded shut, in what was either a threat or a blessing.

"Look at that," Archie said. "He's a showman."

"Put him in the pot."

"I'm going to."

"The water's been boiling for twenty minutes."

"I'm letting it get really ready."

June turned a page. "You did this with the turkey."

"The turkey was already dead. That was a different emotional situation." Archie carried Harold over to the stove, held him above the enormous pot, and looked at him. Harold looked back with small black eyes on stalks, which was more eye contact than Archie had gotten from most of the lobstermen in Merrow.

He put Harold in the sink instead, and ran a little cold water over him.

"Grilled cheese?" June said.

"Grilled cheese," Archie agreed.

The back door opened on a gust of cold air that smelled of salt and woodsmoke, and Lenny came in from the carriage house in all four sweaters, took one look at the steaming pot and the lobster in the sink, and said, "No."

"Nobody's asking you to eat him."

"Good." Lenny took the heel of a loaf of bread from the board and a piece of cheese from the refrigerator, built himself a sandwich standing up, and left again without another word. Lenny did not eat anything that came out of the sea. He said it was a matter of principle. Nobody had ever been able to find out which one.

Archie was buttering bread when his mother arrived.

Vivienne Fairweather never simply came into a room. She occurred in it. At seventy-three she had a silver bob cut sharp as a paper edge, cheekbones she'd been told about since 1966, coral lipstick, and tonight a purple kaftan under a man's tweed overcoat she'd taken from the Merrow Players' costume shop and had no intention of returning. She'd been in Merrow nine weeks. She already ran the costume shop, sang alto in the Congregational choir, and was being courted, with great formality and a thermos of coffee, by the harbormaster.

"Darling," she said, kissing June on the top of the head. "Archibald." She did not kiss Archie. She looked at the sink. "Why is there a lobster in the sink?"

"That's Harold," said June.

"Of course it is." Vivienne unwound a scarf. "I hear you bet two million dollars against Lorelei Crane this morning while dangling from the flies."

Archie put the butter knife down. "Who told you that?"

"Bev told Ozzie at the fuel dock, and Ozzie told me in the car. Hollis told Bev. I imagine the whole peninsula knew before you'd gotten your shoes back on." She sat down across from June and helped herself to one of June's grapes. "I've signed up."

"For what?"

"For Saturday. The séance." She said it the way other mothers might mention a book club. "The producer was very sweet about it. He said they'd been hoping for a family member. I'm to sit in the circle."

"I should have fought the town and canceled the whole thing in September."

"Hollis was quite right to talk you out of it. *Don't cancel her, Archie. Beat her. In Gus's house.*" Vivienne did Hollis's brisk classroom voice rather well. "I've never liked a woman more on short acquaintance." She touched one of the blue flowers in the jug. "She says hers are a foot taller than these, by the way. Dad's monkshood. He planted it along the fence for the name — wolfsbane, for Halloween. Hollis says the deer won't touch it."

"No," said Archie.

"Yes."

"Mother, no. Absolutely not. She's a professional. Do you understand what these people do? They find the softest place in you and they press on it until money comes out. She's going to sit in the dark in Gus's theater and pretend to be *Gus*, and she's going to do it to *you*, on television—"

"I know exactly what these people do," said Vivienne. "I was in the room when my father exposed eleven of them, Archibald. I held the flashlight." She ate the grape. "I'm going."

"I forbid it."

June made a small sound into her chemistry book.

Vivienne regarded her son with enormous patience. "You forbid it."

"I'm — strongly against it."

"Noted." She reached over and patted his hand. "I'm still going."

Archie turned to the stove so he wouldn't have to look at either of them, and put the first two sandwiches in the pan. Behind him he heard June close a textbook. That was never a good sign.

"Can I ask a question?" she said.

"You're going to anyway."

"Is it true you bet two million dollars?"

"It's for charity."

"Is it *your* two million dollars?"

"It won't come to that."

"That's not what I asked."

He flipped the sandwiches. "Yes, Junebug. If, by some miracle, Lorelei Crane produces a genuine paranormal phenomenon under scientific controls in front of the people of Merrow, Maine, I will write a check for two million dollars to whatever charity she names. And then I'll retire from magic and become a monk, because the universe will have stopped making sense."

"You already retired from magic."

"I'm not *performing*. I'm *debunking*. It's completely different. It's practically a public service."

"Will there be cameras?"

He hesitated a fraction too long, and June put her pencil down.

"Dad." This one meant: *You promised.*

He had. Last spring, in a hotel room in Boston, the night after her debate final — the one he'd finally made it to, after vanishing from his own show to get there — he had promised her a real year. One town. One school. One house with a kitchen where people ate dinner. No crews, no tours, no press. A regular year, she'd said, like regular people have, and he'd said yes before she'd finished the sentence, because there was nothing in the world he wouldn't have said yes to at that moment.

Nine weeks in, he had bought the town's opera house, booked himself a Halloween show, and bet two million dollars against a television psychic while hanging upside down.

"It's her cameras," he said. "Not mine. It's one night. I'll sit in a chair, I'll hold some hands, I'll catch her lifting a bell with her foot, and then it's over and nobody in this town will ever have to hear the word *séance* again. No circus. I promise."

June looked at him for a long moment with her mother's level eyes.

"Okay," she said, and opened her textbook again, and he could tell that she didn't believe him and had decided to let him find that out for himself.

The grilled cheese was good. Harold spent dinner in the sink, waving occasionally.

---

Later, when June had gone up to bed and Vivienne had gone out on the porch to say goodnight to Ozzie on the phone at some length, Archie climbed the narrow stairs to the turret.

The turret room had been Gus's study. It was round, with windows on every side, and on a clear night you could see the whole harbor from it — the moorings, the wharf lights, the dark hump of Shag Ledge and the bell buoy beyond, Merrow Light flashing green at the end of the point. Archie had left almost everything the way he'd found it in August: the rolltop desk, the shelves of conjuring books, the framed posters of the Great Fairweather sawing ladies in half and escaping from milk cans and holding a live dove in each hand with an expression of profound personal dignity.

And the trunk.

It sat against the wall under the north window, black, brass-cornered, painted with faded gold stars, THE GREAT FAIRWEATHER stenciled on the side. The lock was something Gus had built himself: seven small brass dials in a row, each one stamped with the alphabet. On the lid, under a yellowed square of celluloid, was the card.

*To be opened when I'm gone. Not before. — G.F.*

Archie sat down on the floor in front of it, the way he had almost every night since August, and took the notebook out of his back pocket.

He had tried names. He had tried places. He had tried every seven-letter word he could think of that might have meant something to a magician born in Merrow in 1925 — MYSTERY, ILLUSION, VANISHED, which was eight letters and which he'd tried twice anyway out of spite. He'd tried his grandmother's name and his mother's name spelled backward and the name of Gus's first rabbit. He had tried PRESTO and ABRACADABRA, and felt ashamed of himself both times.

He'd brought the opera cloak up with him to air out. It was Gus's — black wool, lined in red silk that had gone the color of old wine, with a high collar and a silver clasp — and Archie meant to wear it for the Halloween show. He shook it out now and folded it without thinking: lining out, shoulders tucked in, then in thirds, collar on top, the clasp centered. He laid it on the lid of the trunk.

"Just like Dad," said his mother from the doorway.

He hadn't heard her on the stairs. She came in and stood looking at the cloak, her arms crossed over the tweed coat.

"Like Gus what?"

"Folded it. Lining out, shoulders in, thirds. He never once left it in a heap, not even after a three-show day. He said a cloak in a heap was the sign of a man who'd given up." She sat down on the edge of the rolltop desk chair. "You were too small to remember him doing it."

"Apparently I remember."

"Apparently you do."

For a while neither of them said anything. Down in the harbor the bell buoy was ringing, faint and irregular, which meant a little swell was running.

"She told me he says I haven't opened it," Archie said finally. "The trunk. This morning. Before I'd said a word about it."

He'd expected his mother to look shaken. Instead she looked amused, and very slightly sorry for him.

"Darling, everybody in Merrow knows about the trunk."

"Do they?"

"It was in the paper. The *Merrow Packet* ran a whole page after he went — 'The Great Fairweather's Final Mystery,' with a photograph of that trunk right there under the window and the card on the lid. Half the people in this town have it in a scrapbook. If that woman has anyone working for her with a library card, she knew about it before she ever left California."

Archie looked at the trunk. It was obvious, now that she'd said it. It was so obvious that it was embarrassing. He'd been reading people professionally for thirty years, and he knew that the most powerful thing a cold reader can say is something that sounds private and is actually public. He had explained this on national television. He had a whole routine about it.

He found he didn't feel any better.

"She said I was looking in the wrong place," he said.

"Well, you are trying VANISHED again," said his mother, glancing at the notebook. "Which has eight letters."

"I was making a point."

Vivienne stood and put one hand on his hair as she passed, as she had when he was small, and he let her.

"He said when he's gone," she said from the doorway. "Not before. Nobody's opened it in thirty-seven years, Archibald." She shrugged. She had been shrugging on stages since the Johnson administration, and she was very good at it. "Draw your own conclusions."

Then she was gone down the stairs, and he heard her laugh at something on the second-floor landing — Ozzie, still on the phone — and a door close.

Archie sat on the floor for a long time. Out on the point, Merrow Light flashed and went dark, flashed and went dark.

At five minutes past one in the morning, because he was tired and because it was seven letters and because he couldn't help himself, he spun the dials to L-O-R-E-L-E-I and pulled the hasp.

Nothing.

He sat back against the desk, oddly relieved, and listened to the bell buoy, and thought about a dark red smile and a ringed hand laid flat on his chest, and a voice saying, very gently, that he was looking in the wrong place.


---

# Chapter 3

## The Liars' Table

The Galley opened at five, and by six-thirty on Friday morning every stool at the counter was taken and Archie Fairweather had been ignored by the same three lobstermen for the fifty-ninth day in a row.

He kept count. He had a system.

The diner sat at the head of the town wharf, a low gray-shingled building with a long counter, six booths along the harbor windows, a pie case lit like a jewelry store, and a coffee urn the size of a lobster pot. Outside, the sky over the water was still dark, going to pewter at the edge. Inside, it was warm and loud and smelled of bacon and wet wool. A bell over the door rang every time someone came in out of the cold, and every time it rang, half the room looked up to see who it was and the other half didn't need to.

The round table by the kitchen door was the liars' table. It belonged to three men in their seventies who had fished out of Merrow since before Archie was born — Elwin Coombs, Dickie Thibodeau, and a narrow, silent man everybody called Pickle for reasons nobody would tell him. They sat there from five until the light came up, drinking coffee and saying things about the weather that Archie had come to believe were in code.

"Morning, gentlemen," Archie said, passing them with his mug.

Elwin looked at the window. Dickie looked at his eggs. Pickle looked at nothing at all, with great concentration.

Fifty-nine.

"Sit down," said Bev Thibodeau from behind the counter. "You're letting the heat out just standing there."

Bev owned the Galley. She was sixty-seven, with forearms like a stevedore, reading glasses pushed up into gray hair, and a flat Down East voice that could make *good morning* sound like a ruling from the bench. In nine weeks, Archie had tried flattery on her, and generosity, and card tricks, and a sincere and detailed appreciation of her blueberry pie. She had accepted the money for the pie.

He took the last stool, between Chief Dale Pinkham and the register.

"Archie," said the chief, glowing. "Heard about yesterday."

Dale Pinkham was the entire Merrow Police Department, if you didn't count a part-timer and a young officer named Kayla, and most people in Merrow didn't. He was sixty-three, with a walrus mustache and a uniform jacket that had stopped closing around the middle sometime during the Obama administration. He owned every one of Archie's television specials on DVD. He had opinions about the riverboat.

"Two million dollars," the chief said. "Upside down."

"It was mostly upside down."

"Ayuh. That's what I heard." He leaned in. "So how does she do it?"

It wasn't only the chief asking. Archie could feel it move down the counter and through the booths — a little lift of heads, a slowing of forks. For two months Merrow had been polite to him the way Maine is polite to people from away: carefully, and from a distance. Now, for the first time, the whole room wanted something he had.

He couldn't help it. He never could.

"Bev," he said. "I'm going to need your hand."

"You are not."

"Thirty seconds. For science."

Bev looked at him over her glasses for a long moment. Then she wiped her hands on her apron, came around the end of the counter, and put out her right hand like a woman offering it to be fingerprinted. A small cheer went up from the second booth.

"Chief, your left. Now — everybody at a séance holds hands, right? In a circle. That's the rule. That's how everybody knows the medium can't be ringing bells or floating tambourines, because both her hands are being held." Archie took Bev's hand in his left and the chief's in his right. "Close your eyes. Both of you."

The chief closed his eyes immediately. Bev closed hers like she was doing him a personal favor.

"Now. In the dark, the medium says the spirits are restless and the circle needs to tighten up. So she shifts her hands a little —" he brought them closer, murmuring, guiding Bev's fingers, adjusting the chief's wrist "— there, that's better, everybody comfortable — and you both feel a hand, and you both think it's mine."

He took his right hand away. Neither of them moved.

Then, with that free hand, he picked up a cold spoon off the counter and laid it against the back of the chief's neck.

Dale Pinkham shot about four inches off his stool and opened his eyes. So did Bev. They looked down and discovered they were holding each other's hands, and that Archie had his right arm in the air, waving the spoon.

The Galley exploded. Even Dickie Thibodeau, at the liars' table, made a sound that might have been a cough.

Bev took her hand back from the chief and looked at it.

"Well," she said, "that's disgusting," and went back behind the counter.

"The hand release," said Archie. "Every medium since 1850. Half the time the person next to her is in on it. The bells are on a stick. The cold touches are a wet glove. The voices —"

"What about the trumpet?" someone called from the booths. "She does a trumpet on her show. It floats around and the dead people talk out of it."

"Page one-twelve," said a voice from the corner booth.

Hollis Tibbetts was sitting alone by the window with the *Merrow Packet* folded to the crossword, a mug of tea and a plate of toast in front of her, and her reading glasses on the end of her nose. She didn't look up.

Before Archie could open his mouth, three people in three different booths said, in ragged unison, "*Lips to the small end!*"

The room roared. Archie looked around, lost.

"Mrs. Tibbetts's séance unit," explained the woman in the second booth, wiping her eyes. "Senior English. Everybody had to do it. Gus's book, Houdini, the Fox sisters—"

"'How the Grieving Are Fooled,'" said a young man at the counter, in a fair imitation of a schoolteacher. "Five-page paper. Typed."

"Double-spaced," said Hollis, filling in a word. "And you got a C-plus, Corey, because you spelled 'medium' with two *e*'s."

"Lips right on it?" said Chief Pinkham, who had evidently gone to school before the unit existed. "In the dark? That's not sanitary."

That got the biggest laugh of the morning, and Archie let the chief have it.

The bell over the door rang. A gust of cold came in, and with it a man in a camel overcoat and a red bow tie, holding a leather portfolio under one arm.

Warren Gilley was sixty-one, chair of the select board and the third Gilley to run Gilley Insurance & Realty on Main Street, with a tidy gray haircut, a tidy gray mustache, and the look of a man who had walked in on a party he wouldn't have attended. In September, at a special town meeting, he had spent twenty minutes at the microphone explaining why Merrow should sell its crumbling opera house to a Boston developer who wanted eighteen luxury condominiums. Archie had then offered the town more money and a deed restriction keeping it a theater forever. The vote had been 312 to 41. Warren had not forgiven him.

"Coffee, Bev. To go." Warren set his portfolio on the counter and looked down it at Archie. "I gather you've been entertaining."

"Educating."

"Mm." Warren accepted his coffee. "Since I'll be sitting in that circle on Saturday as the town's representative, I'll say this once, here, in front of witnesses. That sale doesn't close until the fifteenth of November. Until it does, the opera house belongs to the people of Merrow, and the select board will take a very dim view of this town being made into a laughingstock on national television by anyone." He paused. "Anyone at all."

"Warren," said Archie pleasantly, "the town hired her. I'm just the fella who's going to catch her."

"I'm sure that's how it seems from where you're standing."

"What's she gonna do, Warren?" Elwin Coombs said suddenly, from the liars' table — the first words Archie had ever heard him address to anyone outside it. "Bring back some drowned fella to tell us the price of bait?"

The room laughed again, louder, and Warren Gilley's ears went pink. He picked up his portfolio and his coffee and left without another word, and the bell rang behind him, and somebody in the booths said *Ayuh, there he goes*, and Archie thought: *I won that.* He felt good about it for nearly three seconds.

Then he saw the waitress.

She was at the end of the counter with the coffee pot in her hand — a woman in her thirties with red-gold hair pulled back in a ponytail and a Galley apron over a gray thermal shirt. Her name was Shannon; he knew that much. She'd poured his coffee every morning since August and called him *hon* exactly the way she called everybody *hon*. She had a wedding ring on, and she had stopped pouring with the pot held out over an empty mug, and her face had gone very still.

She put the pot down on the warmer. She took off her apron and hung it on its hook. Then she went out through the kitchen, and Archie heard the back door bang.

Bev was watching the kitchen door. She looked at Archie, once, and didn't say anything at all, which was worse than anything she could have said.

He left a twenty on the counter and went after her.

---

The back door of the Galley opened onto the wharf itself: wide gray planks, lobster crates stacked six high, coils of pot warp, the smell of bait from the shed. The sky over the harbor had gone from pewter to a thin cold pink, and the moorings were full of lobster boats swinging on their lines, white hulls and green ones, their names on their sterns — *Second Wind*, *Miss Dottie*, *Three Daughters*. A gull stood on a piling and screamed at nothing.

Shannon had walked all the way to the end of the wharf, where it stopped above the water. There was a block of granite there, waist-high and the size of a bank safe, with a bronze plaque on top — MERROW REMEMBERS — and on its seaward face, row after row of names cut into the stone. Archie had walked past it a dozen times. He'd never stopped to read it.

He stopped now, a few feet behind her, and read it.

The oldest names, at the top, were weathered almost smooth: 1847, 1851, a whole family in 1898. Farther down they were sharper. *Arthur Coombs 1978. Robert & James Haskell 1991. Peter Langley 2003. Dennis Pinkham 2012.* And at the bottom, alone, a name cut so recently that the granite inside the letters was still pale and new, like a scrape on a knee.

*THOMAS MAYO.*

"February," Shannon said, without turning around. "Off Monhegan. The boat went down in about four minutes. That's what the Coast Guard said. About four minutes." She had her arms wrapped around herself in the thin thermal shirt. "Tommy. He was thirty-five. We've got two kids."

"I'm sorry," Archie said. "I didn't know."

"Everybody knows. Everybody in there knows." She jerked her head back at the Galley. "Elwin went to the service."

Archie took off his coat, the red-and-black buffalo-check wool he'd bought in Camden on his first day in Maine, and held it out. After a second she took it and put it around her shoulders without looking at him.

"I'm in the circle," she said. "Saturday."

He'd guessed. It landed anyway.

"Her people messaged me in the spring. On Facebook. They said Lorelei had felt something — a strong presence near the water, a man who wanted to reach his wife — and would I like a free reading. On the phone." She laughed, without any humor in it at all. "I almost deleted it. I thought it was a scam."

"Shannon—"

"He came through." She turned around then, and her eyes were red and dry and furious. "She knew things. About Tommy. About the boat, and the kids, and what he called me. She knew he hated the cold. He *said* things, through her. He told me he wasn't cold anymore." Her voice cracked on it, and she pushed straight through the crack. "I've had three readings. The first one was free. The others were three-fifty. I borrowed it against the insurance. And she said on Saturday, in the theater, with all of you there, there'd be something special. For me. From him."

Archie had spent thirty years explaining exactly how this worked. A researcher, an obituary, a widow's public posts, the boat's name, the funeral notice in the *Packet*. Free the first time, because the first time was the hook. He could have drawn her a diagram on a napkin.

He didn't say any of it.

"So you all go on in there," Shannon said, "and laugh about drowned fellas and the price of bait. You hang upside down and bet your millions. But don't you *dare* make fun of it. Not to me. He talks to me. You don't get to take that away just because you know how to do card tricks."

The gull screamed again. Out past the moorings, the bell buoy rang once, faint and far, and then was quiet.

"I won't make fun of you," Archie said. "I promise."

It came out plainer than he meant it to. There was no patter in it at all. Shannon looked at him for a long moment, as though she was checking it for a trick, and he let her look.

Then she took off his coat, handed it back, and walked past him down the wharf toward the Galley's back door. She had six booths and a counter full of people waiting for coffee, and she was going to go and pour it.

Archie stood alone at the end of the wharf with his coat over his arm and the cold coming straight through his shirt. He read the bottom name on the granite again, and thought about a dark red smile, and a free reading, and three hundred and fifty dollars borrowed against a dead man's insurance.

He'd agreed to the séance yesterday because it was a game, and because she'd known about the trunk, and because he had never in his life been able to walk away from a dare.

He was going to it now for an entirely different reason.


---

# Chapter 4

## Sea View

On Friday afternoons, Archie Fairweather did magic for people who would not remember it.

Sea View sat on the hill above town, a long white 1920s summer hotel with green shutters and a porch full of rocking chairs that nobody rocked in after Labor Day. Inside, it had become a memory-care residence — locked doors with keypads, a nurses' station where the front desk used to be, a smell of coffee and lemon cleaner and, faintly, of the sea. The common room had been the hotel's ballroom. It still had the tall windows, and on a bright cold October afternoon like this one, the light came off the bay in sheets and lay across the old maple floor like something spilled.

He'd started coming in September. Nobody had asked him to. He'd been walking past one afternoon and seen through the windows a room full of people sitting very still in front of a television showing a cooking competition, and he'd gone in and asked the woman at the nurses' station if he could do a few card tricks, and she'd looked at him for a long moment and said, *You're him, aren't you,* and then, *Are there going to be cameras?* There weren't. There never were. That was the rule, and it was the only rule in his life he had never once broken.

Today there were eleven residents in the semicircle of armchairs, an aide named Marisol doing a word search by the window, and a piano nobody played.

"Pick a card," Archie said to a tiny woman in a pink cardigan. "Any card. Don't let me see it."

She picked one, studied it with tremendous seriousness, and showed it to the man beside her, who announced, "Seven of hearts," to the room.

"Thank you, Raymond," said Archie. "Very helpful."

Everybody laughed except Raymond, who didn't know why everybody was laughing, and then laughed anyway because they were. That was the thing Archie had learned in this room. It didn't matter if they followed the trick. It mattered that for a few minutes something was happening, and they were the audience it was happening for.

He put the seven of hearts back in the deck, shuffled, and asked the tiny woman to say her husband's name.

"Bernard," she said, without hesitation, and then her face clouded. "He's not here. He went to get the car."

"Then we'll do it for Bernard." Archie fanned the deck face down. "Tap one."

She tapped one. He turned it over: the seven of hearts. Across the back of it, in ballpoint, in handwriting that was very clearly not Archie's, someone had written *BERNARD*.

The tiny woman put both hands over her mouth.

Down at the end of the semicircle, a different small woman — younger than the others, in her early sixties, with neat gray hair and a cardigan buttoned wrong — had begun to cry, very quietly, without any change in her face. Marisol got up from the window and went to her.

"Is Warren coming?" the woman asked. "Is it Tuesday? He comes on Tuesday."

"It's Friday, Carol," said Marisol gently. "He'll be here Tuesday."

"He went to get the car."

"I know, hon."

Archie watched them for a moment, and then went on with the next trick, a little louder, because sometimes that helped. He didn't know Carol's story. He didn't know most of their stories. He knew a few of their names, and which ones liked the cards and which ones liked the coins, and that the big man in the wheelchair by the piano liked best of all to talk about Archie's grandfather.

"Gus," said Walt Calloway, as Archie came over. "Gus Fairweather."

"That's right, Chief."

"You're his boy."

"His grandson. Archie."

"Archie." Walt nodded slowly. He was seventy-four, and had been the police chief of Merrow for twenty-nine years, and even sitting down he was enormous: broad shoulders under a plaid flannel shirt, a big square head with white hair cut short, and hands like catcher's mitts that shook a little now, all the time, on the arms of the chair. His eyes were blue and very clear, and when they focused on you, it was like being looked at by a lighthouse. When they didn't, he was somewhere else. Usually 1988.

"He did the Chair," said Walt. "The last time. At the opera house."

"I was there," said Archie. "Second row."

"I was in the back. I always stood in the back. In case." Walt's big hand moved on the chair arm. "He came out after. In the cloak. He shook my hand." The blue eyes went somewhere past Archie's shoulder, to the window, to the bay. "Gus Fairweather still owes me a boat."

Archie laughed. He'd heard Walt say a lot of things about Gus over the past six Fridays — that Gus could hold his breath longer than any man alive, that Gus had once made a police cruiser vanish from the lot behind the town hall as a birthday present for the dispatcher — and this was a new one. "Does he? I'll see what I can do."

"He knows," said Walt, and closed his eyes for a moment, and opened them again, and was back. "Show me one."

Archie showed him one. He did the thing with the three coins that turn into four and then into two, slowly, and Walt watched with his lips moving, trying to catch it, and didn't, and grunted with satisfaction. Archie was shuffling for the next one when the door from the hall opened and a woman came in fast, pulling off her gloves.

He knew she was a cop before he knew anything else about her. It was the way she came through the door and had the whole room in about a second and a half — exits, aide, residents, him — without moving her head. She was in her late thirties, tall, with dark-blond hair in a single braid down her back, a charcoal wool coat over a navy fleece, jeans and good boots. No jewelry but a plain steel watch. Gray eyes.

The gray eyes landed on him and stayed there.

"Dad," she said, without taking them off Archie. "It's Tess."

"Tessie." Walt's whole face changed. "You're early. Is school out?"

Something went across her face, too quick to read, and was gone. "School's out," she said, and came and bent and kissed the top of his head, and then straightened up and looked at Archie across her father's wheelchair as though he was something that had been left on the floor.

"Can I talk to you?" she said. "In the hall."

"I'm in the middle of a—"

"In the hall."

He went into the hall. She followed and shut the door behind her and stood with her back to it, and he understood that this was a woman who was used to standing in front of doors.

"I'm Detective Sergeant Calloway," she said. "Maine State Police. That's my father."

"Archie Fairweather." He put out his hand. She looked at it until he took it back. "Your father's a great guy. He's been telling me about my grandfather. He says Gus owes him a boat."

"How long have you been coming here?"

"Since September. Fridays."

"Who set it up?"

"Nobody. I walked in."

"Who's filming it?"

"Nobody's filming it."

"Mr. Fairweather." Her voice was very level. "I know who you are. Everybody in Maine knows who you are. You walked out of your own show in Las Vegas and turned up in my hometown and bought the opera house and you've been in the *Portland Press Herald* three times since August. My father has Alzheimer's. Most days he doesn't know what year it is. He thinks I'm fifteen. He is not a — prop. He's not a heartwarming segment. If there's a camera crew somewhere in this building and I find it, I'm going to—"

"There's no camera," said Archie.

She looked at him for a long moment. Then, without a word, she walked past him back into the common room, and he watched through the glass in the door as she went around it — methodically, corner by corner. She checked the window seats. She checked behind the piano. She checked the coat rack and the bookshelf and the top of the television, and she said something to Marisol, who shook her head and looked offended.

She came back out.

"There's no camera," she said.

"I told you."

"People tell me things all day." She put her gloves in her coat pocket. "Why do you come here?"

He thought about giving her the answer he gave reporters, which was charming and fifteen seconds long and true enough. Instead he found himself saying, "Because nobody here cares who I am. They don't know. They don't remember from week to week. Every Friday I walk in and I'm just a fella with a deck of cards." He shrugged. "Do you know how long it's been since anybody looked at me and didn't know the ending?"

She didn't answer. Behind her, through the glass, Walt Calloway was patting the pockets of his flannel shirt with his big shaking hands, looking for something.

"Excuse me," said Archie. "I think your dad wants his trick."

He went back in. Tess Calloway followed him and stood by the wall with her arms folded.

"Chief," said Archie, crouching beside the wheelchair, "I need to borrow something from your daughter. Something every police officer always has on them." He held up one hand and snapped his fingers in the air. "Sergeant, could you check your pen?"

She didn't move. Then, slowly, she put two fingers into the inside pocket of her coat. Her face did not change, which he had to admire. The pocket was empty.

"Chief," said Archie, "would you check your shirt?"

Walt fumbled at his breast pocket. It took him a moment. Then his big trembling fingers came out holding a black ballpoint pen, MAINE STATE POLICE stamped along the side in gold, and he held it up to the light and looked at it, and at his daughter, and at Archie.

And Walt Calloway laughed.

It came up out of him like something that had been stuck in a pipe for a long time — a huge, startled, rumbling laugh that filled the old ballroom and made the tiny woman in pink jump and then start laughing too. Raymond laughed. Marisol, at the window, laughed. Walt sat there in his wheelchair with the pen in his hand and laughed until there were tears in his clear blue eyes, and said, "Gus used to do that. Gus used to do that to me," and laughed some more.

Archie looked at Tess Calloway.

She was standing very still by the wall. She was watching her father. Her arms had come uncrossed, and one hand was pressed flat against the front of her coat, and her face was doing something she clearly hadn't given it permission to do.

Then she saw him looking, and it stopped.

She came over, took the pen gently out of her father's hand — "Thanks, Dad" — and put it back in her pocket. On the way past Archie, without looking at him, she said, very low, "Do that again and I'll arrest you for theft of state property."

"Noted."

"I'm serious."

"I know. That's the best part."

She gave him a look that should have frozen the bay. Then she sat down in the empty armchair next to her father and took his big hand in both of hers, and Archie went back to the semicircle and did three more tricks, and when he finally looked over again she was talking to Walt about the Red Sox and Walt was answering, and it was clearly 1994 for both of them, and neither of them minded.

She caught him at the door when he was leaving.

"The séance," she said. "Tomorrow night. At the opera house."

"You heard."

"Everybody heard. My lieutenant heard in Augusta." She held out a business card between two fingers. "I'm going to say this once. Don't make it a circus."

"It's already a circus, Sergeant. It's got a medium, a TV crew, a ghost and a two-million-dollar bet. I'm just the guy bringing the elephant."

She didn't smile. She didn't put the card away either.

"For when somebody gets hurt," she said.

He took it. *Det. Sgt. Theresa Calloway, Major Crimes Unit,* and a cell number. He put it in his breast pocket, and she watched him do it, and then she turned on her heel and went back into the common room without another word.

Walt Calloway was watching from his wheelchair by the piano. As Archie lifted a hand to say goodbye, the old chief beckoned him over, and Archie went.

"That's my daughter," Walt said, with enormous pride, pointing across the room at Tess, who was arguing with the TV remote. "Tessie. She's fifteen."

Archie looked at her: thirty-eight, State Police, braid, boots, the thousand-yard stare of a woman who checked behind pianos for cameras.

"She's terrifying, Chief," he said.

Walt Calloway nodded slowly, satisfied.

"Ayuh," he said.


---

# Chapter 5

## The Sign-Out Card

The Merrow Public Library closed at five on Fridays, and at half past four Deb Coombs was already looking at the clock over the circulation desk as though it owed her money.

It was a small granite building on Elm Street with a green copper dome and a brass plaque that said 1911, and inside it was all oak and radiator heat and the vanilla smell of old paper. There was one long reading room with tall windows, going blue now as the afternoon gave up; a children's corner with a rag rug and a stuffed moose; a microfilm machine under a dust cover; and, behind the circulation desk, a single glass-fronted bookcase with a hand-lettered card taped to it: FAIRWEATHER COLLECTION — LOCAL HISTORY — ASK AT DESK.

Archie asked at the desk.

"No," said Deb Coombs.

She was in her sixties, small and square, with cropped gray hair, half-moon glasses, and a cardigan the color of a tennis ball. She'd been the Merrow librarian for twenty-six years. Her husband was Elwin Coombs's nephew, which meant, as far as Archie could work out, that she was related to a third of the liars' table and disapproved of all of them.

"I haven't said what I want yet," said Archie.

"You want Gus's book. Everybody in town's been in for it since yesterday. The answer is in-library use only, it's the only copy north of Portland, the binding's going, and I don't care whose grandson you are."

"That's fair."

"I know it's fair. I made the rule."

"How about if I just look at it here? At a table. Under supervision."

Deb considered him over the half-moons. She had the look of a woman who had been bargained with by every sixth-grader in Merrow for a quarter of a century, and had won.

"What's in it for the library?" she said.

Archie leaned on the desk. "What does the library want?"

It turned out the library wanted a great many things — a new roof on the dome, a second microfilm reader, the stuffed moose cleaned, a Wi-Fi signal that reached the back of the reading room — and Archie, who had never in his life been able to resist a list of things somebody wanted, said yes to the Wi-Fi before she'd finished, and was reaching for his checkbook when she held up one hand.

"Not money," said Deb. "Everybody in this town thinks you'll fix everything with money, and you probably can, and that's what worries me. Second Saturday in November. Story hour. Ten a.m., children's corner." She pointed at the rug and the moose. "You'll read what I give you, you'll do one trick, and you will not saw anybody in half."

"Deal," said Archie, and meant it so much that she looked faintly alarmed.

She took a key from a drawer, unlocked the glass case, and drew out a thin hardback in a faded green cloth binding. The spine said *THE DEAD DON'T WHISPER* in gilt letters that had mostly worn away. Underneath, smaller: *AUGUSTUS FAIRWEATHER*.

Archie took it in both hands.

He'd read it as a boy — twice, three times — sitting on the floor of the turret room on summer afternoons while Gus worked at the rolltop desk. He'd had his own copy in Las Vegas, signed. But he hadn't seen this edition, the town's edition, since he was ten years old, and the weight of it in his hands was the weight of a summer he'd mostly lost track of.

"Reading table," said Deb. "Not the window. The radiator under the window leaks."

He took it to the long oak table in the middle of the reading room and sat. The first page had a library stamp — MERROW PUBLIC LIBRARY, a little anchor in the middle — and a bookplate in Gus's hand: *For the people of Merrow, who were never fooled for long. G.F., 1971.*

On the inside back cover there was a manila pocket, and in the pocket there was a card.

He slid it out without really meaning to. Archie had always had trouble leaving a pocket unexplored. It was the old kind of library card, buff-colored, ruled in faint blue lines, with columns for DATE DUE and BORROWER'S NAME. Most libraries had thrown these out decades ago. Deb, evidently, had not.

The card was nearly empty. In more than fifty years, it seemed, Gus's book had left this building only a handful of times.

*H. Coombs — 2/79.* A round, girlish hand.

"My mother," said Deb, who had come up behind him with a stack of returns on her hip. "She wanted to know how they did the table-tipping at the Grange. Nobody ever told her."

*R. Thibodeau — 4/79.* A schoolteacher's careful cursive.

*W. Calloway — 11/88.* Square block capitals, pressed hard.

"Walt Calloway," said Deb. "He took it home after Gus went. Kept it a month. I had to go up to the station and ask for it."

"Why'd he want it?"

"He didn't say. I expect he wanted to see if there was anything in it." She shrugged. "There wasn't. It's about séances."

Deb drew breath to go on down the card, the way people do with a family album. Archie's eye had already gone past her finger — past an *M. Pinkham* and an *H. Langley* and a long gap — twenty years and more of nobody — and three lines at the bottom, all in the same neat, slanting, very familiar hand:

*J. Fairweather — Sept.*

*J. Fairweather — Oct.*

*J. Fairweather — Oct.*

Archie stared at it.

"My mother?" he said.

"Your mother's name starts with a V, Mr. Fairweather," said Deb, and went off to shelve.

He sat there for a moment. Then he looked up, past the long table and the dark windows and the microfilm machine under its cover, to the far corner of the reading room, where there was a deep old armchair by a bookcase full of bound volumes of the *Merrow Packet*. He hadn't looked at it when he came in. He looked at it now.

The top of a dark bun with a pencil stuck through it was just visible over the back of the chair.

Archie got up, very quietly, and walked over.

June was curled sideways in the armchair with her Doc Martens tucked under her and her chemistry textbook open on her knees. On top of the textbook was a deck of cards. She had a card in her right hand — the jack of clubs — and as he watched, she squared the deck in her left, lifted the top half with two fingers, slipped the jack into the break, closed it, and then, with a small twist of her wrists that he'd have recognized from across a stadium, brought the jack invisibly back to the top.

It was a pass. A two-handed pass, the classic one, the one magicians practiced for years in front of mirrors and still flubbed half the time. She did it again. It was clean. Not perfect — her right thumb flashed a little at the end, she'd need to fix that — but clean. Clean enough to fool a room.

"Junebug," he said.

She jumped so hard the deck went everywhere.

Cards slid off the textbook and fanned across the floor, under the chair, into the bound volumes of the *Packet*. June looked at them, and then up at her father, and her whole face went red from the collar of her cardigan to the pencil in her hair.

"What are you doing here?" she said.

"What are *you* doing here?"

"Homework."

He crouched and began picking up cards. The jack of clubs had landed face up by his shoe. He held it up.

"This is not chemistry."

"It's a break. People take breaks." She snatched the jack out of his hand. "You're not supposed to be here. You never come here."

"I came for Gus's book." He couldn't stop it; he felt the grin take over his whole face. "June. That was a *pass.*"

"It was nothing."

"It was a two-handed pass! You've been learning out of his books. You signed it out three times. You've been — how long have you been doing this?"

"It doesn't matter."

"How long?"

June looked at the cards in her lap. "Since August," she said, very low. "There's a whole shelf of his stuff in the turret. I just — I was bored. There's nothing to do here. I read some of it. It's not a big deal."

"Not a big deal? Your thumb flashes a little at the end, but you've got the hard part, that's the part people spend years on, I spent two years on that, I used to practice in the bathroom on cruise ships — June, this is *wonderful.*" He was talking too fast. He knew he was talking too fast and he couldn't stop. "Do you know what we could do with this? The show. Halloween. You could come out in the second half, we'd do something together, something of Gus's — the Chair, we could do the Chair, nobody's done the Chair on that stage since 1988, think of it, Fairweathers three generations—"

"Dad."

"—I'd teach you the rest, the misdirection's the easy part once you've got the—"

"*Dad.*"

He stopped.

June was sitting very straight in the big armchair. Her face was still red, but it had gone set, the way it did before a debate round when she'd decided what the argument was going to be and nobody was going to talk her out of it.

"It's not for the show," she said. "It's not for you. It's not for anybody. It's mine." She gathered the cards out of her lap and squared them with a sharp tap against the textbook. "It's the one thing in this whole town that's mine. You've got the opera house and the hearse and the TV specials and the — the *bet*, and everybody knowing who you are. I've got this. So I'm not doing it on a stage, and I'm not doing it on Halloween, and I'm not doing it with you. Okay?"

The reading room was very quiet. Over at the circulation desk, Deb Coombs had stopped shelving.

Archie looked at his daughter. He had stood in front of thirty thousand people at a time, and walked through a wall at Fenway Park, and been locked in a safe at the bottom of Lake Tahoe with four minutes of air, and he could not remember ever in his life having been this completely without a next line.

"Okay," he said.

June looked at him suspiciously, as though she was waiting for the rest of it.

There wasn't a rest of it. He stood up, and his knees cracked, and he handed her the four of diamonds, which had gotten under his shoe.

"Your thumb," he said. "At the end. If you turn your wrist about ten degrees more toward the—" He stopped himself. "Sorry. Never mind. It's yours."

She took the card. After a moment, very slightly, she turned her wrist ten degrees, the way he'd meant, and looked at it, and didn't say anything.

He went back to the long table, sat down in front of his grandfather's book, and opened it at random, because he needed something to look at that wasn't his daughter. It fell open — as old books do, to the place where they've been opened most — at a chapter headed *THE TRUMPET IN THE DARK*, page one hundred and twelve.

*The medium,* Gus had written, in his dry, amused, unmistakable voice, *will invariably arrange to have one hand free. With it she lifts the trumpet, which has been painted with luminous bands so that the sitters may admire its flight. She places the small end to her own lips and speaks through it, moving it about as she speaks, and the voice appears to come from the air. It is the oldest trick in the room, and it works because nobody in the room wants it not to.*

Archie read the paragraph three times. Then he sat looking at the dark windows with his hand flat on the page.

At five o'clock exactly, Deb Coombs turned the lights off over the children's corner, and June came past the table with her backpack on one shoulder and her chemistry book under her arm. She paused beside his chair.

"Grandma's making soup," she said.

"I'll be right there."

She nodded. She stood there a second longer than she needed to. Then she went out, and the bell over the library door jingled behind her, and Archie sat alone in the reading room with Gus's book open in front of him and the old buff card beside it, and the uncomfortable feeling — new to him, and very unwelcome — that for once in his life, the right thing to do was nothing at all.


---

# Chapter 6

## Two of a Kind

The note had been waiting on the kitchen table when he got home from the library, propped against the salt shaker in an envelope of heavy cream paper that smelled faintly of smoke. *Drinks. Terms. Spindrift Inn, eight o'clock. Come alone, honey, I don't bite until Saturday. — L.C.*

"She's flirting with you," said Vivienne, reading it over his shoulder.

"She's negotiating."

"Darling, at our age it's the same thing."

The Spindrift Inn was a white sea captain's house on Water Street, built in 1840 by a man who'd made his money in ice and spent it on columns. It had a fanlight over the door and a widow's walk on the roof, and in the summer it was booked a year ahead by people from Connecticut. In late October it was booked by Marchand Media, all eleven rooms, and the little parking lot behind it was full of rental SUVs and a white production van with California plates.

The front parlor had a fire going in a fireplace big enough to roast a goat. Lorelei Crane was sitting in a wingback chair beside it with her shoes off and her feet tucked under her, a glass of red wine in one ringed hand and a paperback mystery face down on the arm of the chair.

"You came," she said, delighted, as if there'd been any doubt.

"You said come alone. I couldn't resist a dare."

"Nobody can, honey. That's my whole business." She waved at the chair opposite. "Sit. Pru's bringing you something. I told her you looked like a bourbon man."

He was a bourbon man. He sat.

"None for you?" he said. Her glass was nearly full.

"Ivy poured me this one at six, and I'm making it last, because she won't pour me another." Lorelei turned the glass in the firelight. "My daughter doesn't let me drink anything she hasn't poured herself. Twenty years on the road, honey — people slip things to psychics. Holy water. Love potions. A laxative, once, in Albany." She smiled. "She takes very good care of me."

Without the velvet coat, Lorelei looked smaller and more tired than she had in the opera house. She was in black slacks and a soft gray sweater, and the white streak in her hair caught the firelight. But the rings were all still there, the big pale moonstones on almost every finger, and the dark red mouth, and the eyes — amused, steady, missing nothing. She watched Pru Haskell set down a short glass of bourbon at his elbow and thanked Pru by name, and asked after Pru's daughter in Orono, whose name she also knew.

"So," she said, when Pru had gone. "Terms."

"Terms," Archie agreed. "One. I sit next to you."

"No."

"Two. Before we start, a woman of your choosing searches you, on camera—"

"No."

"Three. I bring my own camera. One. Infrared. Positioned where I like."

"No." She smiled at him over the wine. "Teddy owns every frame shot in that room, honey. It's in the contract. Union, network, lawyers, the whole circus. I couldn't say yes to your camera if I wanted to. Which I don't."

"You said this morning I could bring all the controls I liked."

"And you can. Bring them. Inspect the table. Look under the cloth. Shake the trumpet and see if anything falls out. Do it on camera; Teddy'll love it." She sipped. "But you don't sit next to me, and you don't put your hands on me, and you don't bring your own eyes. I'm not a fool, Mr. Fairweather. I've read your book."

"I haven't written a book."

"Your grandfather's book." She set her glass down. "Every medium of my generation learned the trade out of *The Dead Don't Whisper*. Did you know that? We passed it around. Church basements, Spiritualist camps, the back of the bus. He wrote the manual." She tilted her head. "He just thought he was writing a warning."

Archie had no idea what his face did. Whatever it was, it made her laugh — a real laugh, low and smoky, that turned two heads at the bar in the next room.

"Oh, I've upset you," she said. "I'm sorry. I'm not sorry. You'd have found out."

"How long have you been doing this?"

"Since I was twenty-six. Church halls first. Grange halls. Rented rooms over laundromats. All over New England — Massachusetts, New Hampshire, up here in Maine, back in the day." She waved her hand, and the moonstones flashed. "They all blur, honey. Two hundred little towns with a white church and a war memorial and somebody's mother who wanted to hear from her boy. Then a man from a cable channel came to a sitting in Worcester and here I am. Eight seasons. Billboards." She raised her glass to herself. "And you? Since you were a baby, I bet. Some old man in a cloak pulling quarters out of your ears."

"Four years old," said Archie. "A silver dollar. Out of my left ear. I thought he'd been keeping it in there for me."

"There," she said softly. "You see? We're the same."

"We are not the same."

"We are exactly the same, Mr. Fairweather." She leaned forward with her elbows on her knees and the wine glass held loose between her hands. "Here's the only difference between us. People buy a ticket to your show, and you lie to them for two hours, and they go home amazed and a little lighter in the wallet, and they're happy. People come to me, and I lie to them for two hours, and they go home with their dead." She shrugged. "Tell me which one of us they come back to."

"The ones who come to me know they're being lied to."

"Do they? Really? Every single one?" She smiled. "You've never had a lady in the front row who went home and told her sister it was real magic? Who *needed* it to be?"

He didn't answer that. He drank some of the bourbon, which was very good, and looked at the fire, and when he looked back she was watching him with her head on one side.

"You came to Merrow for your girl," she said.

It wasn't a question, and it wasn't a guess. She said it gently, the way she'd said it to Shannon Mayo on the telephone, probably, and to a thousand others. He watched himself almost fall for it.

"That's in the *Press Herald*, Lorelei."

"The girl's in the *Press Herald*. The rest of it isn't." She reached over and tapped one finger on his knee. "You don't know how to stay anywhere longer than a season, and you're scared she's going to figure that out before you do."

He sat very still. Then, because he'd been doing this for thirty years too and because he could feel the old reflex rising in him like a tide, he set down his glass and looked at her properly — the way he'd look at a volunteer from the audience in the half second before he chose them.

"You rub your wrists," he said. "Both of them. You've done it six times since I sat down. Your left shoulder sits lower than your right — you've been holding something up at arm's length in the dark for twenty years, and it hurts. You've got a paperback mystery on the arm of your chair, which means you like stories where somebody gets caught. You know Pru's daughter's name, and you know mine, and you know the bartender's, because names are the job; but you haven't said your own daughter's name once since I walked in." He picked the glass back up. "You're tired, Lorelei. You're tired of the trumpet. I think some part of you would give anything for somebody to catch you, so you could stop."

For a long moment the only sound was the fire.

Then Lorelei Crane laughed again, softly, and rubbed her left wrist, and caught herself doing it, and laughed harder.

"Oh, you're good," she said. "Oh, honey. You're wasted on rabbits." She sat back in the wingback chair and pulled her feet up under her again and looked at him with something that was almost affection. "This is my last season. Did you know that? I haven't told anybody. Not Teddy. Not the network. Eight seasons and this Halloween special and then I'm going to buy a house somewhere warm with no phone and read mysteries until I die."

"Why tell me?"

"Because you're the only person in this whole sorry business who'd understand what it means to walk off the stage." She raised her glass to him. "To the vanishing man."

He touched his glass to hers. He couldn't think why not.

"Mom."

Ivy Crane was standing in the parlor doorway. She was still in the oversized gray sweater, with the silk scarf at her throat, and she had a tablet held against her chest like a shield. She didn't look at Archie. She looked at her mother, and then at the wine, and then at her mother again.

"Teddy needs you to approve the cold open," she said.

"Tell Teddy I'm busy flirting with the competition."

"He says it has to go to the network tonight."

"Then tell him tonight's not over." Lorelei didn't turn around. "My daughter thinks I talk too much, Mr. Fairweather. She's probably right. She's the brains of the outfit. She does all the research." A small, hard smile. "Don't you, baby."

Ivy didn't answer. She stood in the doorway a moment longer, and her eyes went — not to Archie's face, but to his hands, the right one holding the glass and the left one resting on his knee. Then she turned and was gone down the hall, and somewhere a door closed, not quite a slam.

Lorelei finished her wine.

"Saturday," she said. "Eight o'clock. Wear something dark, honey — the cameras are infrared, and white shirts flare." She put her paperback under her arm. "And bring your mother. She's the one he wants to talk to."

---

He went out the back way, because the hearse was in the lot behind the inn, wedged between the production van and a rental SUV. In the front hall, as he passed, he heard Teddy Marchand's voice from behind a half-open door, pitched low and fast — "*—no, the key-person policy runs through the end of the season, that's the point, that's why I need her on the—*" — and then the door swung shut.

The night outside was cold and absolutely clear, the stars thick over the harbor, the air smelling of woodsmoke and low tide. His breath smoked. He had his keys out and was halfway across the gravel when he heard voices on the far side of the production van, and stopped without meaning to, in the dark between two cars.

"I mean it, Mom." Ivy's voice — low, flat, shaking a little at the bottom. "After this one I'm done. I'm *done*."

"Baby, you're tired. We're all tired."

"I'm not tired. I'm finished. I'm twenty-nine years old and I've never had a — I've never done anything else. I'm done after Saturday."

A pause. Then Lorelei, very soft and very clear, in a voice Archie hadn't heard her use yet.

"You're done when I say you're done, sweetheart."

The van door slid open and shut. Footsteps went away across the gravel toward the inn's back door, quick and light — Ivy's. After a moment, slower footsteps followed. The back door opened, let out a rectangle of yellow light and the sound of someone laughing in the bar, and closed again.

Archie stood in the dark between the cars for a while longer. Then he got into the hearse and sat with his hands on the wheel without starting it.

He'd come to the Spindrift Inn tonight to negotiate with a fraud. He'd been ready for that. He'd been ready to dislike her, and to beat her, and to enjoy every second of it on Saturday night with the cameras rolling. He'd been doing exactly that to people like her since he was nineteen.

What he hadn't been ready for, he thought, turning the key, listening to the old Cadillac engine cough and catch and settle, was to like her.

He drove home along Shore Road under the stars with the heater roaring, and found that he was thinking not about the séance, or the bet, or the trunk, but about a tired woman rubbing her wrists by a fire, raising a glass to the vanishing man.


---

# Chapter 7

## Controls

"The prop-room door was unlocked this morning," said Hollis Tibbetts, by way of hello.

It was a quarter to seven on Saturday night, and she was standing at the top of the opera house stairs with her ring of keys in one fingerless-gloved hand and a clipboard in the other.

"Unlocked how?"

"Unlocked the way a door is when somebody doesn't lock it." She lowered her voice, which for Hollis meant that only half the hall could hear. "I lent Warren my key yesterday afternoon. He wanted to do the inventory for the sale — the posters and the old fixtures and so on, for the closing. He swears up and down he locked it after." She looked at Archie over her glasses. "Nothing's missing that I can see. But I can't see everything."

"Did you tell anyone?"

"I'm telling you." She checked something on the clipboard. "Your mother's in the green room terrorizing the makeup girl. Your widow is in the front row looking like she'd rather be anywhere else. And there's a young man in a sweatshirt with his own face on it asking for you."

She went off down the aisle toward the stage, keys jingling, before he could ask anything else.

Archie stood at the top of the stairs and looked at his theater.

It wasn't his theater anymore — not tonight. In two days Marchand Media had turned the plain, cold, high hall into something out of a dream about a funeral. Black drapes hung over every one of the tall arched windows except the last one at the stage-left end, left bare so the camera could catch the harbor lights. The folding chairs were stacked against the walls. Cables ran everywhere in taped black rivers. Up on the stage, in the middle of all that bare dark wood, under a single soft work light, stood a round oak table with seven chairs pulled up to it, and on the table, laid out like the place settings for a very strange dinner, were a brass hand bell, a tambourine, a child's chalk slate in a wooden frame, and a long cone of dull silver aluminum with three bands of pale green paint around it.

The trumpet.

Two cameras on tripods faced the table from either side of the stage, each with a fat black lens and a ring of small dark lights around it — infrared, for the dark. Their cables ran off to the stage-right wing, where Teddy Marchand sat in a folding chair in front of a monitor, a headset around his neck and a phone in each hand. A third camera — a handheld, carried by a broad, quiet young man in a black watch cap and black gloves — was drifting around the edge of the stage, filming the candles that hadn't been lit yet and the painted harbor curtain and the empty chairs.

It was cold. The radiators were knocking, doing their best. Archie could see his breath, faintly, in the work light.

"Mr. Fairweather!"

The young man in the sweatshirt was coming up the aisle at something close to a run. He was about thirty, round-faced, with a scruff of beard, a lanyard that said SITTER — CONTEST WINNER, and a black hoodie that said SPECTRAL KEV across the chest in dripping green letters over a cartoon of his own face. He was holding an orange plastic gadget with a row of little lights on it.

"Kev Duchesne," he said, breathless. "Haverhill, Mass. I'm the contest. I've seen every one of your specials. And every one of *hers*, which is sort of the problem—" He held out the orange gadget. "Would you sign my EMF meter?"

Archie looked at it. "What does it do?"

"It detects fluctuations in the electromagnetic field associated with spirit activity."

"It detects the wiring in the walls, Kev."

"I know," said Kev miserably. "That's why I need you to sign it. Then at least it's worth something."

Archie signed it. Kev looked at the signature with his mouth open, and then over at the stage, where Lorelei Crane had just come up the stairs from below in a long black velvet dress, her white streak gleaming, her rings catching the light — and his face did something complicated and torn.

"She's signing it too," he whispered. "After."

"Kev," said Archie, "you're going to have a very confusing night."

---

At seven-thirty, the cameras rolled.

That was what Teddy called it — *we're rolling on controls, people!* — and the hall went quiet, and Archie Fairweather walked out into the work light at the center of the stage in a black shirt and black trousers, because a medium had told him white shirts flared, and he hated that she was right.

"Ladies and gentlemen," he said to the camera, to the empty chairs, to the knot of sitters by the stage steps, "nothing up my sleeves."
