Archie didn't take a card. He sat back in Gus's chair at the end of the table with his hands flat on the felt, and the idea arrived the way they always did, all at once and fully dressed, with a hat on.

"Hear me out," he said.

"No," said Lenny.

"Teddy Marchand." Archie leaned forward. "Teddy's show is about to end — she told me herself, she was quitting. Teddy also has an insurance policy on her through the end of the season; I heard him say so through a door. Now. What's the biggest television event in history? A medium who dies on camera, in the dark, talking to the dead, in the most haunted theater in New England. That's not a special. That's a *franchise*. And what does Lorelei want? A house somewhere warm, with no phone, and a stack of mysteries." He spread his hands. "Everybody wins."

"She did die," said Bev.

"*Did* she?"

"Archie," said Vivienne.

"A hospital where nobody'd ever seen her except on a bus. A doctor I'd never met. And then she's gone, into a room I couldn't go into." He was going faster now; he could feel it carrying him. "I've made a woman go gray onstage, Mother. Greasepaint and glycerin, twice a night in Vegas. Right now Lorelei Crane is on a beach in Baja with a paperback, and Teddy Marchand is on the phone to the network with tears in his voice, and the ratings—"

"There'd have to be two ambulances," said Dale Pinkham, with dawning wonder. "One for the — and then a second one, see, at the hospital, round the back—"

"*Exactly*, Chief—"

"She's dead," said Lenny.

"Lenny—"

"Last night you said poison. You told the police poison."

"Last night I was tired."

June's voice came from the hall, where she was sitting halfway up the front stairs with a library book and a blanket, in a position from which she could see the whole table and pretend she couldn't. "Dad."

"I'm just *saying*—"

"Dad. You were holding her."

He stopped.

The fire popped again. Ozzie dealt him two cards, very slowly, and Archie looked at them without seeing them.

Bev cleared her throat. She did not look at him. She picked up her coffee cup, found it empty, set it down.

"You brought my thermos back, anyhow," she said. "Took you long enough. I washed it. Cups and all." She took a card. "I wash everything that comes back. You'd be amazed what comes back."

Archie looked up.

It took a second to land. Then it landed, and he felt it go all the way down, cold. The thermos. The two cups that screwed onto the top. The cup he'd poured for Lorelei, and handed to her across the table, on camera, and which she'd drunk half of. The one thing in the world that might have proved the tea was only tea.

"She'd wash the Pope," said Dale comfortably, "if he sat still long enough."

"She washed my hat," said Ozzie. "Nineteen ninety-four."

"It needed it," said Bev.

Vivienne laughed. Lenny didn't. He was watching Archie across the table, and so, Archie realized, was his mother — the laugh had been for the room, not for him.

He didn't say anything funny. He couldn't find anything.

"Archie?" said Dale.

"Dad," said June from the stairs, in a different voice. "There's somebody in the driveway."

Headlights had swung across the dining-room windows and stopped. A car door shut. Boots on the porch. Lenny, who was nearest, got up and opened the front door before anyone knocked, and there was Tess Calloway on the porch in her charcoal coat with the cold coming in around her.

She looked past Lenny at the table — the fire, the felt, the chief of the Merrow Police Department holding a pair of jacks — and her eyebrows went up a sixteenth of an inch.

"Evening, Dale," she said.

"Sergeant." Dale put his cards face down, very fast.

"I've just come from Augusta." She didn't come in. She spoke to Archie, across the room, past Lenny's shoulder. "The autopsy's done. The toxicology's gone to the lab. I'll have it when I have it."

"Archie thinks she's in Baja," said Dale helpfully.

"I was in the room this afternoon, Dale." Tess said it quietly. "She's not in Baja."

Nobody at the table said anything. Archie looked down at the felt.

"You were at the Spindrift with Ivy Crane at one o'clock," Tess went on. "That I know about, because I was there. At four you were at Shannon Mayo's house."

"How do you—"

"It's Merrow." She took off one glove. "Stop. That's what I drove down here to say. Stop visiting my witnesses. Stop finding things in umbrella stands. Stop."

"Sergeant," said Vivienne. She had risen from her chair in her plum kaftan, and she was holding out her hand. "You look absolutely frozen, and you've driven an hour and a half to say one word. Come in and sit down. Ozzie, pull out a chair. One hand."

"I'm on duty, Mrs. Fairweather."

"Then you'll play carefully." Vivienne kept her hand out. "One hand. It would be rude to go straight back out into that."

Tess looked at her for a long moment. Then she stepped in, and somehow she was sitting at the end of the table opposite Archie, with her coat over the back of the chair and a stack of Vivienne's chips in front of her.

Ozzie dealt. Five each.

Archie picked up his cards. Nothing. Ten high, two hearts, two clubs, a spade. He took four, slowly, watching her across the table, and got nothing back. Ten high still.

Bev could read his face; fine. Tonight he made it a wall. When the bet came round he pushed in his whole stack, six dollars and five cents in red and white clay, and sat back and looked at Tess with no expression at all, while his fingers riffled three chips over and under, over and under.

Bev folded. Dale folded. Vivienne folded with a sigh. Lenny had never been in.

Tess looked at her cards. She looked at him.

"Call," she said, and pushed her chips in, and turned over a pair of sixes.

He turned over ten high.

"How," he said. It wasn't a performance this time. "My face didn't move. I *know* my face didn't move."

"It didn't." Tess began, without hurry, to stack his chips. "It's your hands. When you've got something, they stop." She nodded at his right hand, which was still riffling three red chips. "You haven't stopped doing that since I sat down. You had nothing." She stacked another column. "You did it on the stage this morning, too, before you went down to the umbrella stand. Your hands stopped. That's when I knew you'd thought of something."

Archie sat very still at the end of his grandfather's table.

His hands, he realized, had stopped.

Nobody watched his hands. That was the whole point of the face. For thirty years, in every room he'd been in, he had been the one doing the watching — and here was a woman in a charcoal coat, stacking his chips into neat red towers, who had apparently been reading him since the afternoon she walked into the common room at Sea View, and had kept it to herself until it was worth six dollars and five cents.

"Thank you, Mrs. Fairweather," said Tess, standing. She left the chips where they were and picked up her coat. "Walk me out, Mr. Fairweather."

---

The porch was cold and black, and the stars were out thick over the harbor. Out on the point, Merrow Light swept its green flash across the water and was gone, and came back. Her gray car was parked beside the hearse, so precisely parallel to it that it looked measured.

"You could have called," Archie said. "About the autopsy. You didn't have to drive all the way down here."

"No."

"So why did you?"

Tess pulled on her gloves. "I wanted to see your face when I told you to stop."

"And?"

She looked at him in the light from the dining-room windows for a moment. Then she went down the steps.

"And you're not going to," she said, over her shoulder, and got into her car.

He stood on the porch until her taillights had gone down Shore Road, and then went back in. Bev was dealing. June had gone back to her book on the stairs and was pretending she'd never looked up from it.

Vivienne was gathering the chips Tess had left. She stacked them neatly, red on red, and slid the whole pile back across the felt to Archie's place, and didn't look up.

"I like her," she said. "She doesn't need you to be wonderful."


---

# Chapter 15

## Hauling

At five-thirty on Monday morning the town float was black and slick with frost, and Archie Fairweather stood on it in his buffalo-check coat with a white plastic bucket in both arms, so happy he could barely keep still.

It was cold — real cold, the first of the year, the kind that got into the bones of your face. The harbor was flat and dark and steaming faintly. Out past the moorings the bell buoy rang once, slow, and out on the point Merrow Light swung its green flash over the water every six seconds. Up at the head of the wharf the windows of the Galley were yellow, and in one of them, by the kitchen door, three shapes sat around a round table. One of them was looking down at the float. When Archie raised his free hand, it looked away.

Sixty-two.

"You brought it," said Dale Pinkham.

"I said I would."

"Ayuh. You did." The chief came down the ramp in orange oilskin bibs and a hooded sweatshirt that said KNOX COUNTY SHERIFF'S OFFICE SOFTBALL 2004, carrying a thermos and two pairs of rubber gloves. He peered into the bucket. Harold peered back.

"Gramp, we're losing the tide," said a voice from the boat.

*Second Wind* lay against the float with her engine already rumbling, a white lobster boat with a square wheelhouse and a work deck crowded with gear: the hydraulic hauler at the starboard rail, a long wooden bait box, a seawater tank. A girl in orange bibs was standing on the rail with a line in her hand. She was sixteen, broad-shouldered, with a round freckled face and a fuzzy black hat pulled down to her eyebrows, and she looked at Archie and his bucket with the open, cheerful contempt of the young for the useless.

"You're June's dad," she said.

"I am."

"June says you can't drive stick."

"June is a gifted researcher. Shouldn't you be in school?"

"Teacher workshop." Mattie looked at the bucket. "June's asleep. June said you'd be late."

"Permission to come aboard?"

"Don't stand in the rope," said Mattie Pinkham, and jumped down onto the deck.

---

They went out through the moorings in the dark, past *Miss Dottie* and *Three Daughters*, with their running lights on and the diesel thumping under Archie's feet. The bait box held a hundred pounds of salted herring and was the worst thing Archie had ever smelled. He stood in the stern with Harold's bucket against his chest and watched the steeple and the opera house bell tower go black against a sky that was beginning, along the edge, to go gray.

Dale throttled down off the end of Shag Ledge, inside the harbor, where the water was still flat.

"Here's good," he said. "Deep enough. Rocky bottom. He'll be all right here."

Archie set the bucket on the rail and lifted Harold out. Harold raised both claws, which were no longer banded — Archie had cut the bands on Friday because it seemed rude — and waved them.

"Hold on," said Dale. "Measure him first."

"Dale, he's from the co-op. He's legal. I paid for him."

"Measure him anyhow." Dale held out a brass gauge, worn bright at the edges. "You don't put a lobster over the side of my boat without you measure him. Them's the rules."

Archie measured him, eye socket to the back of the shell. Legal. Dale nodded, satisfied, as if a small piece of the world had been put back where it belonged.

"Give him here a second," said Mattie. She took Harold from Archie with one hand, flipped him over with total efficiency, and looked at his underside. "Huh," she said. "Harold's a girl."

Archie stared at her.

"Swimmerets." Mattie pointed. "See, soft. That's a female. You named a girl Harold."

"She never said a word," said Archie. "Not one word in four days. She let me go on calling her Harold, and she never once corrected me." He took the lobster back and held her up at eye level in the gray light. "Harold. You absolute professional."

Mattie laughed out loud, a big unguarded honk that went off across the water. Even Dale's mustache moved.

Archie leaned over the rail and lowered Harold into the harbor. She hung for a second in the green-black water just under the surface, claws spread, and then she tipped forward and backed away down into the dark, tail flicking, and was gone.

He stayed leaning over the rail a moment longer than he needed to.

"Okay," said Mattie. "Can we *work* now?"

---

They worked.

It was a rhythm, and he saw it at once, because rhythm was the one thing in the world he understood completely. Dale brought the boat alongside a buoy — white with a green band, his colors — and Mattie gaffed the line and threw it over the hauler's sheave, and the hauler whined, and the line came up dripping and coiled itself on the deck, and then the trap broke the surface with water pouring out of it and Mattie swung it onto the rail. Open the door. Pick the lobsters. Measure. Throw back the shorts. Band the keepers. Bait bag out, bait bag in. Shut the door. Push it over. Next.

"Let me do one," said Archie.

Mattie looked at her grandfather. Dale looked at the sky, going pink now behind Merrow Light, and at the long string of buoys ahead of them. Three hundred traps to get up before the weather. He looked at Archie's face, and sighed.

"One," he said. "Mattie, stand by him. Archie, stay out of the bight."

"What's the bight?"

"The loop in the rope on the deck. When the trap goes over, the line goes with it. Your foot's in the bight, you go with it." Dale said it without any drama at all. "Fella over in Tenants Harbor went with it in 2011."

Archie stepped very carefully out of the bight.

He did one. He did it well. The trap came up streaming and he swung it onto the rail, and there were four lobsters inside and a crab and a sculpin, and he had his hands on them before Mattie could say anything — the gauge, short, over it goes; the gauge, keeper; the band snapping onto the claw clean on the first try. He threw the sculpin back with an apology. He stuffed a new bait bag with herring, which was cold and slippery and unspeakable, and shut the door, and slid the trap over, and kept his feet out of the bight while the line went whipping after it.

He straightened up, breathing hard, with herring scales on his coat.

Mattie looked at the two banded lobsters in the tank. She looked at him.

"Not bad," she said, as though it had been taken out of her with pliers.

Archie was so pleased he could have sung. It lasted until they cleared the ledge.

Dale opened the throttle and took them out past the end of the ledge, where the harbor stopped being a harbor. The swell came in under the boat — long and slow and gray, nothing at all, Dale said afterward; you'd hardly call it a sea. *Second Wind* lifted her bow and lay over a little and came back, and lifted, and lay over, and came back. The diesel fumes rolled back over the stern. The bait box sat in the middle of the deck and breathed.

Archie had escaped from a safe at the bottom of Lake Tahoe. He had held his breath for four minutes in a water tank on live television.

He lasted about six minutes.

He made it to the lee rail, which was the one dignity he was afforded, and hung over it and was sick into the Gulf of Maine, comprehensively, while the gulls, who knew a good thing, came in low over the wake.

"Gramp," said Mattie, with enormous satisfaction. "He's chumming."

"Leave him be."

The radio in the wheelhouse crackled. A voice came out of it, flat and amused, from a boat Archie could see a quarter mile off to the east, a green hull with its hauler going.

"*Second Wind, Second Wind. That your magician, Dale?*"

Dale picked up the handset. "Ayuh."

"*He all right?*"

"He's fine."

"*Looks like he's making something disappear.*"

There was laughter on the radio — not just from the green boat. Somebody else on the channel, and somebody else. The whole working harbor, Archie understood, hanging over the rail with his eyes streaming, was on that channel at this hour. The whole working harbor had just watched him do it.

He was sick again.

---

Dale took pity on him after a while and throttled back and let the boat lie quieter in the lee of Shag Ledge, close to the bell buoy, while Mattie baited bags. Archie sat on an upturned bucket in the stern with his back against the traps and a cup of coffee from Dale's thermos in both hands, and the sun came up red over the islands to the east, and slowly, very slowly, the sea agreed to stop moving quite so much.

The bell buoy was thirty yards off — a big red iron cage on a float, with four clappers hung round a bell, rolling with the swell. Up close it wasn't a sound so much as a feeling: a slow, iron, irregular tolling that came across the water and into the deck and up through the soles of his boots.

"Right about here," Dale said. He was standing in the wheelhouse door with his own coffee, looking at the buoy. "Where they found the skiff. Eighty-eight. I was on my father's boat, that morning. Everybody went out. Every boat in the harbor. Coast Guard come up from Rockland." He tipped his cup toward the point. "She was drifting down toward the Light on the ebb. Oars shipped. Nobody in her. His cloak folded on the thwart, neat as you please, like he'd just stepped out for a minute."

Archie looked at the buoy. The bell tolled, and tolled.

"I was there that night," he said. "At the show. I was ten. I was in the second row."

Dale didn't say anything. He nodded slowly, and kept looking at the buoy, and after a while Mattie stopped baiting bags and looked at it too.

Dale's phone rang in the pocket of his bibs.

He looked at the screen and his face changed, just slightly — the face of a man seeing a number he'd been waiting for. He stepped into the wheelhouse to take it and pulled the door to behind him. Archie could see him through the salt-streaked window: the phone at his ear, his free hand flat on the wheel, nodding. Saying *ayuh* and *ayuh* and *what time*. Not looking at the stern.

He was in there a long time.

When he came out he stood in the wheelhouse door with the phone in his hand. He looked at Mattie and said, "Go mind the wheel a minute, Matts," and she looked at his face and went into the wheelhouse without arguing, which Archie gathered was rare. Then Dale came and sat down on the bait box beside Archie's bucket, heavily, and put his elbows on his knees.

"I'm not supposed to tell you this," he said.

"Then don't, Dale. I mean it. I don't want to get you—"

"That was the sergeant." Dale looked at his big hands. "The blood came back. From the hospital. It's what you said. Aconite." He said the word carefully, the way he'd say the name of a town he'd never been to. "They've opened it up. It's a homicide now. State Police, the AG's office, all of it."

The bell buoy tolled. Somewhere off to the east, the green boat's hauler whined.

"I shouldn't have told you," said Dale.

Archie looked down into his coffee. "Thank you."

"Lieutenant Brannock's down from Augusta. He's setting up in the select board room. He wants you in at two o'clock this afternoon." Dale turned his head and looked at Archie properly for the first time since the phone rang. "Bring a lawyer."

"I don't need a lawyer, Dale. I haven't done anything. I'll go in and explain about the trumpet and—"

"Ayuh," said Dale Pinkham. He stood up off the bait box, and put his hand on Archie's shoulder, once, and went back toward the wheel. "Bring one anyhow."


---

# Chapter 16

## Person of Interest

The select board room was on the ground floor of the town hall, and it was the coldest room Archie had ever been in that wasn't a walk-in freezer or a lake.

It was a long, plain room with an oak table, five high-backed chairs for the select board, a flag on a stand, and a wall of framed photographs of past selectmen, most of them in beards. The radiator under the window was clanking with enormous energy and producing no heat at all. Out the window, across Main Street, the white steeple of the Congregational church stood up into a hard blue two o'clock sky.

The State Police had moved in. There were two laptops on the table, a printer on a folding chair, a whiteboard on an easel with nothing on it yet, three banker's boxes, and a large Dunkin' cup in front of the man in the middle chair.

"Mr. Fairweather," said the man. "Sit down. Thank you for coming in."

Lieutenant Neil Brannock was in his fifties, tall even sitting down, with a gray crewcut, a wedding ring, and a pair of reading glasses that he took off as Archie came in and put on again a moment later, irritably, to read something. He wore a navy State Police fleece zipped to the chin over his suit. Tess sat at the end of the table to his left with a notebook and her charcoal coat still on. Her breath showed faintly in the air.

Archie sat. The folding chair was so cold he could feel it through his trousers.

"You don't have a lawyer with you," said Brannock.

"I don't need one."

"Everybody says that." Brannock pressed a button on a small digital recorder. He gave the date and the time and the names of the people in the room, in a flat, efficient voice. "Mr. Fairweather, you're here voluntarily. You're not under arrest. You can leave whenever you want, and you can have a lawyer whenever you want. Do you understand that?"

"Yes."

Brannock sniffed. He looked up over his glasses. "Have you been fishing?"

"Hauling. With Chief Pinkham. This morning." Archie had showered twice. The coat had not. "It's herring. I'm told it doesn't come out."

"It doesn't," said Tess, without looking up.

"Lieutenant, before we start, may I just say what I came here to say? Because I think it'll save us all a great deal of time." Archie leaned forward. "It wasn't the tea. It was the trumpet. She had a hand free in the dark — her left — and she picked up the trumpet and put the small end to her lips to do the voices, and somebody had put the aconite on the mouthpiece. Your sergeant saw a reconstruction yesterday. I can show you in thirty seconds."

He had a deck of cards in the inside pocket of his coat. He took it out, plainly, and fanned it on the oak.

Nobody had asked him to. Brannock looked at the cards and then at Archie, and didn't stop him, which Archie took for encouragement.

"Watch my right hand," Archie said. He picked a card out of the fan with his right — the eight of clubs — and showed it to the recorder, and to Tess, and to Brannock, and slid it into the middle of the deck with a great deal of fuss and a squared tap. "Now. Where's the eight?"

"On top," said Brannock.

Archie stopped.

"Your left thumb," said Brannock. He hadn't moved. "When you squared the deck. Your right hand made all the noise and your left thumb pushed the card back up. I don't know what that's called. I saw it because I was watching your left hand, because I was told that's what you'd do." He took his glasses off. "Do it again. Slowly. And tell me, the whole time, where your left hand is."

Archie looked at Tess. Tess was writing.

"That's the problem I have, Mr. Fairweather," Brannock said. "You've just shown me — voluntarily, for the tape — that you can make two people look at one of your hands while the other one does what it likes. That's not a thing I had to prove. You came in and did it." He tapped the deck. "Put those away, please."

Archie put them away.

There was a knock, and the door opened before anyone answered it. Two large young men in canvas work jackets came in backward, carrying an object the size of a jukebox wrapped in blankets. Behind them came a third man with a dolly, on which was strapped a tall finned oil-filled radiator, the industrial kind.

"Delivery," said the first young man, consulting a clipboard. "Fairweather? From Portland. Espresso machine, two-group, and the heater. Where do you want it?"

Brannock looked at the espresso machine, and at the heater, and for a long moment at Archie.

"Dale told me this room was freezing," Archie said. "And I've had the coffee at the Dunkin' on Route 1. Lieutenant, I'm not trying to bribe anybody, I'm trying to keep you all from getting pneumonia. It's paid for. It's a gift to the town. You can leave it here when you go."

"Take it out," said Brannock.

"It's paid for," said the young man.

"Then take it out paid for." Brannock didn't raise his voice. "All of it. Thank you."

The young men looked at each other. They began, with some difficulty, to back the espresso machine out the door.

"Leave the heater," said Tess.

Brannock turned his head.

"It's forty-eight degrees in here, Lieutenant," Tess said. She hadn't stopped writing. "I checked."

Brannock looked at her for a second. Then he looked at the radiator, which had chosen that moment to clank twice like a man hitting a pipe with a wrench. He said nothing. The third young man plugged the heater into the wall by Tess's chair, turned the dial to high, and left, and the door shut on the sound of the espresso machine going down the hall on its blankets.

Brannock opened the laptop in front of him and turned it so Archie could see.

"I've been through every frame Marchand Media has given us," he said. "There's a lot of it. This is the part I keep coming back to."

It was the stage in the work light. Archie in his black shirt, sleeves pushed up, picking up the trumpet, looking through it, holding the small end to his ear. *Anybody home?* Then the small end to his lips. The sound came out of the little speaker thin and tinny. The crew laughing. *No spirits in here, folks.* The trumpet going back down in the center of the table, exactly where it had been. The time code in the corner said 19:35:12.

Brannock clicked. Now it was Archie unscrewing the two cups from Bev's thermos, filling both, holding one out across the table. Ivy's hand coming flat over her mother's place. Lorelei laughing, taking the cup. *To the vanishing man.* Lorelei drinking. 19:41:50.

"You poured her tea," said Brannock.

"I drank mine. The same tea. From the same thermos. I drank all of it."

"You drank from a cup you poured. She drank from a cup you poured." Brannock leaned back. "We went to collect the thermos this morning. Mrs. Thibodeau washed it yesterday afternoon. The thermos and both cups, with soap. Somebody brought it back to her at about noon on Sunday. Who was that?"

The radiator clanked. The new heater ticked as it warmed.

"Me," said Archie.

"Noon on Sunday. Fourteen hours after you told Sergeant Calloway, in the hospital, that Lorelei Crane had been poisoned with aconite. Before anybody else in the state had used that word."

"My mother told me to take it back."

It came out before he could stop it, and it sounded exactly as bad as it was. He heard it land on the recorder. Across the table, Tess's pen stopped for a moment and then went on.

"Mr. Fairweather," said Brannock, quite gently, "you knew the poison before the lab did. You poured her the only drink on that tape. You had two million dollars on her being a fraud, and forty minutes later she was doing your grandfather's voice in the dark. And the morning after she died, the cup she drank from went back to a diner sink."

Archie sat in the cold folding chair and felt the room tilt. It wasn't a trick. It was just the shape of things, laid end to end by a man who was good at his job.

He thought about June counting on her fingers at the breakfast table. Then he told them the rest, because it was true and because they'd find it anyway.

"There's wolfsbane in my garden," he said. "Monkshood. A whole stand of it, along the fence at the bottom, where it goes down to the rocks. My grandfather planted it. He thought the name was funny, for Halloween. My mother cut some on Thursday and put it in a jug on the kitchen table." He looked at Brannock. "Half the gardens in this town have it. But mine's one of them, and I'd rather you heard it from me."

Brannock had stopped writing. He looked at Archie for a long time with no expression at all. Then he wrote something down, carefully.

Tess put her pen flat on the notebook.

"Lieutenant," she said. "For the recording. Mr. Fairweather's here voluntarily, and he's now said three times he doesn't want a lawyer. Before we go any further I'd like him to hear again that he can stop this and have one."

Brannock turned his head and looked at her. Something passed between them that Archie couldn't read and didn't need to.

"He's heard it, Sergeant."

"I'd like him to hear it again. On the tape."

Brannock took his glasses off. "Mr. Fairweather, you can stop this interview at any time and consult a lawyer. Do you want to?"

"No," said Archie. "I want you to listen to me about the raspberry."

"The raspberry."

"Seven thirty-five. On your laptop. I put the small end of that trumpet in my mouth and blew, as hard as I could, in front of two cameras and a dozen people. And I'm sitting here. I'm fine. I've never felt better in my life, apart from this morning on the boat." He tapped the table. "That horn was clean at seven thirty-five. Whatever was put on it was put on after. That's your window, Lieutenant. Seven thirty-five to eight o'clock. That's who you want."

Brannock actually thought about it; Archie watched him do it.

"Or," he said, "it's the one moment on that tape when a professional sleight-of-hand man had his hands, and his mouth, on the thing that killed her." He put his glasses back on. "Mr. Fairweather, your whole career is getting a camera to record something that didn't happen. You're very good at it. I've seen the riverboat. I'm not going to take the tape's word for what was or wasn't on your lips."

Archie opened his mouth and found he had nothing to put in it.

"Sergeant Calloway let you onto a sealed scene yesterday," Brannock went on, in the same flat voice, "and you picked a lock, and then you picked up a piece of evidence with your bare hands and put your prints all over it."

"That's on me, Lieutenant," said Tess.

"Yes," said Brannock. "It is." He didn't look at her. "We'd like to search your garden. And your kitchen. You can make us get a warrant."

"Search it," said Archie. "Search the garden. Search the house. Lenny'll show you the trapdoor in the pantry. Search the trunk in the turret, if you can get it open. I've been trying since August, and if you crack it I'll owe you dinner for the rest of your life."

Brannock almost smiled. It was there and then it wasn't.

"Sergeant Calloway will bring a consent form out to the house this afternoon." He clicked the recorder off. "And Mr. Fairweather. Don't go near my witnesses. Ivy Crane. Shannon Mayo. Warren Gilley. The production company. Kevin Duchesne. All of them."

Archie stood up. His knees were stiff from the cold; the heater by Tess's chair had only just begun to breathe warm air into the corner of the room.

"Lieutenant," he said, "I live here. I buy my coffee from Shannon Mayo. Warren Gilley is selling me the building you're sitting in." He buttoned his coat, which smelled of herring. "Where exactly would you like me to go?"

Brannock looked at him over his glasses.

"Somewhere I can find you," he said.


---

# Chapter 17

## Malvolio

Pudding Lane climbed away from Water Street for six houses, then lost interest and turned into a footpath through somebody's blueberry barren. Hollis Tibbetts lived in the fourth house: a small weathered Cape with a picket fence, a potting shed whose roof had been sagging since the Carter administration, and a blue front door. At six o'clock on Monday evening it was full dark, and every window she owned was lit.

Archie parked the hearse along her fence, which used up most of the fence, and sat for a minute listening to the engine tick.

He had not gone home. A news van from Portland had been parked at the bottom of Shore Road since four, and his mother had texted: *Don't come in the front, darling. There is a young man with a microphone in the rhododendrons and Lenny has gone out to speak to him.* Then, five minutes later: *Lenny is back. Don't ask.* So Archie had driven around the peninsula instead, twice, in a hearse with his grandfather's name on the side in gold, which was not how a person of interest kept his head down. At five Hollis had called. She'd pulled some costumes for his show, and there was a fish chowder on the stove, and she had a fair idea which one he needed more.

It was the first conversation he'd had since two o'clock that didn't have a lieutenant in it.

She opened the door before he reached the step — oatmeal cardigan, fingerless gloves, glasses on their beaded chain, and an apron that said MERROW PLAYERS · *THE MUSIC MAN* · 1994. She looked him over. Thirty-five years of sophomores had been looked over like that, on mornings they'd turned up without the book.

"You've been in that select board room," she said.

"Two hours and a quarter."

"It shows. Shoes off. Mind the cat."

The cat was on the third stair. He was large and gray and flat-faced, with yellow eyes, and he sat with his front feet together and considered Archie as if Archie had come to the wrong party in the wrong clothes and was not on the list.

"That's Malvolio," said Hollis. "He bit the Methodist minister at Easter, so you're in good company."

"Why Malvolio?"

"He's pompous, he has no sense of humor, and he believes the lady of the house is secretly in love with him."

"Is she?"

"Kitchen," said Hollis.

He followed her through the front room, which had stopped being a front room a long time ago. The Merrow Players' wardrobe lived there now: thirty years of shows standing about on dress forms in the lamplight like guests nobody had told to sit down. A widow in black bombazine. A Union cavalryman. A Puss in Boots with one boot. A sewing machine on a card table still had a hem caught under its foot, and there were books on every flat surface, including one end of the sofa where a leg had given out. A woodstove ticked in the corner. Against the far wall stood an upright piano under a fringed shawl, its top crowded with photographs.

The kitchen was low and warm and smelled of onions and salt pork and hot milk. Hollis ladled two bowls from a dented pot, set out oyster crackers and the heel of a loaf and a saucer of butter, and sat down across the oilcloth from him.

"Eat first," she said. "Then you can tell me how dreadful everything is."

He ate. It was haddock and potatoes and a great deal of black pepper, and it was the best thing that had happened to him since Saturday night. He finished the bowl. She took it and filled it again without asking.

Halfway through the second bowl, something heavy landed in his lap.

Malvolio turned around twice, kneaded Archie's thighs with great thoroughness, and lay down. He was very heavy and very warm.

Hollis stared at him. "Well," she said. "I'll be."

"Animals love me."

"That one doesn't. He's never sat on anybody in his life." She looked at the cat with real concern. "He must be coming down with something."

Archie couldn't reach the bowl now without disturbing him. He took a quarter out of his pocket instead and walked it across the knuckles of his right hand above the cat's head — over the forefinger, under the middle, over the ring finger and back. The yellow eyes followed it. A gray paw came up off Archie's knee and hung in the air, considering.

"Don't let him," said Hollis. "He'll have your finger off at the joint."

The paw went back down. Malvolio shut his eyes. He had decided the quarter was beneath him.

"So," said Hollis. "The show."

"I don't know that there's going to be a show."

"There is going to be a show. It's on the posters. Deb Coombs put one up in the library over the new fiction, which she has never done for anybody, including the governor." She buttered a piece of bread and handed it to him. "Go on, then. Say it."

He said it. He hadn't meant to say all of it, but the kitchen was warm and the cat was heavy and nobody here was taking notes. He kept seeing Lorelei on the stage boards with her head in the crook of his arm. She'd asked him if he'd caught her, and he'd told her no. He'd sat across a fire from her on Friday night and liked her better than anybody he'd met in years, and now she was in the medical examiner's office in Augusta, and if he'd shouted for the lights five minutes sooner—

"Stop that," said Hollis.

"I'm only—"

"I know what you're only. Stop it." She put down her spoon. "I'm sorry she's dead. I'd be sorry if a bus had hit her. But that woman was a vulture, Archie, and she came to this town to pick it over, and half of Merrow will tell you so to your face and the other half will tell you after church. She took a thousand dollars off a girl with two babies and a husband on the bottom off Monhegan. Over the *telephone*. I'm Rosie's godmother. I've had Shannon at this table more nights since February than I could count. Don't you sit in my kitchen and mourn Lorelei Crane like she was your sister."

"She was funny," Archie said.

"Plenty of vultures are funny. Have you ever watched one walk?" Hollis picked her spoon back up. "Eat your chowder."

---

When he'd finished, she made him put the cat on the floor, which Malvolio took as a betrayal of the first order, and marched him into the front room.

She had pulled three things for him and hung them on the back of the door. He tried on eleven.

He tried on a tailcoat from a 1971 *Pygmalion* whose sleeves stopped halfway down his forearms. He tried on a scarlet cape and felt horns from a *Faust* nobody in Merrow would admit to having seen. He tried on a fez, and Puss in Boots's one boot, and Hollis barked — the short, sharp note that was her laugh — without looking up from the seam she was letting out. He was in front of the long mirror in a striped convict suit from some melodrama, holding the number board under his chin, when he stopped clowning and looked at himself.

"The lieutenant would like me in this," he said.

"Then take it off. Don't give him ideas." Hollis was sitting on the arm of the sofa with her glasses on and the tailcoat across her knees. "What did he do to you?"

"Nothing. He was perfectly fair. That's what's so awful about him." Archie started on the buttons. "He's got film of me pouring her tea. He's got film of me with my mouth on the horn. And he said I fool cameras for a living."

"You do."

"He made it sound like a crime."

"It isn't one, or they'd have to put a fence around Las Vegas." She bit off a thread. "That man sat in my booth this morning with a Dunkin' cup the size of a fire extinguisher and asked me to explain the dimmer board to him slowly. So I explained it slowly, twice, and told him there'd be a quiz on Friday." She held the tailcoat up to the lamp. "Archie Fairweather, I spent thirty-five years in a classroom watching people decide what they were going to be. I can spot a fool across a gymnasium with the lights off. You're not one. You're a show-off, which is a different thing entirely, and it isn't fatal."

He stood there in the convict trousers with his mouth open and found that he was grinning, for the first time all day.

"Is that your professional assessment?"

"It's going on your permanent record. Try the cape."

The cape was the last thing on the door: black wool, floor-length, with a high collar. It wasn't Gus's. Gus's lay folded on the trunk at home, lining out, shoulders in. But it was near enough, and when he put it on and turned to the mirror, the lamp behind him did something to the shape of it, and he stood still.

Hollis set the tailcoat down.

"I was in the booth that night," she said. "Did you know that?"

He didn't.

"Twenty-nine. Running the board for him with my coat on, because the radiators were worse then, if you can believe it." She was looking at him in the mirror. "From up there you can see past the edge of the proscenium into the stage-left wing. Nobody down in the house can. Before the Chair, he stood in that wing with his hand on the curtain for a full minute. The stage manager was beside himself. Gus didn't move. He was looking out at the second row." She got up and came over and lifted the cape off Archie's shoulders, briskly, and folded it over her arm. "I always supposed he was looking at you."

He hadn't known there had been a minute. He'd been ten years old in a clip-on velvet bow tie, and the minute had simply been part of the waiting.

"I didn't know that," he said.

"Well. Now you do." She was already turning away. "Sit down, I want to see you in boots."

He sat on the piano bench and pulled on a pair of cavalier boots from somebody's *Three Musketeers*, and Malvolio came in from the kitchen, looked at him with loathing, and climbed back into his lap. The photographs on the piano were just above Archie's shoulder: a cast photo from some long-ago *Our Town*, a black dog in a snowbank, a man in oilskins squinting into the sun, and a boy of about twelve on a dock, sunburned to the ears, holding up a mackerel in both hands as if it had put up a tremendous fight.

"Who's the big-game fisherman?"

"Danny. My son." Hollis handed him a waistcoat over the cat's head. "Try this."

It was plum velvet, with a shawl collar and jet buttons, cut long in the body. He stood up, dislodging Malvolio a second time, and put it on, and it fit everywhere a waistcoat had never once fit him off a rack.

"Made for a Scrooge in 2009," said Hollis. "He came down with shingles the week we opened. Nobody's ever worn it." She stood back with her head on one side and nodded. "That one. And the tailcoat, once I've let it out. And a box of white gloves for your ushers. Now go home. You've got a daughter."

She loaded him at the door: the waistcoat over one arm, the tailcoat over the other, the box of gloves on top, and a foil-covered dish of chowder for his mother wedged under his chin. The porch light was on. Under it, the long border along her fence had been cut down to the ground and the bed turned over for the winter, and Archie went down the path past it with his arms full of velvet and got everything into the hearse without spilling the chowder, which he counted as the first unqualified success of his week.

The news van was gone from the bottom of Shore Road. He was halfway up the drive before he noticed he'd been humming.

The kitchen light was on. June was at the end of the farm table in pajama pants and her school cardigan, with a pencil through her bun and her laptop open. It was half past nine. She had clearly been waiting up, and had clearly decided not to look as if she had.

"Put that down, Dad," she said. "I need to show you something. Upstairs."


---

# Chapter 18

## Harborview Antiques

June's room was the only room in Fairweather House that didn't do anything.

She had chosen it in August for exactly that reason, after an afternoon going around the second floor with a flashlight and a butter knife, tapping paneling and pressing knots in floorboards, while Lenny followed her with his hands in his sweater pockets and said "No" at intervals, meaning there was nothing there. It was a narrow room at the back of the house, over the kitchen, with a ceiling that sloped down to a single window on the harbor. A desk made from an old door laid across two filing cabinets. A narrow bed under a quilt Vivienne had bought at a church fair. Her Doc Martens side by side, toes to the wall. And above the desk, four thumbtacks pressed into the plaster in a neat rectangle, with nothing between them.

June sat cross-legged on the bed with the laptop on her knees. Archie sat on her desk chair, which had been built for someone a foot shorter, with his knees somewhere near his chin.

"Three conditions," said June.

"I love conditions."

"One, you don't take over. Two, you don't do anything tonight. Three, whatever you do do, you tell me first. Before. Not after."

"June—"

"Those are the conditions."

He looked at her: the pencil through the bun, the freckles, the set of her mouth. It was the face she wore at the podium, and he had once watched it dismantle the state champion from Bangor in a cross-examination that lasted four minutes.

"No," he said. "No conditions, no laptop. You're sixteen. You have school in the morning. There's a lieutenant downtown who thinks somebody in this house—"

"I know what the lieutenant thinks. Grandma told me at dinner. Then Lenny told me again, because he didn't think Grandma had said it right." June didn't raise her voice. "So I'm not going to sit downstairs doing chemistry about it. Those are the conditions. You can have them or you can go to bed."

He could have gone to bed. He considered it, honestly, for almost a second.

"Fine," he said. "Show me."

She turned the laptop around.

It was an auction site, the big one, open to a seller's page: HARBORVIEW ANTIQUES · MERROW, MAINE · member since 2011 · 214 ratings · 100% positive. Below that was a grid of little square photographs — a painted lobster buoy, a ship's lantern, a stack of Grange cookbooks, a brass barometer, a pair of oyster plates.

"I was looking for a poster," June said. "One of Gus's."

"What for?"

"My own reasons."

His eyes went, without permission, to the four thumbtacks over the desk, and then away again. He didn't ask. He'd been practicing not asking since a Friday afternoon in the library.

"I set an alert," June went on. "'Great Fairweather.' Mostly it's programs and reprints, and one man in Florida who keeps listing a signed photograph that isn't signed. But in September there were three real ones. Originals, framed. All sold by Harborview Antiques, of Merrow." She clicked. "Opera house provenance."

The first listing came up. The photograph showed a framed poster leaning against a wall, lit by a flash: a man in white tie striding into a brick wall with half his body already through it, and over him, in red circus letters, THE GREAT FAIRWEATHER WALKS THROUGH WALLS! *From the Merrow Opera House (Maine), removed during restoration. Original stone lithograph, c. 1956. Light foxing.* SOLD · $1,150.

Archie knew the frame. Black, with a narrow gold lip inside. It was the twin of the frame on the ASYLUM poster in the opera house stairwell, the one he touched for luck every morning — and a few feet past that one, on the landing, there was a clean pale rectangle on the old cream paint with two nail holes along the top. He'd walked past it forty times since August. He'd assumed Hollis had taken something down to be reframed.

"Removed during restoration," he said. "I'm the restoration."

"There are two more." June clicked. A 1949 one — THE GREAT FAIRWEATHER, RADIO'S MASTER OF MYSTERY — sold for $1,400 to someone in Connecticut. And a third, which made him lean forward so fast the little chair squealed: a girl of about fourteen in spangles and a feathered headdress, lying in a long box with her head out of one end and her feet out of the other, while a tall silver-haired man in a cloak stood over her with a saw. THE GREAT FAIRWEATHER SAWS A LADY IN HALF — TWICE NIGHTLY! 1966. SOLD · $975.

"That's your grandmother," he said.

June looked. "Seriously?"

"Four thousand times, she says. You'll hear about every one of them." He stared at the screen. "Somebody sold my mother to a man in Ohio for nine hundred and seventy-five dollars."

"In two pieces," said June. "Keep going. This is the part."

The fourth listing had gone up on Saturday morning at 6:48. It wasn't framed. The poster lay flat on a desk under a lamp, curling at the corners, with a soft crease across the middle where it had been folded for a long time: THE GREAT FAIRWEATHER · HALLOWEEN AT THE OPERA HOUSE · 1962. *Rare. From storage at the Merrow Opera House. Never framed.* Buy It Now, $1,200. Across the top of the page ran a gray bar: *This listing was ended by the seller on Sunday at 12:04 p.m.*

"From storage," said Archie slowly.

There was a big gray steel flat file at the back of the prop room, wide shallow drawers full of old programs and, Hollis had told him once, "who knows what, I haven't been to the bottom of it since Reagan." He'd never opened it.

"Now look at the third photo," said June.

It was the same poster, closer, the lamp moved for a better angle. In the bottom left corner, half cut off by the edge of the picture, was the corner of a dark green leather desk blotter with a gold-tooled border, and a few letters stamped in gold: *—LEY INSURANCE & REA—*

June didn't say anything. She let him have it. She'd learned that from someone.

"Gilley," said Archie.

"I've been in that office. Grandma took me in September when she was fighting with him about the homeowner's policy. He's got that blotter. It says GILLEY INSURANCE & REALTY on it in gold. I think it was his grandfather's." June pulled the laptop back toward her. "And I asked the seller a question. About the cookbooks. He answered in four minutes and signed it *W.*"

"You asked about a Grange cookbook to identify a seller."

"It's a good cookbook. There's a whole chapter on aspic."

Archie got up out of the little chair. He couldn't sit in it anymore — not because of his knees, but because the thing had started in him, the thing that always started: the small sweet click of a lock turning over in the dark. He walked the four steps the room allowed, to the window and back. He found he was holding his left wrist in his right hand and winding his grandfather's watch, slowly, without looking at it.

"Friday afternoon," he said. "Warren borrows Hollis's key to the prop room. For the inventory, he says. Saturday morning, before seven, there's a poster from the prop room for sale on the internet. Saturday morning, Hollis finds the prop-room door unlocked. Saturday night at the table, Lorelei leans across Ivy and says something in Warren's ear, and he goes white. I saw it, June. I was right there. I filed it and I forgot it." He turned. "And the sale doesn't close until the fifteenth of November. He told the whole Galley so. He wanted that developer; he spent twenty minutes at the town meeting microphone wanting him. If the man who bought the opera house turns out to be the man who poisoned a television psychic in it—"

"Dad."

"—then it goes back to the town, and the Boston people come back with their condominiums. And if Lorelei knew about the posters — and she knew everything, June, she had Ivy, Ivy researched every one of us — then she wasn't just whispering to him. She was *telling* him." He stopped at the end of the bed. "And the horn was locked in that prop room Friday night. And Warren had the key."

June looked up at him from the bed. Her eyes were very bright. Then she looked down at the laptop and did something unnecessary with the trackpad, and when she looked up again they were back to normal.

"That's a theory," she said. "It's not evidence. Everything after the blotter is you."

"Everything after the blotter is *beautiful*."

"That's not a legal standard."

"It should be." He sat down on the end of the bed, which bounced her, and she grabbed the laptop. "June. You found this. In an evening. The State Police have had three days and a lieutenant from Augusta, and they're looking at *me*. You found Warren Gilley's blotter."

"I found a corner of a blotter."

"It's a beautiful corner."

She pressed her lips together hard. It didn't entirely work.

"Condition three," she said.

"I'm going to ask Warren about it."

"Where?"

He didn't answer fast enough. He was already seeing the Galley at seven in the morning: the long counter, the booths full, Warren at the end stool in his bow tie. He was thinking of how a room leans in when you lay three things face down on a counter and ask somebody to pick one. He could feel it in his hands.

June closed the laptop.

"Dad," she said. "Don't do it in public."

"Junebug. Would I?"

She looked at him for a long moment with her mother's level eyes. Then she lay back on the pillow, pulled the church-fair quilt up to her chin, and rolled over to face the wall with the four thumbtacks on it.

"The printer's in Lenny's workshop," she said. "Don't wake him up."

He went down the back stairs in his socks with her laptop under his arm, already deciding which stool.


---

# Chapter 19

## Town Business

By seven o'clock on Tuesday morning the fog had come in off the harbor so thick that the Galley's windows showed nothing but white, and the bell over the door rang every minute or so to let in another wet wool coat and another person who had heard something.

Archie had been on the stool nearest the register since ten to seven, with a manila folder under his elbow and a mug of coffee he had poured himself.

That was new. Shannon Mayo was working the counter — ponytail, apron, the gray thermal shirt — and she'd come down the line with the pot, filling mugs without asking, *there you go, hon*, *morning, hon*, and when she'd reached Archie she had set the pot down on the counter in front of him and walked on. He'd poured. It was the first time in nine weeks he had poured his own coffee in the Galley, and he'd done it carefully, without spilling a drop, and nobody had seen him do it except everybody.

At the liars' table, Elwin and Dickie and Pickle looked out the window at the fog. Sixty-three.

Chief Pinkham was two stools down with a plate of corned beef hash. He'd looked at the folder when Archie sat down, and then looked at it again, and he hadn't said one word about the riverboat. Hollis Tibbetts was in the corner booth by the window with the *Packet* folded to the crossword and a pot of tea, her glasses on the end of her nose. She'd lifted two fingers to Archie when he came in, and gone back to seven across.

Warren Gilley came in at seven exactly.

Camel overcoat, the fog beaded on its shoulders. A bow tie, navy with small white dots — a good one, a Sunday bow tie on a Tuesday. In one gloved hand he carried a bunch of yellow chrysanthemums in grocery-store cellophane. He hung the coat on the hook by the door, took the end stool by the pie case, laid the flowers carefully on the empty stool beside him, and said, "Two poached, Bev, dry wheat toast, thank you," in the voice of a man who had said exactly that at exactly this hour for thirty years.

"Morning, Warren," said Bev.

"Good morning."

Archie picked up his folder and his mug and moved down the counter to the stool on Warren's other side.

"Flowers, Warren," he said. "Courting?"

"Good morning, Mr. Fairweather." Warren didn't look at him. He unfolded his paper napkin and laid it across his knee.

"I've got something I'd like to show you."

"I'm having my breakfast."

"It'll take a minute. It's a trick, actually. You'll like it." Archie raised his voice — not by much; he never needed much — and the counter heard him, and the booths heard the counter. "I'll need three volunteers. Bev?"

"No."

"Chief?"

"Archie," said Dale Pinkham, low. "Maybe not here."

"Corey!"

Corey — C-plus, two *e*'s in *medium* — was already sliding out of the second booth, grinning, and the woman from the hardware store came with him, and a big lobsterman in orange bib pants whom Archie didn't know at all. Then there were three people standing at the counter and the whole room had turned in its seats to see what the magician was doing now, and he felt it come up through the floor into his feet: the lean. A room leaning in. There was nothing in the world like it. There never had been.

He took three sheets of paper out of the folder and laid them face down on the counter in a row, in front of Warren's coffee cup. He moved them — not fast; this wasn't Harvard Square — left over right, right over center, the old three-card shuffle, the paper whispering across the Formica.

"Find the Fairweather," he said. "Corey."

Corey pointed. Archie turned the sheet over.

It was the WALKS THROUGH WALLS listing, printed in color in Lenny's workshop at five that morning: the poster in its black-and-gold frame, the description, the price. SOLD · $1,150. *From the Merrow Opera House, removed during restoration.*

The woman from the hardware store read it out loud. Somebody in the booths said, "Isn't that the one off the stairs?"

"That's the one off the stairs." Archie turned the second sheet. RADIO'S MASTER OF MYSTERY, $1,400, Connecticut. Then the third, slowly, because it was the best one — a girl of fourteen in feathers, in two halves. "And that," he said, "is Vivienne Fairweather, sold to a gentleman in Ohio for nine hundred and seventy-five dollars. She doesn't know yet. I'm letting somebody else tell her."

The room laughed — it couldn't help itself — and Archie laughed with it, and beside him Warren Gilley sat with both hands flat on the counter on either side of his plate, his eggs going cold, and didn't move.

"All three sold in September," Archie said, "by a seller called Harborview Antiques, right here in Merrow. Lovely seller. A hundred percent positive. Answers your questions in four minutes and signs off *W.*" He took one more sheet out of the folder. This one he didn't lay face down. He held it up. "And on Saturday morning at 6:48 — the morning after somebody borrowed Hollis's key to the prop room, the morning Hollis found the prop-room door unlocked — Harborview Antiques put up this. From storage. And if you look down in the corner of the photograph—" he laid it in front of Warren's plate and tapped it "—you'll see a very handsome green leather desk blotter. I wonder whose."

It went quiet in a wave, from the counter outward. Archie heard the coffee urn tick.

"Archie," said Pinkham.

He heard it. It didn't reach anywhere that made decisions. Every face in the Galley was turned his way, and he had been waiting sixty-three days for this room, and he went on.

"And Saturday night, at the table, with the cameras rolling, Lorelei Crane leaned across her daughter and whispered something in Warren's ear. I was sitting right there. I watched him go white." He turned on the stool to face Warren. "What did she say to you, Warren? Did she tell you she knew? Because the sale doesn't close until the fifteenth — you told us that, right here, Friday, in front of witnesses — and if something terrible happened in that theater before then, something bad enough, with the new owner right in the middle of it—"

He didn't finish. He didn't need to. He'd built it so he wouldn't need to. In one of the booths a woman said, "Oh, *Warren*," and someone else, in a voice Archie didn't know, said, "Good Lord."

Warren Gilley put down his fork.

He did it carefully, laying it across the rim of the plate at an angle, tines down. Then he picked up the sheet with the blotter on it and looked at it for a while, and set it down again on top of the others, and squared the edges.

"Carol," he said.

He said it to the counter. Then he turned his head and looked at Archie for the first time that morning, and his face wasn't white or pink or anything Archie had a word for. It was only tired.

"She told me Carol understood. About the pictures. She said Carol wanted me to know she wasn't angry." He said it quite clearly; everyone could hear. "My wife isn't dead, Mr. Fairweather. She's up the hill at Sea View. You've met her. You do your card tricks for her on Fridays. Marisol tells me she cried at one, but she cries at most things now, so I wouldn't take it to heart."

Archie heard himself not breathe.

He could see her. Early sixties. Neat gray hair. A cardigan buttoned wrong. *Is Warren coming? Is it Tuesday? He comes on Tuesday.*

It was Tuesday. The flowers were on the stool.

"Eleven thousand six hundred dollars a month," said Warren. He said it to Archie, and to the counter, and to nobody. "That's what it costs. Her long-term care policy ran out in June. I've taken a second mortgage on a house my grandfather built. I sold the boat in July. In September I took three of your grandfather's pictures off the walls of that building and sold them to strangers, and on Friday I took one more out of the flat file in the prop room with Hollis's key, because the October bill was due Monday. I locked the door after. I thought I locked it." He took the napkin off his knee and folded it in half, and in half again. "Gus Fairweather bought her about five weeks. She doesn't know where she is for most of it. But her window looks down on the bay, and some mornings she likes to watch the boats go out. I'm sorry nobody asked him."

Nobody in the Galley moved.

Archie looked down at the counter, at his mother in two halves, at the man walking through the wall, at the corner of the green blotter. He knew what came next in a trick: you turned the cards back over and showed them the whole thing had been undone. Gus had taught him the other thing first, at four years old, at the kitchen table. Once they've seen it, they've seen it.

"Sit down, Archie," said Bev.

He hadn't known he'd stood up. He sat.

In the corner booth, Hollis folded the *Packet*, let her glasses drop on their chain, and got up. She took Warren's camel coat down from the hook and brought it over and held it open behind him, and after a moment he stood and put his arms into it without a word. She picked the chrysanthemums up off the stool and put them in his hands.

"Come on, Warren," she said. "I'll drive you up. I want to see Carol anyway, and she likes my car."

She didn't look at Archie. She steered Warren toward the door with a hand under his elbow, and the room made way for them, booth by booth, people suddenly very busy with their eggs.

Archie went after them. He caught them at the door, with the bell still ringing overhead and the fog pouring in around their ankles.

"Warren." His voice came out wrong. He didn't own a voice for this. "Let me. Please. Sea View — all of it, whatever it costs, as long as it costs. Nobody would have to know. I'd consider it a—"

Warren stopped on the step and turned around. He was a head shorter than Archie, sixty-one years old, in a camel coat and a polka-dot bow tie, holding a bunch of supermarket flowers, and he drew himself up to every inch he had.

"No," he said.

"Warren—"

"You bought the opera house out from under this town with your checkbook, Mr. Fairweather." His voice shook, once, and then it didn't. "You are not going to buy my wife."

He turned and went down the steps with Hollis into the fog, and the white closed over them before they'd gone the length of the wharf.

The bell stopped. Archie stood in the open doorway with the cold on his face and the whole Galley at his back, not making a sound.

Then someone came up the steps out of the fog and stopped in front of him. Charcoal coat. Braid. Her phone still in her hand. She must have passed them on the wharf; he could see in her face that she had. Behind him, two stools down from the register, Dale Pinkham was looking very hard at his own phone and not up.

"Outside," said Tess Calloway. "Now."


---

# Chapter 20

## Outside. Now.

She didn't stop at the bottom of the steps. She walked him the length of the town wharf — past the bait shed and the stacked traps and the fuel dock, all the way out to the end, where the planks stopped above the water — and she didn't say a word the whole way, and for once neither did he.

The fog was starting to lift. It had pulled back off the wharf and hung over the harbor in a low white bank, so that the nearest moorings showed, *Second Wind* and *Miss Dottie* dripping on their lines, and everything past them was only shapes. Merrow Light was a green pulse somewhere inside the white, every six seconds. Off Shag Ledge the bell buoy was ringing slow. It was half past seven and cold enough to see his breath, and the granite memorial stood at the end of the wharf with the wet shining on it. Tess stopped beside it and turned around.

"Half that room can read lips," she said. "Keep your voice down."

"Sergeant—"

"Who printed those?"

"I did."

"Who found them?"

He didn't answer. She looked at him and he watched her work it out, and then he watched her decide not to say it, which was worse.

"Okay," she said. "Go ahead. Tell me."

He told her. He meant to tell it well — he'd told it beautifully fifteen minutes ago — but it didn't come out the same at the end of a wharf to one listener who wasn't leaning in. The key on Friday. The listing on Saturday. The unlocked door. The whisper. The sale. He heard it getting thinner as he went, and went faster to make up for it, and finished with, "He was selling the building out from under the town, Tess. He had the key. He had every reason in the world to want her quiet."

"Are you done?"

"I think so."

"Warren Gilley walked into my command post at four o'clock on Sunday afternoon and told me about the poster," Tess said. "Nobody asked him. Nobody had any idea. He sat down across the table from me in his bow tie and told me he'd taken it out of the prop room Friday, and listed it Saturday morning, and taken the listing down Sunday because he was ashamed of himself. He told me about the three in September. He told me about Carol. He told me what Lorelei Crane said to him at the table. He did all of that before I'd asked him a single question, because he thought it was the right thing to do." She hadn't raised her voice once. "I've known for two days. You've known for about ten hours, and you used it for a card trick."

"I didn't—" He stopped. He had.

"Now the part you'd have known if you'd asked me." She held up one gloved finger. "Warren was never within reach of anything she ate or drank. Ivy poured every drop that went into that woman except your tea, and Warren didn't touch your tea or Ivy's bottle." A second finger. "And forget the tea. Say you're right. Say it's the horn. Then Friday doesn't matter, and the key doesn't matter, and the prop room doesn't matter, because at 7:35 on Saturday night you put that thing in your mouth and blew a raspberry through it on two cameras, and here you stand. Whatever was on it went on after 7:35." A third. "Until they called cut, Warren was in his chair in the work light, on film. At 7:47 he went downstairs to the men's room. Dale Pinkham was standing at the stair door, and Dale watched that door until Warren came back up through it at 7:57." She looked at him. "You know Dale was standing there. You were standing next to him."

*Nerves*, the chief had said, kindly, watching Warren go by with a hand on his stomach. Archie had been talking about the riverboat.

"When he came back up," Tess said, "he sat down in a lit circle with six people and a cameraman around him and didn't get up again until the lights went out. He never touched it. I have the frames."

Archie looked at the granite. THOMAS MAYO, at the bottom, the letters still pale inside the cuts.

"And the posters were Gus's," said Tess. "He lent them to the opera house in 1985. There's a letter in the town office files that says so, on his stationery. They were never the town's to sell and they were never Warren's. They belong to Gus's estate." She paused. "That's your mother. I called her last night and told her. It's her decision whether there's a complaint."

"What did she say?"

"She asked if Warren was eating."

He laughed. It came out of him without permission, and it sounded terrible, and he stopped.

"And Carol Gilley," said Tess, "is two doors down the hall from my father."

He turned and looked at her.

She stood with her hands in the pockets of the charcoal coat and her face turned partly to the water. The fog had laid a fine wet shine over her braid. She didn't look at him.

"She sits with him at lunch. She thinks he's her brother Paul. He lets her. On Tuesdays Warren brings her flowers, and he brings Dad a roll of Necco wafers, because Dad likes the brown ones and nobody else on earth does." Her voice had gone flat — Maine-flat, every corner sanded off. "I've known Warren Gilley my whole life. He wrote the policy on our house. He's a pompous old man with a bad haircut, and he never did a dishonest thing in his life until this year. And you stood up in the Galley in front of forty people and called him a murderer with a magic trick."

"I didn't call him—"

"You didn't have to. You built it so you wouldn't have to." She looked at him then. "I know how a trick works, Archie. I've watched yours."

There was nothing to say to that. He knew there was nothing. So he said something anyway, because that was what he did when there was nothing to say; it was the only thing he had ever known how to do with a silence.

"All right," he said. "Not Warren. I was wrong about Warren. I'll be wrong about Warren in writing if you like, in the *Packet*, a full page. But hear me out—"

"No."

"—thirty seconds. Kev Duchesne."

Tess closed her eyes.

"Eleven thousand subscribers on Friday. Six hundred thousand this morning. I looked. He's monetized. He's got a mattress sponsor. Who wins the contest to sit in the most-watched séance in America? A nobody from Haverhill with a cartoon of himself on his chest. Nobody wins those contests, Tess. They're rigged. I've rigged them. So what if he's not a nobody? What if he's a plant — a rival network, one of the streamers, somebody who wants *The Other Side* gone and a hit of his own—"

"A deep-cover agent."

"I'm only saying it's elegant."

"Kev Duchesne," said Tess, opening her eyes, "sweated through his sweatshirt in my interview on Sunday. He asked me if I could get him your autograph. He cried when I asked for his home address." She took a breath. "He's lying to me about something. I don't know what yet. But he's not a spy, Archie. He couldn't spy on a houseplant."

Archie filed it. He felt himself file it.

Then — because she was still angry, and because he couldn't bear her being angry, and because his hands had always known what to do when the rest of him didn't — he stepped in close and lifted his right hand to make a point about rival networks, and his left went to the slash pocket of her coat, where the black edge of a notebook had been showing all along.

His fingers closed on it. Her hand closed on his wrist.

She didn't grab. She simply had it, gloved fingers around the bones of his wrist with his hand still in her pocket, and she held it there. He could feel the steel of her watch against the back of his hand. Neither of them moved. Out in the white, the bell buoy rang.

"Give it back," she said.

He took his hand out of her pocket empty. He hadn't even gotten the notebook clear.

"That's twice," said Tess, and let go. "The pen. And this."

"You're keeping count."

"I'm a cop. I keep everything." She moved the notebook to an inside pocket and buttoned her coat over it. Then she looked at him for a long moment, gray-eyed, with the fog behind her, and seemed to come to a decision she didn't care for.

"I'm going to tell you one thing," she said, "so you'll go home and stop tearing my town apart looking for a better answer. The crime lab's doing the trumpet first. Your trumpet. The lieutenant signed off on it last night. It goes ahead of everything else they've got." She turned up her collar. "That's it. That's all you get. Go home, Archie."

She went back up the wharf toward the gray Interceptor parked exactly between the lines in the little lot by the co-op. She didn't look back.

He stood by the memorial a while longer. The fog was coming apart over the harbor in long white rags, and the boats were coming out of it one at a time, and in the Galley's side window he could see a shape at the liars' table — Elwin, maybe — looking out at the water, or at him. There was no telling which.

*He's lying to me about something.*

"Sergeant," he called.

She stopped halfway up the wharf and half turned.

"That man wears his own face on his sweatshirt," Archie said. "He's never kept a secret from an audience in his life."

"He's my witness, Archie."

He was already walking — the other way, up the far side of the wharf toward Main Street, fast, with his hands in his pockets and his breath going on ahead of him. She hadn't said no. He decided that wasn't the same as no.


---

# Chapter 21

## Spectral Kev

By ten o'clock the fog had burned off, and Main Street was all hard bright light and long blue shadows. The yellow tape was gone from the town hall's side door. Officer Bissonnette had taken it down at nine, and Archie knew this because Kev Duchesne had narrated the whole thing, live, to six hundred thousand people.

Kev was on the town hall steps in a black beanie, with the SPECTRAL KEV hoodie under an unzipped puffer jacket and his phone on a little motorized handle that swiveled to keep his face in the middle of the screen.

"—so the State Police have *released the scene*, guys, which means technically, *technically*, it's just a building again, and I'm going to see if anybody will let me—" He saw Archie coming up the sidewalk. His face went through delight, terror and delight again. "Oh my God. Guys. Guys, it's—"

Archie put one finger over the lens.

"Hi, Kev."

"I'm live," whispered Kev.

"Not anymore."

Kev turned it off. Up close he hadn't slept since Saturday. He smelled of energy drinks.

"I want to go in," he said. "It's the Great Fairweather's *theater*. It's the site. Do you know how many people want to see that room right now? Six hundred and four thousand." He said the number the way other people said the name of a saint. "On Friday I had eleven thousand. I got a check, Mr. Fairweather. Nobody's ever sent me a check. A mattress company wants me to say their name at the top of every video. I don't even like the mattress."

"I have keys," said Archie.

Kev stopped breathing.

"One hour. In the dark, the way it was. Just you and me. No camera."

"Audio?"

"No."

"Okay. Okay." Kev was nodding hard. "What do you want?"

"Whatever you're not telling Sergeant Calloway," he said.

Kev went the color of the clapboards.

---

Marchand Media's black drapes still hung over every tall window in the hall, and with the doors shut it was as dark as it had been on Saturday night. Darker; there was no red lamp. By flashlight, Kev unpacked on the stage boards: two EMF meters, a spirit box that gabbled through radio stations, a thermometer on a lanyard, a roll of glow-in-the-dark tape, three spare flashlights and a gallon freezer bag of batteries. The round oak table was still there, its seven chairs pushed back where people had left them. The props were in evidence bags in Augusta.

Kev switched on the orange EMF meter — the one Archie had signed — and walked toward the stage-left wall, holding it out in front of him like a dowsing rod.

It shrieked. Every light went red.

"Oh my God," Kev breathed.

"That's the new wiring," said Archie. "Eleven thousand dollars of it, from a man in Thomaston. I can show you the invoice."

Kev moved the meter over the table. It said nothing at all.

"Huh," said Kev, sounding betrayed.

"Sit. Your own chair." Archie pulled out the one across from the head of the table and sat, and switched off his flashlight, and after a moment Kev did the same, and there was nothing.

Then, from somewhere inside the table, three sharp knocks.

Kev made a small sound.

"Toe," said Archie in the dark. "Against the leg. Or a knee up under the top — I think that was hers. Again?" Three more. "You can't place it, can you? In the dark you let the room tell you where a sound came from, and the room lies."

"Okay," said Kev, faintly. "Okay, but—"

Something cold and wet and soft touched the back of his neck.

Kev screamed. It was a high, pure, operatic note. It went up into the dark of the pressed-tin ceiling and came back off the balcony, and somewhere in the middle of it Archie distinctly heard the word *Amos*.

He switched on his flashlight and pointed it at himself. He was holding Kev's selfie handle, fully extended, and clipped where the phone should have been was Archie's own glove, dunked in Kev's water bottle.

"You left it by your chair," he said. "You didn't feel me take it."

Kev looked at the glove for a long time.

"That's the cold touch," he said. "That's — Warren yelped. Your *mom* laughed. That was a *stick*?"

"A rod. Hers was nicer — black, telescoping, it'd go up a sweater sleeve." Archie unclipped the glove. "Now the static. Give me your phone."

"I don't — I'm not—"

"The phone, Kev."

Kev handed it over. Archie held it up, filming the dark, and moved his thumb, and the screen went black.

"Crane Static," he said. "In the dark you can't see a hand press a button in a pocket. All you see is the picture go."

Kev didn't say anything.

"Apports," said Archie. He put the phone down and held his empty right hand up into the beam of the flashlight, turned it front and back, closed it slowly on nothing, and opened it on a playing card — the four of spades, standing up between his first two fingers. "Mediums did it with roses. Sometimes a live pigeon, if the room was big enough. Very good with widows."

"Do it again," said Kev, and then, "No. Don't."

Archie took the glow tape and a rolled-up program from the box of old ones in the wing, and wound three bands of tape around the paper cone where Kev could watch him do it. Then he switched off the light. The three pale green rings hung in the black. They rose. They tilted, and drifted, and swam up over the table, up and to the left, exactly the way they had on Saturday night, and stopped high over Kev's head and turned toward him.

"*Kevin*," said the paper horn, in a slow, deep, faintly Welsh voice.

"Please stop," said Kev.

Archie stopped. He brought the program down and switched the flashlight back on. Kev was sitting in his chair with both hands over his face.

"I did forty minutes on the Crane Static," he said through his fingers. "Forty minutes. With graphics. A guy in Brazil did subtitles. I told six hundred thousand people Brother Amos was in the room with me." He took his hands away. "I *felt* him."

"You felt my glove."

"Before that. Saturday. I felt *something*." He looked at the empty table. "Didn't I?"

Archie let that one go by.

"Kev. Who's paying you?"

"Nobody! The platform! The mattress!"

"And the prop room?"

Kev's mouth opened and nothing came out of it.

Then it came out all at once. Friday night, half past nine, Marchand's crew still up here rigging the drapes and the street door propped open. He'd carried a coil of cable off the van and up the stairs, "because nobody stops a guy carrying cable, that's like a rule." The prop-room door wasn't locked. He'd just turned the handle. "I swear. I don't even know how to pick anything." The trumpet had been on a middle shelf in an open case lined with red velvet.

"I just wanted to film it. Ninety seconds. I put on gloves first! Nitrile! I'm not an *animal*. I held it up to the light like you did, I looked down it." His voice dropped. "And I took one selfie. With it. Not touching my face. Near my face."

"And after Saturday?"

"I deleted the selfie." Kev swallowed. "Then I deleted it out of Recently Deleted. Then I googled *can the police recover deleted photos*. Then I deleted my search history. Then I googled *can the police recover search history*." He looked up, stricken. "Then I deleted that."

Archie didn't laugh. It cost him something.

"Kev," he said. "At 7:35 on Saturday night I put my mouth on that horn and blew a raspberry through it in front of two cameras and the entire crew of *The Other Side*. Whatever you did to it on Friday, I'm sitting here."

"Oh," he said. "Oh. You're the — you're like the control sample."

"I'm exactly the control sample."

Kev put his head down on his knees for a moment. When he came up he looked five years younger.

"I just wanted to see it," he said. "It's *the* horn. All eight seasons, and the church halls before that — there are photos from a Grange in Vermont with the same dent. Except for the Trumpet Hiatus."

"The what?"

"Oh-three to oh-six. Sister Lori stops using the horn in private sittings. Three years. Then it's back like nothing happened." Kev waved a hand. "There's a forty-page thread. Most people think Brother Amos told her to rest it. One guy in Ohio thinks it was her rotator cuff."
