"There's a *thread*."

"There are *eras*, Mr. Fairweather. The Church Hall Era. The Hiatus. The Cable Years."

"Do I have eras?"

Kev looked at him with pity. "Pre-Fenway and Post-Fenway," he said. "Obviously."

Archie decided he could live with that.

"Is it true about Gus's trapdoor?" Kev asked.

It was a few feet downstage of the table, two leaves cut into the boards with a flush iron ring at one edge. Archie should have said no. He knew it as he got two fingers into the ring and pulled, and cold air came up out of the black opening smelling of earth and mice and a hundred years.

He shrugged out of his coat — it would be filthy down there — dropped it over the back of Lorelei's chair, and went down the iron ladder first in his velvet jacket. The crawlspace ran the length of the stage, three and a half feet high, on brick piers, with a packed-dirt floor. Old programs and a rusted paint can lay in the dust.

"It's eleven degrees colder down here," Kev reported.

"It's a cellar, Kev."

They sat with their backs against a pier, knees up. Overhead the old building creaked in the wind off the harbor.

"Just tell me one thing," Kev said, quietly, to the dirt between his sneakers. "You've been in more dark rooms than anybody alive. I had your Challenge poster over my bed all through high school. *Nobody fools Archie Fairweather.* There must have been one time. One. Something you couldn't explain. You don't have to tell me what. Just tell me there was one."

Archie looked at the beam of the flashlight lying across the dust.

He thought of a church basement in Somerville when he was nineteen. A woman in a hot tub in Reno. Four hundred rooms, give or take, and the same hands in all of them, and him in every one with his eyes wide open, wanting.

"I've been looking since I was seven years old," he said. "I've never found one, Kev. Not one."

"Then why do you—"

"I haven't stopped looking."

Kev didn't say anything. After a while he nodded.

A shaft of white light came down through the open trap and swept the crawlspace and found them, two grown men sitting in the dirt under a stage with their knees up around their ears.

"The clerk downstairs," said Tess Calloway's voice from above, "says there's been somebody screaming up here for twenty minutes."

She was crouched at the edge of the trap. She took them both in: the dirt, the thermometer, the wet glove sticking out of Archie's pocket.

"Mr. Duchesne," she said. "Mr. Fairweather."

Kev looked up into the light, and then at Archie. Then he got his phone out with a hand that wasn't steady and scrambled on his knees to the foot of the ladder.

"I have to show you something," he said. "Both of you. I should have on Sunday. I'm sorry."

Tess came down two rungs. Archie crowded in beside Kev.

"I had another camera," Kev said. "In my hoodie pocket. Infrared, thirty-nine dollars, I cut a hole for the lens. The contract said no devices, and then she — and the lawyers—" He gave up and pressed play. "I started it when she said hands, everybody. It never got the static. Not once."

The picture was gray-green and grainy, shot low across the table: the edge of the tabletop, a candle stub, and lying on its side in the middle, the trumpet, its three bands glowing hard white in the infrared. The clock in the corner said 19:59. The house lights went, and the picture didn't change. Tinny voices came out of the phone — the prayer, Brother Amos, three raps. Kev dragged his thumb along the bottom of the screen. 20:06. 20:10. A peal of laughter that was Vivienne's. The horn lay where it was. Nothing moved near it at all.

At 20:17:04 a hand came into the top of the frame.

It was a woman's hand, and there were rings on three of its fingers, big stones that blazed pale in the infrared. It closed around the narrow end of the trumpet and lifted it out of the picture, and the bands went up and away to the left, and were gone.

Nobody said anything. Down in the dirt, very small, the phone said, *Shan.*

Kev stopped it.

"How long," said Tess, "have you had this?"

"Since Saturday."

"On Sunday you sat across a table from me and said you hadn't recorded anything."

"I said I hadn't recorded anything on my *phone*. Which was technically—" Kev saw her face and closed his mouth.

Tess looked at the frozen screen a while longer. Then she climbed back up.

"Upstairs," she said. "Both of you. Now."

Kev climbed. Halfway up he turned and looked down at Archie.

"They're going to arrest me," he whispered.

"You?" Archie put a hand on the bottom rung. "You're getting a thank-you, Kev. The arrest is for somebody else."


---

# Chapter 22

## Evidence

The select board room had been freezing on Monday. On Tuesday afternoon it was freezing everywhere except one corner, where the space heater Archie had ordered from Portland sat under Tess's end of the long table, ticking and glowing orange, with Tess's boots as close to it as the law allowed.

It was ten past two. Lieutenant Neil Brannock had driven down from Augusta at noon, and in the forty minutes since he'd walked in he had watched Kev Duchesne's footage seven times on Tess's laptop, said about a dozen words, and drunk most of an extra-large Dunkin' coffee. Kev sat at the far end of the table in his hoodie with his hands between his knees. At eleven a trooper had taken the little camera from him and an evidence tech had copied its memory card and given him a receipt, which Kev had folded into a very small square and kept turning over and over in his fingers. Archie sat across from him, because nobody had told him where to sit and nobody had told him to leave. On the wall above them hung framed photographs of every chair of the Merrow select board since 1899. The one nearest the door was a stern man in a bow tie who could only have been Warren's grandfather. Archie had been not looking at it for half an hour.

Brannock took off his reading glasses.

"Mr. Duchesne. When did you start the camera?"

"When she said hands, everybody. I pushed the button through my pocket."

"Did you edit the file?"

"No, sir."

"Trim it, filter it, brighten it, convert it, run it through any app at all?"

"I put it on my laptop," Kev said. "And then on my phone. And then I watched it about a hundred times. And then I didn't sleep."

"And on Sunday, in this room, Sergeant Calloway asked whether you'd recorded any part of the séance, and you said—"

"Not on my phone." Kev's face crumpled. "I know. I signed a thing that said they'd sue me for a million dollars if I brought a device in. And then she died, and I thought if I told, everybody would think I — and the lawyers — and I've got six hundred thousand—"

"Mr. Duchesne." Brannock didn't raise his voice. "I don't care about your contract."

Kev stopped.

"I care that a woman is dead and you sat on the best piece of evidence in this case for three days. I care that it reached me out of a crawlspace by way of my person of interest." He didn't look at Archie. He didn't have to. "And I care that I'm going to have to explain to the Attorney General's office how a file from a thirty-nine-dollar camera spent seventy-two hours on a YouTuber's laptop before it got anywhere near an evidence locker. It goes to the lab tonight for verification. If anybody has touched one frame of it, they'll tell me."

"Nobody touched it," said Kev. "I swear on—" He stopped, apparently unable to think of anything left that he could swear on.

"A trooper next door is going to take your formal statement. All of it. Friday night in the prop room too. Don't post anything. Don't post that you can't post anything."

Kev got up. At the door he turned and looked at Archie, and Archie gave him a small nod, and he went.

Brannock put his glasses on and pressed play an eighth time.

Archie watched it with him: the gray-green picture, the edge of the table, the white bands of the horn lying on its side in the dark. 20:00. 20:05. The tinny voices. Then 20:17:04, and the hand coming in at the top of the frame, rings on three fingers, the stones bright as new dimes, closing round the narrow end.

"That's her left hand," Archie said. "She was at the head of the table. It goes up to her left. That's the hand Ivy says she held."

"I can see what it is, Mr. Fairweather."

Brannock's phone buzzed on the table. He looked at the screen and picked it up. "Brannock." He listened. "Say again." He listened some more, and took a pen out of his shirt pocket and wrote something on the legal pad in small square capitals and underlined it. "Inside. Not the outer surface. Okay. Who ran it? — Okay. I want it in writing in an hour." He put the phone down and sat for a moment looking at what he'd written.

Then he turned the pad so that Tess could read it, and after a second, so that Archie could too.

ACONITINE — POS — INSIDE SMALL END (SEAM).

"The lab," Brannock said. "Inside the mouthpiece of the trumpet. In the seam where the metal's rolled over. The outside had been wiped. The inside wasn't, or not well enough." He took his glasses off again and pressed the bridge of his nose between finger and thumb. "So. You were right about the horn, Mr. Fairweather."

Each word sounded expensive.

"Holy Houdini," said Archie.

It came out of him the way the laugh had on the wharf, without asking. He was on his feet. He didn't remember standing. He'd been right. It wasn't the tea and it wasn't Bev's thermos and it wasn't the man who'd poured it; it was the horn, it had always been the horn — page one hundred and twelve, *lips to the small end*, half of Merrow could have told them on Sunday morning. He wanted to hug somebody. He looked at Tess, who took her boots off the heater and moved her chair six inches back, and he looked at Brannock, and decided against it.

"So it was on the horn," he said. "Then it's simple. Who got near the horn? In the pictures, it's always the stagehand."

"This isn't a picture," said Tess.

"No. In a picture they'd have cast it better."

"Sit down, Mr. Fairweather," said Brannock.

Archie sat. He was still grinning. He couldn't stop.

Brannock let him grin. He finished his coffee, set the empty cup down very precisely on the corner of the legal pad, and folded his hands on the table.

"Here's what your fan's footage does for me," he said. "Two things. One. It shows the victim's left hand free in the dark at 8:17, which means the daughter lied when she said she held it. You'd told us as much. Now I can show it to a jury." He turned the laptop so that the frozen frame faced Archie: the horn on its side on the table, three white bands. "Two. It shows that horn lying on that table, in the dark, untouched, from the moment the lights went out until the moment the victim picked it up. Nobody reached for it. Nobody brushed it. Seventeen minutes. Which means whatever was in that mouthpiece was in there before the lights went out."

Archie stopped grinning.

"Saturday night, at the hospital in Rockport," said Brannock, "you told Sergeant Calloway that when the lights came back up you were on the floor with the victim, and you saw Ivy Crane pick up the trumpet and wipe the small end with her scarf. Twice. Then put the scarf in her coat pocket." He waited. "Is that still your statement?"

Archie saw it then. He saw it all at once, the way you see a trick from the wrong side of the table, when the volunteer's back is turned and the whole method is lying in your palm in plain sight.

The daughter who poured everything her mother drank. Who sat at her left in the dark and said, twice, on tape, that she'd held her hand. Who'd been told she would be done when her mother said she was done. Who had stood in the full glare of the house lights while her mother was dying on the boards and picked up the horn and wiped the small end hard, twice, and put the scarf away in her pocket, and then wouldn't give it up.

He'd told Tess it was a reset. He still believed it. He believed it with his whole body; he had done it himself ten thousand times.

It didn't matter what he believed. It mattered what he'd seen.

He looked at Tess. She looked back at him with no help in her face at all, which was right. Out in the hall the radiator knocked. Under the table the heater ticked.

He could say he'd been on the floor with a dying woman's head in his arm. He could say it had happened very fast, and he couldn't swear to it now. He was a wonderful liar. He had lied to stadiums. He had lied to Lorelei Crane on the floor of his own stage, and she had smiled at him.

"Yes," he said. "That's what I saw."

Brannock nodded once and wrote it down.

"And I want something put in with it," Archie said. "I want it in the statement that a performer does that. After a show you wipe the props. You put them back. It's a reflex — it's how you protect the method. She's been doing it since she was twelve years old. It's called a reset."

"Put it in," said Brannock. "A jury likes a reason." He drew a folder from under the legal pad and opened it. "Here's what else I have. The remote you pulled out of the umbrella stand on Sunday kills the infrared feed on both of Marchand's cameras at the push of a button. We tested it. The prints on it that aren't yours belong to Ivy Crane." He turned a page. "The scarf. We took it from her room at the Spindrift last night, on a warrant. It's at the lab." He closed the folder. "And she inherits."

Archie said nothing. There was nothing left in him that would have helped.

"Sergeant," said Brannock, "get me an arrest warrant for Ivy Crane. Tampering with physical evidence, falsifying physical evidence. I'll call the AG's office and tell them what's coming."

Tess stood. For a fraction of a second she didn't move at all. Then she picked up her laptop and her coat.

"Yes, sir," she said, and went out, and didn't look at Archie on the way.

Then it was the two of them, and the heater, and Warren's grandfather on the wall.

"Trooper Lavoie is going to take your formal statement," said Brannock. "On tape, from the beginning. Thursday on. You're here voluntarily and you can leave whenever you want." He stood and picked up his empty cup. "I'd rather you didn't."

"I'm not going anywhere."

"Good."

Trooper Lavoie turned out to be a large, mild young man with a head cold and a small digital recorder, and he took Archie through it from the straitjacket on Thursday morning to the waiting room on Saturday night, and then took him back through the last two minutes of the séance four different ways. Archie answered the same way four times. He talked too much, the way he always did when he was frightened, and Lavoie let him and blew his nose and wrote things down. Brannock came and went with his phone at his ear. The tall windows went from white to gold to a deep cold blue, and someone brought the lieutenant another coffee, and nobody brought anything for Archie.

At 5:26 Lavoie said, "I think that's everything, sir," and turned off the recorder.

At 5:30 Archie came out through the town hall's front doors onto the granite steps.

The sun was behind the hill. Main Street was going dark from the bottom up — the Congregational church across the way still white at the steeple and gray below — and the streetlights were coming on one at a time along the sidewalk with a faint buzz. It was very cold. His coat was upstairs on the back of a dead woman's chair, and he couldn't face the stairs to get it.

A State Police cruiser came out of the lot beside the town hall, without lights, without hurry. Tess's gray Interceptor came after it. At the bottom of the hill, where Main Street met the harbor, both of them slowed, and turned left.

Left was Water Street. On Water Street was the Spindrift Inn.


---

# Chapter 23

## The Wrong Woman

They let him go at five-thirty. At five thirty-one he was running.

He had spent the afternoon in the select board room giving a formal recorded statement to a trooper with a head cold. They had asked him the same question four ways, and he'd given the same answer four times, because the truth didn't change when you turned it over: he had seen Ivy Crane wipe the small end of the trumpet with her scarf and push the scarf into her coat pocket, and he'd told Sergeant Calloway so that same night. Then they'd opened the door, and he had come out onto the town hall steps just in time to see two cars turn off Main Street at the bottom of the hill — a gray Interceptor and a State Police cruiser, no lights, no hurry — onto Water Street.

He knew what was on Water Street.

The hearse was in the lot behind the building. It took two tries to start on a cold night and woke every dog it passed. He ran.

Main Street went down to the harbor in one long hill, and he went down it in his velvet jacket with no coat, past the hardware store and the dark ice-cream stand with its sign that said SEE YOU IN MAY, his breath going out ahead of him in clouds. He was forty-seven and he had legs like a heron's, which were wonderful for stepping over things and no use at all for this. Below him the harbor was black, with the riding lights of the moorings strung across it.

He'd been rehearsing it all afternoon. *I told them why. I told them it was a reset — that every performer alive puts the props back clean, that I'd have done exactly what you did.* He'd said all of it into the trooper's recorder. He wanted her to hear it from him first. It wasn't much. It was what he had.

The Spindrift Inn stood at the corner with every window lit. The front door was open, letting the heat out. Both cars sat in the curved drive with their engines running, and on the porch steps, in a Merrow PD jacket with her thumbs hooked in her belt, stood Officer Kayla Bissonnette.

"Mr. Fairweather." She came down one step. "You can't go in."

"I'm not going in." He was breathing like a bellows. "I'm going to stand here."

"Then stand there."

He stood there. Kayla was twenty-four and had been told to hold a door by someone she admired, and she was holding it. Past her, in the front hall, Pru Haskell stood in the parlor doorway with a dish towel in both hands, and two troopers came down the stairs carrying cardboard cartons and a laptop in a plastic bag.

Then Tess came down the hall from the back of the house with Ivy.

Ivy's hands were behind her. She was in the big gray sweater and canvas sneakers with no socks, and Tess had a hand on her elbow, not gripping it, and was speaking in a low, even voice — words Archie had heard on television his whole life and never once heard a person he knew say to another. Ivy nodded at each sentence.

Her throat was bare. The police had the scarf; they'd come for it Monday night with a warrant. Her neck looked thin and much younger without it.

They came down the hall. Kayla stepped aside for them at the door, and that left Archie at the foot of the steps in the porch light with nothing between him and them but the cold.

"Ivy," he said. "I told them why. I told them it was a reset—"

"Mr. Fairweather." Tess didn't raise her voice. "Step back."

He stepped back one step. "—that every performer in the world—"

"Further."

He went further. Ivy stopped at the top of the steps, and Tess let her stop. For the first time since the day they'd met, Ivy Crane wasn't looking at his hands. She was looking at his face.

"You told them about the scarf," she said.

"I told them what I saw."

"You told them the truth." She said it flatly, a fact she was filing. "Mom always said that about you people. You tell the truth, and then you stand there waiting to be thanked."

Tess moved her on. Down the steps, across the gravel, to the back door of the gray Interceptor, which Kayla had gone ahead to open.

"Sorry — sorry, Archie, one sec." Teddy Marchand had come out the side door by the parking lot with a phone at his ear and a mango-smelling cloud of vapor around his head, and he gave Archie the little chin-lift that men in fleece vests give each other at airports. Into the phone he said, "No, I'm here. I'm literally watching it. It's awful. It's genuinely awful, nobody's saying it isn't." He listened. "Sure. But between us? The shot of her coming down those steps? The numbers are going to be insane."

Archie had never hit anyone in his life. He stood in the drive of the Spindrift Inn and found he was considering it with real interest, like a new trick.

He took out his phone instead.

Margaret Okafor practiced criminal defense on Exchange Street in Portland and was, by the unhappy agreement of every prosecutor in New England, the best there was. Twelve years ago, at her own written request, Archie had made her wedding cake disappear — six tiers, commissioned by her mother-in-law without consultation — in front of a hundred and forty guests. Margaret had cried, and told him, *Anything, Archie. Ever.* She had been on his speed dial since, between June and Lenny.

She picked up on the second ring. "Who's dead?"

"Nobody new." He said it loudly, in Teddy's direction, the way you play to the back row. "Margaret, a woman named Ivy Crane is about to be driven to the jail in Rockland. Tampering with evidence and falsifying it — that's what they're saying tonight. They'll say more tomorrow. She's twenty-nine and she hasn't got anybody. Her mother was Lorelei Crane."

"The medium."

"The medium. I want you to represent her. I'll pay. Whatever it costs. I don't want to know what it costs."

"You'll know. I'll send you a letter and you'll need to sit down." He heard a drawer, then keys. "Knox County, by nine. Don't speak to her. Don't speak to the police about her. Don't call me on anything but my office line. Is she going to want me?"

Archie looked across the gravel. Tess had her palm on top of Ivy's head, guiding it in under the roof of the car.

"No," he said.

"Fine," said Margaret Okafor. "They never do." She hung up.

Teddy had lowered his own phone. "Buddy. Are you paying for her lawyer? Because the optics of that—"

Archie walked past him.

Ivy was in the back seat with her hands behind her, sitting very straight, the way she'd sat in the blue plastic chair in Rockport. The door was still open. Tess was saying something to Kayla across the roof. Ivy turned her head and found him at the edge of the gravel.

"Don't help me," she said.

"Her name's Margaret," said Archie. "She'll be there at nine. Don't say a word until she comes."

Ivy looked at him a moment longer. Then Tess shut the door.

Tess came around the car and stopped beside him, close enough that he could see something in her face that wasn't the cold.

"Go and get some sleep," she said.

"Is that an order, Sergeant?"

"It's advice. The orders start tomorrow." She got in behind the wheel.

Pru Haskell had come out onto the porch with the dish towel. "Can I get you something?" she said. "Coffee?"

"No. Thank you."

"I'll make it anyway," said Pru, and went back in.

The Interceptor pulled out of the drive, and the cruiser after it, and Archie stood alone at the edge of the gravel and watched the two pairs of taillights go down Water Street along the harbor — past the dark co-op, past the fuel dock, red and small and getting smaller — until they turned at the corner by the Galley and were gone.

Then he went back up the hill for the hearse.


---

# Chapter 24

## Family Meeting

At ten o'clock on Tuesday night the kitchen at Fairweather House smelled of soup that nobody had eaten, and Archie was drawing a table.

He'd sat in the lobby of the Knox County Jail for an hour, on a bench bolted to the wall, until a steel door buzzed at nine-forty and Margaret Okafor came through it in a camel coat. Her client, she said, was eating a sandwich. Her client did not wish to see him. His part in this was the paying part, and he could do that from home.

So he was home, at the scarred farm table, with a yellow legal pad. He had drawn a circle for the table and seven small circles around it and labeled them in capitals — LORELEI at the top, then IVY, WARREN, MOTHER, ME, KEV, SHANNON — and a long skinny cone in the middle for the trumpet. Down the margin he had written *7:35 — TOOT*, and at the bottom of the page, *7:59 — KEV'S CAMERA.* Between the two there was a great deal of empty yellow paper.

A half-dollar walked across the knuckles of his left hand, over and under, first finger to little finger, and back. He didn't watch it. His hands had been doing that since he was eleven whenever the rest of him was busy.

Vivienne set a bowl of soup at his elbow. She was in a green kaftan with a man's gray cardigan over it.

"Eat that."

"I will."

"You've said 'I will' to three bowls," said his mother, and sat down across from him. "I counted."

June was at the far end of the table with her laptop open and her chemistry book shut beside it, which was the wrong way around for a Tuesday. When he'd come in she had looked up and said "Dad," in the voice that meant *I saw it on the news*, and he'd said, "I know," and that had been the whole conversation so far.

The back door opened on cold air. Lenny came in from the carriage house in all four sweaters, looked at the soup, looked at the legal pad, and pulled out a chair and sat down at the table — which he did not do, in this kitchen or any other, unless he meant to stay. Archie felt it like a hand on his shoulder.

Lenny turned the legal pad around with one finger. He brought his reading glasses down from his forehead and studied the drawing.

"Who touched it," he said, "after you?"

The half-dollar stopped.

It was the right question. Archie had spent four hours asking *why did she wipe it* and *what is she eating in there*, and Lenny, who in thirty years had never asked about anything but hardware, had walked in out of the cold and asked about the hardware.

"I put it down," Archie said. "Dead center. Where it was."

"Then."

"Then Teddy called cut, and Lorelei and Ivy went down to the green room, and I went to the back and talked riverboats with Dale." He made himself go through it properly. "Ivy must have come back up at some point. I looked under that table at seven-thirty and there was nothing under it but table, and in the dark something touched me on the forehead with a wet glove on the end of a stick. So she put the stick there afterward. I didn't see her do it. And Hollis. Teddy called from the wing for her to slide the horn over, it was fighting the candle, and she slid it a few inches and went off with her tray. That's all I saw. I was watching from a long way off."

Vivienne laughed.

It was her real laugh, the one from the bottom of her chest, and it was the first time he'd heard it since Saturday. "Hollis," she said. "Darling, Hollis Tibbetts taught half the State Police in this county where to put a comma. She isn't poisoning anybody. She hasn't the time. She's doing my hems."

At the end of the table June snorted into her sleeve, surprised out of it, and after a moment Archie heard himself laugh too. It was the first funny thing anybody had said in his kitchen in days.

Lenny didn't laugh. Lenny had not laughed since 1994. He put one blunt finger on the cone in the middle of the drawing.

"Then it's the girl," he said, "or it's nobody."

"It's not nobody." Archie took the pen back. "Somebody put something on that horn in twenty-four minutes, and I was at the back of the room being adored by a police chief." He tapped the empty margin. "The tripod cameras weren't recording. Teddy cut them — I heard him. *Cut controls, fifteen minutes.* And on Monday Brannock showed me every frame Marchand Media had given him. Me with the horn at my mouth. Me pouring the tea. The same two angles, dead level, wide. Tripods." He stopped. "Then the dark, from Kev."

Lenny waited.

"There was a third camera." The coin was moving again. "On a man's shoulder. Drifting around that stage the whole time I was at the back — the candles, the window, the empty chairs. If Teddy had handed that over, Brannock would have been dying to show it to me. Me, from a new angle, near the horn." Archie put the coin down flat on the pad. "Teddy didn't give it to them."

"Who's the cameraman?" said June.

"The big fella. Black watch cap. Gloves." Archie had stood beside the man on Saturday afternoon discussing the light off the harbor window. "His name is—"

"Marco," said Lenny.

"How do you know that?"

"He borrowed my gaffer tape. Thursday." Lenny pushed his glasses back up onto his forehead. "He brought it back." From Lenny, that was a recommendation for a medal. "You never look at the crew."

"I look at the crew."

"Thirty years you've had crews." Lenny folded his arms. "Who ran your flies in Las Vegas? Nine years. Every show."

Archie opened his mouth. A face arrived: young, sunburned, a goatee, a Dodgers cap. Nothing arrived with it.

"Dwayne," he said.

"Luis."

"Luis. Of course. Luis." He felt that land somewhere he didn't like, and didn't look at it.

June's laptop was already turned toward him. "Camera operators all have reels online," she said. "If he's Marco and he works for Marchand, he'll be on a crew list with his last name, and then he'll have an Instagram, and it'll say where he is. Give me twenty minutes."

"No."

"Dad, I found the posters in about an hour—"

"I know you did." He heard how it came out, and tried again, gentler, and it came out the same. "June. There's a woman in a cell in Rockland tonight because of something I said. I'm not putting your name anywhere near this. Not this one."

"It's not your name. It's mine." She said it very evenly. "I get to decide where it goes. I said not in public. Mattie sent me the video before first period."

"Not tonight."

June looked at him down the length of the table, and he watched her decide not to argue. He'd seen her do it at a debate podium, when she'd judged a round was lost and her time was better spent elsewhere. She closed the laptop.

"Okay," she said.

She picked up her chemistry book and set the laptop on top of it and went out and up the back stairs. He heard her door close. June had never slammed a door in her life. She closed them precisely, all the way, until the latch clicked, which was much worse.

Vivienne didn't move. She sat with her hands folded on the table and looked at him.

"Archibald."

"Don't."

"That child is not a volunteer from the audience," his mother said. "You don't get to call her up onstage when it suits the act and then thank her very much and send her back to her seat." She stood, took the cold soup, and poured it back into the pot. "Stop. For one night. Sit in this kitchen with the people who live in it. It will all still be wrong in the morning."

"I can't, Mother."

"I know you can't," said Vivienne. "That's why I asked."

---

The turret was cold, and he didn't turn on the lamp. Every six seconds Merrow Light swept green across the windows and showed him the trunk under the north window with its faded gold stars.

He sat down on the floor in front of it. He hadn't been up here in three nights. He didn't take out the notebook.

*The trunk, Sparrow.*

Seven letters. He had known since Saturday night, in the dark, with the green bands floating over the table.

He put his fingers on the first dial and turned it to S.

The brass was cold. The dial clicked softly into place. He turned the second to P, and the third to A, and his fingers went to the fourth.

Lorelei Crane had found that word somewhere — an old *Packet*, some neighbor quoted in November 1988. Her daughter had found it with a library card and a laptop, and Lorelei had put it into a tin horn and used it to make his mother go rigid in the dark, on camera, for money.

He took his fingers off the dial.

He was not going to let a dead woman and her research open Gus's trunk. Not with that word. He spun the three dials back to nonsense and sat with his hands on his knees.

When he looked up, Vivienne was in the doorway with a fresh bowl of soup, and she had been there long enough. She didn't say anything about the dials. She came in and set the bowl on the rolltop desk, steaming, and touched his hair on her way past, and went back down the stairs.

His phone buzzed.

*Teddy Marchand posted a picture of his boarding pass an hour ago,* June had written. *Cape Air, Owls Head to Boston, 9:05 tomorrow morning. I'm not volunteering. I'm just telling you.*


---

# Chapter 25

## Owls Head

Knox County Regional Airport was the smallest airport Archie had ever loved on sight.

It sat on a flat point of land in Owls Head, south of Rockland, with the bay on two sides and a windsock standing straight out over the runway. The terminal was one story of brown clapboard with a flagpole and a bench. Inside, there was a counter where a woman in a fleece weighed bags on a scale that might have come from a feed store, a single security lane staffed by one officer and a folding table, two rows of black chairs facing a wall of glass, and a vending machine that had run out of everything but peanut-butter crackers. Out on the apron, a white twin-engine plane about the size of a pickup truck with ambitions sat in the gray morning while a boy in ear defenders fueled it. Nine seats. Archie had counted the windows.

It was ten minutes to eight on Wednesday. The fog was coming off the water in rags, and the sky was low and white, the kind that meant rain by noon. He'd parked the hearse across two spaces in the short-term lot, where a family from Connecticut was now photographing it, and come in with nothing in his pockets but a deck of cards, a pen, and the watch.

He stood at the glass and wound the watch, because he was thinking. It had been Gus's. It lost four minutes a day no matter what anyone did to it, and he loved it more than anything he owned that wasn't alive. Twelve turns. In the glass he could see Teddy Marchand at the end of the second row of chairs, with a roller bag between his white sneakers, a phone in each hand, and an unlit vape between two fingers that he kept raising to his mouth and lowering again with a look of real grief.

Teddy saw him in the glass.

"Archie!" He was on his feet before Archie had turned around. "Oh my God. I was literally about to call you. This is — honestly, this is a sign. Sit. Sit, sit." He patted the chair beside his. "Hear me out."

Archie sat.

"Documentary," said Teddy. "Ninety minutes. Prestige. Not the show — the show's done, God rest her. The *real* story. Working title, *Crane*. The medium and the magician. The bet, the respect, the night in the dark. You held her while she—" He touched his chest with a phone. "I've got a call with a streamer at two. If I can tell them you're in, it's not a pitch anymore. It's a deal."

"I'm in," said Archie.

Teddy's mouth stayed open.

"An exclusive," said Archie. "My first sit-down since Las Vegas. You can even ask me about the Glass Box. I won't answer, but you can ask." He smiled. "In exchange for Marco's card."

Teddy's face did something very small, and then nothing at all. Archie had watched four hundred mediums do exactly that in the half second after the right question.

"What card?"

"The handheld. Saturday night. Marco shot the table for the whole quarter of an hour before the séance. I watched him do it."

"Exteriors." Teddy shook his head, sorry for him. "Harbor, lighthouse, the drive in on Route 1, the town. I told the lieutenant. That card's in Los Angeles, Archie. It went out with the drives on Monday."

"I was there, Teddy. He shot the candles."

"He shot the candles and then he went outside and shot the harbor."

"You told a lieutenant of the Maine State Police, in a homicide, that a piece of evidence was in California." Archie said it pleasantly. "I don't know what they call that up here. I'd bet Lieutenant Brannock knows. I'd bet he can spell it."

"Are you threatening me?"

"I'm describing your weekend."

Teddy looked out at the plane. The boy in the ear defenders was coiling the fuel hose. "Get a lawyer to tell you what you'd be describing," he said. "I've got three."

"Friday night," said Archie, "I was walking out the back of the Spindrift past a half-open door, and I heard you on the phone. The key-person policy. Runs through the end of the season. That's the point."

For a second Teddy looked genuinely lost. Then he laughed, short and not at all happily.

"You think I — oh, Archie. Archie, Archie." He leaned back in the chair. "Okay. Here's the policy. It's on the company's books, and every dollar it pays goes straight back to the network, for the eight episodes I now can't deliver and the special I can't air. It makes *them* whole. It makes me nothing. That's what key-person means. You know what her dying got me?" He counted on the edge of his phone. "A twelve-city live tour in March — arenas — canceled. A spinoff with a dead star. Seasons nine and ten in a drawer, waiting on a signature I'm never getting. I'm not a suspect, buddy. I'm a casualty."

"She was quitting," said Archie.

"She quit every August. I talked her back every September. Eight years." Teddy shrugged. "It's the job. I talk people out of things."

"Then why lie about the card?"

Teddy was quiet. He turned the unlit vape over in his fingers.

"Because I've watched it," he said. "Sunday morning, in my room, with the door locked. At seven forty-four the daughter comes back up the stairs on her own and slides a stick under the tablecloth. A telescoping rod, with a glove on the end. So she can touch Warren Gilley on the ear in the dark." He looked at Archie. "You hand that to the police, it's evidence. It's in a courtroom. It's on the news, frame by frame, with a little red circle around her hands. Eight seasons, a hundred and four episodes, eleven international territories, and the whole library turns into a girl with a stick. That's not a show anymore. That's a class action."

"And Ivy sits in a cell."

"Ivy wiped the thing with her scarf in front of you. That's not on me."

They sat. A man in a Patriots jacket came in out of the cold and asked the woman at the counter if the Boston flight was going, and she said it was, and he went and sat by the vending machine.

"I found her, you know," said Teddy. He wasn't looking at Archie. He was looking at the little plane. "Worcester. A Knights of Columbus hall. Folding chairs, a raffle table. She did forty minutes for maybe sixty people, and then she sat with every one of them after, and she remembered all their names. I'd driven out from Boston to laugh at her." He turned the vape over again. "Eleven years. I liked her, okay? That's the stupid part. I really liked her."

Archie didn't say anything. He had liked her too, and he'd known her two days.

Teddy put the vape in one vest pocket and unzipped the other. He took out a little hard plastic case, the size of a matchbook, and opened it on his knee. Inside, on a bed of gray foam, sat a black memory card.

"Here's what happened," he said, in a flatter voice, one that had spent a lot of time on the phone with lawyers. "I was packing this morning, and I found this in Marco's kit. He must have missed it when he shipped. I'm a private citizen about to get on a plane. I'm handing it to a witness, not the police, because you're the expert. What you do with it is between you and the State of Maine." He closed the case. He didn't hand it over. "You never got it from me. You got it from Marco's kit."

"Fine."

"And I want something."

"The documentary. I said yes."

"There's no documentary. Not after this." Teddy held the case up between two fingers. "How'd you do the Glass Box?"

Archie had seen it coming since he'd made the joke himself, ten minutes earlier. Thirty years in the business: you didn't mention the thing a man wanted most unless you were ready to be asked for it. He had seen it coming, and it went through him anyway.

Four people on earth knew how the Glass Box worked. Lenny, who had built it in a rented garage in Henderson, Nevada, over eleven months, starting in August. Two stagehands at the theater, who had signed papers thicker than the box. And Archie. That night in the hotel in Boston, an hour before he promised her a real year, June had looked up from her room-service fries and asked him how he'd gotten out, and he'd said, *Magic, Junebug,* and she had gone back to her fries, and he'd thought it was charming.

"I know what I'm asking," said Teddy. "I'm not stupid. I've watched that tape three hundred times. My kid's got a theory with mirrors."

"It's not mirrors."

"Don't tell me what it isn't. Tell me what it is."

Archie looked at the case between Teddy's fingers. He thought about Ivy in the back seat of the Interceptor with her hands behind her, sitting up straight. He thought about her bare throat.

He got up and went to the coffee counter by the security lane and came back with a paper napkin. He clicked his pen and wrote on it in capitals, pressing hard so it wouldn't tear:

*I, THEODORE MARCHAND, WILL NEVER TELL, SELL, FILM, SKETCH, IMPLY, HINT AT OR HUM THE METHOD OF THE GLASS BOX TO ANY LIVING PERSON, IN ANY MEDIUM NOW KNOWN OR HEREAFTER DEVISED.*

"'Hereafter devised,'" said Teddy. "Who's your lawyer?"

"The best one in New England. I'm paying her a great deal of money as of last night." He held out the pen. "Sign it."

Teddy signed it. Archie signed under him, folded the napkin twice, and put it in his breast pocket. Then he leaned in close, so that the man in the Patriots jacket couldn't have heard if he'd tried, and told Teddy Marchand how the Glass Box worked.

It took about ninety seconds. Good methods always did.

He watched Teddy's face while he talked. He'd seen it before, on the very few people he'd ever told anything: first the hunger, then the attention, the eyes narrowing, following it, keeping up — and then, right at the end, when the last piece went in, something that fell. Every method ever invented was smaller than its trick. It had never once stopped hurting to watch.

"That's it?" said Teddy.

"That's it."

"Huh." Teddy sat back. He looked out at the plane, and at Archie, and at the plane again. "Huh."

He held out the little case. Archie took it.

Over the speaker, the woman in the fleece announced that Boston was boarding. Teddy gathered his phones and his roller bag and went through the folding-table security. At the door onto the apron he turned around, and for a second Archie thought he might say something else.

"Huh," said Teddy, and went out across the wet tarmac to the plane.

Archie stood at the glass. The little white plane taxied to the end of the runway with its propellers blurring, turned into the wind, ran, lifted off over the bay, and went up into the low white cloud and was gone. The windsock went on standing straight out over the empty runway.

The card was in his breast pocket, behind the napkin. He could feel the corner of the case through his shirt.

He had just taken possession of physical evidence in a homicide, from a man who'd lied to the police about it, in an airport, in exchange for a napkin. Lieutenant Brannock was going to kill him. Archie walked back out to the hearse past the family from Connecticut, who asked if they could get one with him in it, and he said yes, and stood by the gold lettering and smiled for them while the first rain came in off the bay.


---

# Chapter 26

## Forty Seconds

At eleven on Wednesday morning the opera house was still dressed for a funeral.

Marchand Media's blackout drapes still hung over every tall window; Hollis had volunteers coming at four to take them down. The cables and tripods were gone, and the police had released the hall on Tuesday morning and taken the props to the lab. What was left stood center stage under two work lights on stands: the round oak table and its seven chairs, exactly where the crew had left them, with little torn corners of the evidence tech's tape still stuck to the boards around the legs. His buffalo-check coat still hung over the back of Lorelei's chair, where he'd dropped it yesterday before going down the trap. He put it on. Rain ticked on the windows behind the drapes.

Lenny had brought a laptop up from the workshop, and a card reader, and fifty feet of orange extension cord, which he'd run from the outlet by the prop room in the firm belief that electricity was out to get him. He set the laptop on the table in front of Lorelei's chair and held out his hand.

Archie put the little plastic case in it.

Lenny opened it, looked at the card, and didn't plug it in.

"What did it cost?"

There was no point lying to Lenny. There never had been. "The Glass Box," Archie said.

Lenny brought his reading glasses down off his forehead, looked at him through them, and pushed them back up.

"You told him."

"On a napkin. He signed an agreement."

"He's a television producer."

"It was a very good napkin, Lenny."

"Eleven months," said Lenny. "In a garage. In Henderson. In August."

"I know."

"A hundred and nineteen degrees. The resin wouldn't set."

"I know, Lenny."

Lenny slid the card into the reader and plugged the reader into the laptop with more force than any connection has ever needed, and after that he said nothing at all, which for Lenny was the same as shouting the building down.

There were footsteps on the stairs. June came up into the hall at ten past eleven in her Doc Martens and a brown thrift-store cardigan, with her laptop bag on one shoulder and her face set. She should have been in third-period chemistry twenty minutes inland. June had never cut a class in her life; she had let the bus go by at the end of Shore Road that morning, and had evidently worked out where Lenny and his extension cord were going. She stopped at the top of the center aisle among the stacked chairs and stood there, ready to be sent home.

Archie looked at her down the length of the hall. Then he went to the table and pulled out a chair — the one at the right of Lorelei's place — and stood behind it.

"That's Shannon's," he said. "You can be Shannon."

June came down the aisle and up the steps and sat in it. She took the pencil out of her bun and a legal pad out of her bag and laid them on the table, squared to the edge.

"Okay," she said. It wasn't last night's okay. He'd take it.

"What did you give Teddy?" she said. "For the card."

Lenny, at the laptop, didn't look up.

"The Glass Box," said Archie.

June looked at him for a long moment. Then she wrote the date at the top of her legal pad and underlined it twice.

The footage came up.

It was a low angle from somewhere near the stage-left wing, handheld but steady, the picture warm and grainy under the work light, with a timecode running in the corner: 19:36:02. The first thing on the screen was Archie's own elbow.

He watched himself at the table in his black shirt with the sleeves pushed up. He watched himself unscrew the cups from Bev's thermos and pour, and hold one out across the table to a woman in black velvet, who laughed and moved her daughter's hand aside and took it. There was no sound for the toast; Marco had been too far away. Lorelei drank half and set the cup down. Archie watched her set it down, and for a second he couldn't watch anything else.

"Seven forty-one," said June, writing. "Cut."

On the screen Lorelei rose and went out of the frame toward the stage-right stairs, and Ivy rose and followed with the water bottle and the lozenges. The work light went down. The camera stayed on the table. On the audio, faint, came Teddy's voice from the wing: *Marco, stay on the table for me. Give me the table breathing. That's the cold open.*

"That's why he never stops," said June.

The table breathed. Empty chairs; the bell, the tambourine, the slate; the trumpet in the center, its three pale bands catching what light there was.

At 19:43:50, Ivy Crane came back up the stairs alone.

She came in from the right and went straight to her own chair, the one at her mother's left. She didn't sit. She bent, half turned from the camera, and her hands went under the overhang of the tablecloth at her own place and slid something in — long and thin and black — and smoothed the cloth down over it. It took about ten seconds. Then she straightened and went back out of the frame the way she'd come.

The trumpet sat in the middle of the table the whole time. Her hands never came nearer to it than the table's edge.

"That's the rod," said Archie. "That's how Warren got kissed on the ear in the dark."

Lenny took a tape measure out of one of his sweaters and ran it from the edge of the real table, at Ivy's place, to the center.

"Twenty-three inches."

"Run it back," said June. Lenny ran it back. Ivy's hands went under the cloth and came out empty and went away. "She never reaches," June said, and wrote it down.

Something was rising in Archie that he didn't trust yet. He sat on it.

Seven forty-six: Kev Duchesne leaned into the frame with his phone held out in both hands, photographing the table like a man photographing a celebrity's lunch, and Teddy's voice came sharp off camera — *Phones away, please, sir* — and Kev jumped and backed off toward the radiators on the stage-left wall, where he stayed, a dark hoodie at the edge of the picture. Seven forty-seven: Warren Gilley crossed behind the chairs with one hand on his stomach and was gone. Seven forty-eight: Hollis in a pair of black stage gloves, bending over the table with a long match. She lit the tall pillar candle in the middle, a hand's width from the trumpet, and then the small ones around the edge. She went off and came back with a tray and went around the circle setting a glass of water at every place. Vivienne came into the frame and fussed with a black shawl over the back of her chair until it hung exactly so. Shannon came up the steps and sat down at her place and put her hands in her lap.

"Does he ever point it somewhere else?" said June. "Ever?"

"Find out," said Archie.

She took the trackpad and did it the way she did everything, with a method: forward ten seconds at a time, writing down what was in the frame at each jump — *table, table, table* — all the way down the page. Lenny watched her with his arms folded. Archie watched the candles.

At 19:51:10 the picture went white.

It bloomed out from the center — the tall flame and its reflection in the polished curve of the trumpet right beside it, flaring together in the lens until the whole frame was a smear of gold — and the camera swung away fast, to the right and up, and settled on the one bare window at the stage-left end of the hall. Out in the dark harbor the riding lights of the moorings were coming on, one by one.

On the audio, Teddy: *Hollis — sorry — can you slide the horn left, it's fighting the candle.*

The harbor. The moorings. A little bob as Marco adjusted something. The harbor.

At 19:51:50 the camera swung back to the table.

The trumpet sat a few inches to the left of where it had been, out of the candle's way. Hollis was walking off toward the stage-right wing with her empty tray under her arm. Behind her, a young man from the crew in black gloves knelt to tape down a cable, and at the very edge of the frame Marco's own black-gloved hand came up and did something to the lens and went away again.

"Forty seconds," said June.

She ran it again, and a third time. Then she went through the rest of the card, ten seconds at a time, to the end, and came back, and put her pencil down.

"That's the only time," she said. "From thirty-six to fifty-nine he never leaves the table, except for those forty seconds."

"So who's there?" said Archie. "In the forty seconds."

June read it off her list, flat, the way she'd read out a citation. "Mrs. Tibbetts, with the tray. Grandma, at her chair. Shannon, at her place. Kev, by the radiator." She looked up. "And the crew going back and forth. You can see two of them at least. Everybody back there's in black gloves."

Archie got up and went around the table to the chair beside his own, the one with the shawl in the footage, and stood behind it. He put his hand out over the tabletop. The center was right there. Anyone at that table could have touched it without leaning. That was what a round table was for.

His mother had been standing exactly here.

He took his hand back.

"Walk it," he said.

They walked it. June sat in Shannon's place with her phone's stopwatch running and reached for the center: three seconds. Lenny stood at the radiator for Kev, walked to the table, touched the middle of it, and walked back: twelve seconds. Archie stood where Hollis had stood and slid an invisible horn; then he stood in Vivienne's place and reached. Every one of them fit, with time left over to stop and say good evening.

"And anybody from the crew could have walked through," said June.

Lenny looked at the radiator as if it had personally let him down.

At 19:55 on the laptop, Archie walked into the frame himself. He watched himself cross to his mother's chair and sit beside her and put his hand on the table, and turn it over when she laid hers on top. On the screen, in the candlelight, she looked very small.

"You're not in the forty seconds, Dad," said June. "You're not on camera at all until fifty-five."

"I was at the back with Dale."

"Dale was with him," said Lenny.

"I know," said June. "I'm saying what the lieutenant's going to say."

Archie barely heard her. He was looking at the frozen frame on the laptop — the little flames, the empty chairs, the horn moved a few inches to the left — and the thing he'd been sitting on since seven forty-four came up through him anyway, all at once, without permission.

"She never touched it," he said. "Ivy. Not after I put it down. She was at her place for ten seconds with her hands under the cloth, twenty-three inches away, and then she was downstairs until seven fifty-eight, and then she sat down, and then Kev's camera has it lying there in the dark until Lorelei picks it up herself." He was on his feet. He didn't remember standing. "The rod's a fraud, fine. The scarf's a reset. The whole act's a fraud. But she never laid a finger on that horn after I had it at my mouth."

"Then it's one of four people," said June. "Or the crew."

"Ivy didn't do it," said Archie. "That's what matters."

June looked at him and opened her mouth, and then shut it again and wrote something on her legal pad that he didn't read.

Lenny ejected the card, put it back in its little case, and held it out.

"Where are you taking it?"

"Tess," said Archie. He took it, and he was halfway down the aisle before Lenny had finished winding up the cord.


---

# Chapter 27

## Narrowed Down

By two o'clock it was raining the way it only rains on the coast of Maine at the end of October: sideways, off the harbor, cold enough to sting, and in no hurry at all.

Lieutenant Neil Brannock's car was a dark gray Explorer with state plates, parked nose-in at the back of the town hall lot under a bare maple. The driver's door stood open. Brannock sat sideways in the driver's seat with his feet out on the running board and a laptop on his knees — Tess's, with a State Police sticker on the lid — and his reading glasses on, which he wore with the resignation other men bring to a cast. Rain was coming in on his left shoulder. He didn't seem to have noticed. Beside the open door, in her charcoal coat with the hood up and her braid gone dark with wet, stood Tess Calloway, holding a clear evidence bag with the little black card case inside it. Archie stood beside her in his buffalo-check coat, which had begun to smell like a sheep.

He had called her at half past twelve from the hearse. She'd listened, and said nothing for so long that he took the phone away from his ear to check it, and then she'd said: *Parking lot. Two o'clock. He leaves for Rockland at ten past. You get ten minutes.* She had hung up before he could thank her. He understood that she'd taken it to Brannock herself, and what that had cost her to do, and he had been grateful about it for an hour and a half.

"Lieutenant," he'd begun, when he came across the lot, "before you say anything, I'd like you to watch something remarkable—"

"I've watched it," Brannock had said. "Three times. Sergeant Calloway insisted."

Now he watched seven forty-four once more — Ivy's hands under the tablecloth, twenty-three inches from the horn — and then sat back and took the glasses off.

"Where did you get this?"

"It was found in the cameraman's kit," said Archie. "By Mr. Marchand. Who gave it to me. As a witness."

"When?"

"This morning."

"Where?"

There was no point. "The airport in Owls Head. Around eight-thirty."

Tess turned her head and looked at him. He felt it on the side of his face like a draft from an open door.

Brannock didn't look at Tess. He folded the glasses and put them in his breast pocket. "On Sunday Mr. Marchand told me that card was exteriors and had gone to Los Angeles with his drives. I'll be having a conversation with Mr. Marchand's lawyers about the word *exteriors*." He looked at the laptop. "So the only footage that clears my suspect has been hand-delivered to me by a man who fools cameras for a living, who got it in an airport from a man who lied to me about it, in exchange for —" He stopped. "What did you give him?"

"An interview."

Brannock waited.

"And something else," said Archie. "It isn't relevant."

"Everything's relevant until it isn't." Brannock closed the laptop and handed it out to Tess, who tucked it under her coat. "This goes to our forensic video people in Augusta before it's worth anything to anybody. Metadata. Continuity. Whether the card is from the camera it says it's from. If it's clean, it's clean."

"It's clean."

"I hope so. I really do." He meant it; Archie could see that he meant it. "Now. You want me to call the jail and open the door."

"She never touched the horn after I put it down. You just watched it."

"I watched twenty minutes of a tablecloth. Here's what I have, Mr. Fairweather, and I'd like you to listen to all of it, because I'm only going to say it once, in the rain." Brannock held up a thumb. "Ms. Crane wiped the mouthpiece of the murder weapon with her scarf in front of you, and the scarf came back from the lab with aconitine on it and her mother's lipstick. She told my sergeant her mother held her hand the whole time, and she's on Mr. Duchesne's tape letting go of it. She ditched a remote with her prints on it in an umbrella stand. And she laid out every prop on that table at seven-twenty, alone, before you ever got near it, while the room was still taking its coats off. That's what put her in a cell. Nothing on this card changes one word of it."

"That's tampering. That's fraud. That isn't—"

"I know what it is. The tampering stands either way; she did it." Brannock looked out at the rain. "Now your card. Your card clears her of the murder on one condition, and the condition is seven thirty-five. Your little trumpet solo." He looked up at Archie. "Did your lips touch the metal, Mr. Fairweather? Or did you make a noise near it and let a roomful of people and two cameras believe they did?"

"I put my mouth on it."

"I'm sure you did. You're very convincing. It's the job."

"Then swab it. You'll find me."

"We did. It's a mixture. A small one — the victim, and whatever the scarf left behind, and possibly somebody else. The lab calls a third person inconclusive and is running six weeks behind." Brannock didn't sound pleased about it. "So I can't put you on it, and I can't keep you off it. All right. Say it was real. Say you tooted, honest as the day. Then the horn's clean at seven thirty-five, and I've got your forty seconds, and in your forty seconds I've got a retired schoolteacher who moved the thing at the producer's request, in gloves, with no connection to the victim we can find. I've got a fan by the radiator whose channel has made more money since Saturday than in the five years before it. I've got a widow who'd paid the victim a thousand and fifty dollars and was waiting to hear from her husband." He paused. "And I've got your mother."

"My mother."

"Your mother, who cut a vase of monkshood out of your garden last week for the kitchen table and told us so herself, very helpfully. Your mother, who's in that frame at her chair, two feet from the horn, with her hands on the back of it." He said it plainly, with no pleasure in it at all. "I don't think your mother poisoned anybody. I'm telling you what that tape says to a defense lawyer who's never met her. A mother and her son. He does the talking; she's at the table. That's a story a jury can follow."

Rain drummed on the roof of the Explorer. Archie found he had nothing to say, which happened to him perhaps twice a year.

"And if it wasn't real," said Brannock, "then I'm back where I was on Monday. Then I've got Ms. Crane at seven-twenty, alone with the props. Or I've got the one man in that building with a motive — two million of them — and poison growing in his own dooryard, and the best pair of hands in the country, putting the horn to his mouth on camera and fooling the room for the length of a raspberry." He let it sit. "Either way, your card doesn't open a door. It takes a door I'd nearly finished with and opens it back up onto you."

"Where would Ivy even get aconite? She lives in Los Angeles. You've been through her room and her car and her phone—"

"Half the dooryards on this peninsula grow it, including yours. She was here three days before the séance, and she knew to the inch what went into her mother's mouth." Brannock swung his feet in out of the rain. "I'm not telling you it's a lovely case. I'm telling you it's a case."

He put the key in the ignition. Then he looked up at Archie, and his face changed — not softer, exactly. Older.

"One more thing, and then I'm going to Rockland. Since Saturday you've been to see the victim's daughter, the widow, the producer, the fan, and the chair of the select board, and you confronted one of them in a diner in front of forty people. Every person on that tape is a witness in a homicide. If you go near any of them again about this case, I'll treat it as tampering with a witness, and I'll charge it. I won't enjoy it. I'll do it anyway." He looked at Tess for the first time. "Sergeant Calloway won't be in contact with you about this case from here on. Anything you have, you bring to me. In daylight. Through the front door."

"Yes, sir," said Tess, in a voice with nothing in it at all.

Brannock pulled the door shut. The Explorer backed out of its space, crossed the empty lot, signaled — at nobody — and turned onto Main Street toward Route 1, wipers going, and was gone.

Tess stood in the rain with the evidence bag in her hand and the laptop under her coat.

"You went to the airport," she said.

"Yes."

"At eight in the morning. Alone. To a witness who'd lied to us." She wasn't looking at him. She was looking at the empty space where the Explorer had been. "And then you called me at half past twelve and let me carry it to him. And let him ask you where it came from with me standing here."

"I didn't think he'd—"

"He thinks I sent you." She turned. Her face was wet, and her gray eyes were very steady. "He's thought I was running you since the walkthrough on Sunday. He was nearly done thinking it. Now he isn't." She held the evidence bag up between them. "Do you know what I'd have done, if you'd called me at seven instead of twelve-thirty? I'd have driven. I'd have walked in with a badge. I'd have had Marchand sign for it in front of a witness, with a time on it. It'd be worth something."

"He'd never have handed it to a badge."

"Then we'd have gotten a warrant."

"And he'd have been in Boston, and that card would have been in a trash can at Logan."

"Maybe." She didn't give him an inch. "Why didn't you call me?"

He thought about lying to her, and knew he wouldn't manage it. He never had, with anyone he cared about.

"Because you'd have told me not to go," he said.

"Yes," said Tess. "I would have." The rain ran off the edge of her hood. "That's what calling me is for, Archie."

She looked at him a moment longer. Then she turned and walked away across the lot to the back door of the town hall, with the bag and the laptop held against her, not hurrying, through the rain. She didn't look back. The door shut behind her, and he stood in the empty lot in a wet coat that smelled like a sheep, with nothing left in his pockets but a napkin.


---

# Chapter 28

## Paint Day

At four o'clock on Wednesday afternoon Archie climbed the opera house stairs with a square of cardboard under his arm that said, in black marker, SHOW CANCELLED — WITH APOLOGIES — A.F., and found the top of the stairs blocked by Bev Thibodeau.

She was in a barn coat and rubber boots, with a clipboard held against her chest, and behind her the hall was full of noise.

"What's that," she said.

He showed her. She read it. She took it out of his hands, folded it in half and then in half again, and put it in the pocket of her barn coat.

"No," said Bev.

"Bev—"

"Sit down. No — don't sit down. Go see Hollis. She's got you on a list."

He went past her into the hall and stopped.

There were fifty-one people in the Merrow Opera House. Ozzie Lund and Dale Pinkham were up on two orchard ladders at the harbor-side windows, unhooking Marchand Media's blackout drapes. The liars' table — Elwin, Dickie and Pickle — had set a plank across a pair of sawhorses by the radiators and were stirring five-gallon buckets of paint with the slow seriousness of men baiting traps. Mattie Pinkham was at the top of the scaffolding along the north wall in a Red Sox cap, shouting down about rollers. Deb Coombs from the library was running blue tape along the window trim with the face of a woman asked to alphabetize the ocean. There were drop cloths everywhere, a radio playing country music, and a folding table by the door crowded with casseroles under foil and a coffee urn Archie was nearly sure belonged to the Galley. Somebody had brought a crockpot of baked beans. Somebody had brought three.

In the middle of it all, on the stage, in front of the painted harbor drape, with a clipboard in one hand and a pencil behind her ear and her ring of keys at her hip, stood Hollis Tibbetts, pointing.

"There you are," she called down. "You're late. You're on the scaffold."

"Hollis, I came up here to cancel—"

"I know what you came up here for. Bev rang me from the landing." She was in her painting clothes: a man's flannel shirt with the sleeves rolled, old corduroys, the gray fingerless gloves. "Listen to me, Archie. I know about that girl, and I know about the lieutenant, and you can't fix either one from a stepladder. But you bought this building with a deed that says it's a theater forever, and it's a theater on Friday night whether you're in a mood or not." She pointed her pencil at the north wall. "Window trim. Cream, not white. You like heights."

"I don't like—"

"You hung upside down from my flies for a month. Up."

He went up.

He was on the second tier of the scaffold with a brush and a can of trim paint the color of heavy cream when Ozzie freed the last hook on the first harbor window and let the drape fall.

It came down in one long black slither and piled on the drop cloth, and the light came in.

The rain had blown through an hour before. Behind it, low over the far shore of the bay, the sun had come out under the back edge of the cloud, and it poured in through the tall arched window at a slant, gold and enormous, across the drop cloths and up the far wall to the pressed-tin ceiling, which lit up square by square. Fifty-one people stopped what they were doing. Ozzie stood at the top of his ladder with the hook in his hand and looked at what he'd done.

"Well," he said, after a while. "Next one."

They took down the next one, and the next, until the hall was so full of light that Archie could see the dust going up through the gold air. Nobody in the room had said one word about Saturday night, and he understood that nobody was going to.

He lasted about twenty minutes on the scaffold before he had to give somebody something.

He climbed down, went out to the stairwell, and called Bev, who was twelve feet away on the landing and answered anyway.

"Bev. How many people are in there?"

"Fifty-one. Pru's coming with the inn's ladders. Fifty-three."

"Lobster rolls," said Archie. "For sixty. People will keep coming."

Bev lowered the phone and looked at him across the landing.

"Galley's closed," she said. "Closes at two."

"I'll buy every lobster in the co-op tank. I'll pay you double. I'll wash the pans. Badly, but I'll wash them."

Bev considered him for a long moment. Then she put the phone back to her ear, though he was standing right in front of her.

"Dickie," she said into it. "Get down off that sawhorse. We're opening."

---

At a quarter past six, with the work lamps coming up on their stands and the harbor going dark in the windows, Bev and Dickie Thibodeau came up the stairs with a hotel pan between them, and went down and came back with another, and another, and the opera house ate.

There were sixty-two lobster rolls in split-top buns toasted in butter on the Galley's flattop, the meat in big pink-and-white pieces with nothing on it but a little mayonnaise. People sat along the edge of the stage with their legs hanging over and their paint-spattered hands full. Dale Pinkham ate his with his eyes closed. Mattie ate three and argued that the third didn't count because it had been small. Ozzie carried one to Vivienne on a paper plate with both hands, as if it were a corsage, and she accepted it the same way. Lenny had heard the word *lobster* at five o'clock and gone home to eat a cheese sandwich on principle.

Over by the radiators, Elwin Coombs took his roll from Archie's own hands. He looked at it, and at Archie, and nodded once, and said nothing whatsoever.

Sixty-four days. Archie didn't mind. He had never had a nod before.

He sat on the edge of the stage between his mother and an old man he'd never met, who told him in great detail about a snowmobile he'd owned in 1979, and ate his own lobster roll, and it was without question the best thing he had eaten since August.

Shannon Mayo had come in at five with her own roller and gone straight to Hollis, who put her on the long south wall beside her. They'd worked side by side ever since, Hollis talking and Shannon not. Shannon didn't come for a lobster roll. Hollis brought her one. Shannon ate it with her back to the stage, looking at the wall she'd painted, and when Archie came past carrying an empty hotel pan, she moved her paint tray out of his way without looking up.

He took it. It was more than he'd had on Sunday.

---

Kayla Bissonnette came up the stairs at a quarter to seven with a liquor-store carton under her arm and an errand she'd clearly rather not run in front of fifty people who had known her since diapers.

"Sorry, everybody," she said. "The lieutenant wants every glove in the building."

"Every glove," said Dale Pinkham.

"Work gloves, rubber gloves, stage gloves, Chief." She looked wretched. "He said every."

So the town of Merrow gave up its gloves. Pinkham went first, peeling off the yellow rubber dish gloves he'd been rolling paint in and laying them in Kayla's carton with tremendous dignity. Ozzie dropped in his work gloves. The liars' table, after a consultation, surrendered six canvas gloves that had last hauled a trap during the Carter administration. Vivienne drew off a pair of long black kid gloves she had not been wearing to paint in, and had produced from somewhere for the occasion, and laid them in the box one at a time.

"I wore those to be sawn in half in Atlantic City in 1966," she told Kayla. "I'll want a receipt."

"Yes, ma'am."

Hollis came down from the stage with the shoebox from the light booth, the one the Players' crew had been taking black stage gloves out of since the eighties, and set it on top of the rest. Then she pulled off her own gray fingerless gloves and dropped them in after it.

"Those haven't got fingers, Kayla Bissonnette, so tell the lieutenant I'll want them back by Friday. My hands get cold." She tapped the shoebox. "And if there's one pair less in there when it comes back than there is now, it's detention. I've still got the forms."

Kayla went pink to the ears. "Yes, Mrs. Tibbetts."

"And sit up straight when you carry that."

Half the hall laughed. Kayla, who had been in Mrs. Tibbetts's senior English, carried the town's gloves down the stairs with her shoulders back.

Archie watched her go from the scaffold. He knew exactly who had sent for that carton. He'd handed over the card at two o'clock; it was not yet seven. The lieutenant was very, very good at his job. Archie found he would have liked that better if it had been somebody else's case.

---

June found him on the scaffold at a quarter past seven.

She came up the ladder with a can of trim paint hooked over her wrist as a pretext, and sat on the plank beside him with her Doc Martens hanging over the edge. They were at the stage-left end now, at the window that had been bare on Saturday night. Out past it the harbor was black, and every six seconds Merrow Light flashed green on the point.

June watched it flash twice.

"I want to do the Chair," she said. "Friday. With you."

He laid his brush across the top of the can.

"Not come out at the end and wave. Not the part you'd show a volunteer. I want you to teach me the method. All of it." She was looking at the window, not at him. "You told Teddy Marchand how the Glass Box works. In an airport. On a napkin."

"I know."

"So." It was the voice she used to close an argument. "Me too."

Archie looked down at the hall. Hollis was making Dale Pinkham move a ladder six inches to the left. His mother sat on the stage edge beside Ozzie with her shoes off, laughing at something he'd said in about four words. Fifty-odd people were painting his grandfather's theater for nothing, with lobster on their breath.

"It's Gus's," he said. "The Chair. It isn't mine. He showed me the summer I was nine, under this stage, and before he'd show me one thing he made me swear. Lenny builds it. Your grandmother comes up through it. Nobody else alive. There's an oath."

"I know there's an oath."

"You'd have to swear it and keep it. For the rest of your life. Not Mattie, not your mother, not some boy at Yale, not the Supreme Court—"

"Dad." She turned and looked at him then, with her mother's level eyes. "I know what a secret is. I kept one from you for two months." She put out her right hand between them on the plank, palm down, as if she were about to lay it on a Bible. "Okay. What do I swear on?"

He reached over and took the jack of clubs out of the air beside her left ear.

He hadn't planned it; his hands did it on their own. June jumped. Then she saw which card it was — the one that had landed face up by his shoe in the library the day he caught her — and her face went red to the pencil in her bun. And then, before she could stop it, it lit up all at once, the whole of it, the way his own face must have lit on a kitchen floor in 1982 with a silver dollar coming out of his ear.

She shut it off almost at once. But he'd seen it.

He laid the card face down on the plank between them. She put her hand flat on it, and he put his on top of hers, and up on the scaffold in the smell of paint and lobster and wet wool, Archie Fairweather said the words his grandfather had said to him on the boards beneath their feet when he was nine years old, and his daughter said them back, every one, without a mistake.

"Yes," he said, when she'd finished. "Yes. Tonight. Lenny's got the practice trap in the workshop."

"Tonight," June agreed. She put the jack of clubs in her cardigan pocket and climbed down without another word, and he watched her cross the floor to Mattie and say something that made Mattie shriek and get paint on Deb Coombs.

---

By eight o'clock the hall was half painted. The north wall was done and the south wall nearly; the tin ceiling would be another day and another argument. People were finding their coats and disputing whose crockpot was whose. In the light of the work lamps the painted drape at the back of the stage glowed — gulls and schooners and a sky far too blue for Maine — and the tall bare windows were full of the night harbor.

Hollis stood at the center of the stage with her clipboard under her arm and her bare hands pushed into the pockets of her corduroys. She looked out at the half-painted hall, and at Archie coming down off the scaffold with cream paint to his elbows, and gave her short bark of a laugh.

"Your grandfather would have loved this," she said. "Every bucket of it."


---

# Chapter 29

## The Method

The carriage house had been built for horses, then for the hearse, and since September it had belonged to Lenny, who had filled it with a drill press, a band saw, two welding tanks chained to the wall, and a workbench twelve feet long with every tool hung inside its own painted outline, so you could tell at a glance which one had been borrowed and by whom. The hearse stood at the near end. At the far end, under two hanging work lamps, was the platform.

Lenny had built it the first week, out of plywood and four-by-fours, exactly as high as the stage of the Merrow Opera House. In the middle he had cut a trap the same size as Gus's — two feet by four, two leaves hinged at the center — and underneath it, where no audience would ever see, he had put three wooden steps, a counterweight on a rope, and a sandbag to sit on.

At ten o'clock on Wednesday night it was cold enough in there to see your breath, in spite of the space heater ticking by the bench. Archie still had paint under his fingernails from the opera house and, he had been told twice, a stripe of it in his hair. He had set a plain ladder-back chair from the kitchen in the middle of the platform, folded a white sheet over its back, and swung one of the lamps down so it fell on the chair in a hard, bright circle.

It looked, he thought, rather wonderful.

June sat on an upturned bucket in front of the platform with a notebook on her knee. She had already ruled the page into columns. She was wearing her oldest cardigan and a pair of Lenny's work gloves, because Lenny had told her nobody touched his trap with bare hands, and she had taken him at his word.

"Ladies and gentlemen," said Archie.

"Dad."

"I'm setting the scene."

"I've seen the scene. There's a video of Gus doing it in 1979, shot from the balcony by somebody's uncle. You can hear a man unwrapping a sandwich." She clicked her pen. "You said all of it. That's what I'm writing down. All of it."

She had asked that evening, on the scaffold, with the jack of clubs under her hand, and he'd said yes. He found now, with the sheet in his hands, that meaning it and doing it were two separate tricks, and he had only ever practiced one of them.

"All right," he said. "The count."

"The count is everything. One, two, three — that's what they hear. But Gus's count took eleven seconds. You say *one*, and then you tell them something. He always started the same way. He said every trick was a promise." June wrote that down. "Then you tell them about the chair, or the person who used to sit in it, or what it's like to wait for somebody. You make them lean in. Then *two*—"

"What holds the sheet up?"

He stopped.

"In the video," June said. "When he puts it on, it doesn't fall flat. It's wide at the top. Like there's already somebody under it." She looked at him over the notebook. "You skipped that."

At the workbench, Lenny, who had been filing something small, stopped filing.

"I skipped that," Archie admitted.

"Why?"

He had a dozen reasons. He'd been giving them to reporters and producers and other magicians for thirty years, and they were all charming, and they all meant *no*.

"Habit," he said.

He turned the sheet inside out and showed her.
