Sewn into the muslin, about two feet below the top, was a channel of doubled cloth, and inside the channel was a long oval of spring steel no thicker than a coat hanger. He dropped the sheet over the chair. The steel caught on the top of the ladder-back and held the cloth out at the width of a man's shoulders, and the rest fell away in folds to the floor, pooling at the back. Under the lamp it looked exactly like someone sitting very still under a sheet.

"That's the secret," he said. "From the second it goes on, the chair looks full. They think they're watching an empty chair. They've been watching a shape, and when somebody comes up into it, the shape doesn't change."

June stared at it.

"It's a coat hanger," she said.

"It's a very good coat hanger."

"Spring steel," said Lenny.

"The chair stands on the front leaf of the trap. The back leaf drops. Whoever's under there comes up the steps behind the chair, inside the sheet — that's why it's long at the back — and sits. Eight seconds if you're quick. Lenny holds the counterweight so the leaf doesn't bang, and there's felt on every edge. On three you pull up and back, and the hoop goes with it, and you throw it behind you and never look at it again. Nobody looks at a sheet once there's a person."

"And the person is Grandma."

"The person is Grandma." That had been settled at the opera house over lobster rolls, with Vivienne holding court from a stepladder. "She comes up. I stand by the chair and put the sheet on and hold the corner so it doesn't move. You do the patter. You do the count. You pull."

"Me," said June.

"You."

She wrote it down, pressing hard.

"She's seventy-three," said Lenny. "She comes up slow."

"Then I count slow," said June.

Lenny looked at her for a moment over his reading glasses. Then he went back to filing.

---

"Tonight," said Archie, "your grandmother will be played by me."

It was dark under the platform and smelled of sawdust and cold concrete, and he had to fold himself onto the steps like a deck chair. Lenny lowered himself onto the sandbag, put one hand on the rope, and took out his stopwatch.

"Don't step on the front leaf," he told June, through the boards. "You'll go through."

"I wasn't going to."

"Everybody isn't going to. Then they do."

Overhead, the sheet went over the chair. June took a breath.

"This is a chair," she said. "It's — an ordinary chair. One."

Lenny pulled. The back leaf swung down without a whisper, and Archie went up into the muffled white of the sheet and got one knee onto the platform, and had his other foot still on the top step when June said, briskly, "Two, three," and the sheet went away and the lamp hit him in the face.

He was on his hands and knees behind the chair with one leg in a hole in the floor. He looked up at his daughter. She looked down at her father.

"Ankle," said Lenny, from below. "That's his ankle."

June put both hands over her face.

"Again," said Archie, backing down the steps. "You counted it like a fire drill."

"I *know.*"

The second time she counted slowly. She told the chair about its long life in a kitchen, and she was good — dry and odd and nothing like him, which was what made it good. He came up and sat and arranged himself under the hoop with time to spare, and still, from inside the sheet, he could tell.

"You're looking at me," he said.

A pause. "How do you know?"

"Your voice is pointed at the chair. If your voice is pointed at the chair, so is everybody's attention. Where you look, they look." He sat in the white dark and listened to her not answering. "You have to look at them, Junebug. The whole count. You can't check."

She pulled the sheet, and he was sitting there. It worked, more or less. Neither of them said so.

---

Afterward she sat on the edge of the platform with her boots hanging over, and he sat beside her. Underneath them Lenny was doing something to the counterweight that involved a wrench and a good deal of breathing.

"The last time he did it," June said. "Did you know it was the last time?"

"No."

"What do you remember?"

On Monday, on Dale Pinkham's boat by the bell buoy, he had said it in one sentence: ten years old, second row. It came out differently now.

"I had a seat on the aisle," he said. "Second row, on the left. Your grandmother wasn't next to me, because she was under the stage. I had a bag of peppermints somebody's husband had bought me at the Galley, and I ate every one before the intermission. The radiators were going. The whole hall smelled like wet coats." He looked at the chair. "He did the Chair last. He always did. And during the count, the whole eleven seconds, he looked straight at me. Straight down into the second row. I thought it was because I was his grandson. I thought it was a secret between us."

"It was the misdirection."

"It was the misdirection," he agreed. "It was probably also because I was his grandson. Both things can be true." He hadn't known he thought that until he heard himself say it. "Then the sheet came off, and it was your grandmother in the chair, in her old sequins, and the whole place stood up. They hadn't seen her on that stage since she was twenty-four. He kissed her hand. The curtain came down. And I went down to the green room to wait for him, because he'd said he'd walk me home, and I fell asleep on a pile of coats."

June looked at her hands.

"Do you think he drowned?" she said.

The heater ticked. Under the platform, the wrench had stopped.

"I think he did the trick," Archie said.

"Then where did he go?"

"I don't know." He held his hands out and looked at the paint under the nails. "I really don't know, June."

She sat beside him for a while. Then she got up, pulled the work gloves back on, and picked the sheet up off the boards.

"Lenny," she said. "Will you go under?"

"No," said Lenny.

"Please."

A long silence from beneath the platform. Then the sandbag creaked.

"I'm going to watch from the house," Archie said, and went and sat on the bucket.

---

She began with Gus's line, the one about promises, and then she left it behind. She told the bucket, the hearse and her father that this chair had come out of a kitchen in this house, and that a great many bad breakfasts had been eaten on it, most of them cooked by her father. She put the sheet on in one clean movement, and the hoop caught, and the shape sat there.

"One," June said.

She looked at him.

He could not afterward have repeated a word of what she said next, because she was looking at him the whole time — not at the sheet, not at the trap, not at the place where every instinct she had must have been telling her to check — straight down at the bucket in the cold, for all eleven seconds. Behind her the back leaf dropped without a sound. The sheet never moved.

"Three," said June, and pulled it up and back and threw it behind her without a glance.

Lenny Szabo sat in the chair in all three of his sweaters, with his reading glasses pushed up on his forehead and his arms folded, wearing the expression of a man waiting for a bus he did not expect to enjoy.

It was perfect.

Archie stood up. He found he couldn't say anything at all, and that was rare enough that June's face changed and started to ask. He held up one hand.

"I'll be right back," he said, and went out.

---

The dooryard was dark and very cold. The fog hadn't come in yet; the sky over the harbor was full of stars, and out on the point Merrow Light swept its green flash across the water and was gone, and swept it again. He stood on the frozen gravel in his shirtsleeves with his breath smoking and let the cold have his face for a minute.

Through the carriage-house window he could see the lamp, and the platform, and his daughter shaking out the sheet to do it again. Lenny's bald head rose out of the trap. He said something. June laughed — short and surprised, the kind she usually caught before it got out — and didn't catch it.

His phone buzzed.

"Archie." Dale Pinkham, low, the voice of a man calling from his own kitchen with his wife asleep upstairs. "Sorry about the hour. Thought you'd want it from me. AG's office is filing on the Crane girl tomorrow. Murder."

Archie closed his eyes.

"And there's a lawyer from Portland been calling the station all evening. Okafor. Says she's the girl's attorney and she wants you at the jail in Rockland at ten tomorrow morning." A pause. "Says you'll know why."

In the window, June put the sheet over the chair, and the hoop caught, and the shape sat under the lamp, waiting for somebody to come up into it.

"I'll be there," Archie said.


---

# Chapter 30

## Visiting Hours

The Knox County Jail was a low brick building on the edge of Rockland with a flagpole, a lot full of pickup trucks, and a lobby that smelled of floor wax and burnt coffee. At five minutes to ten on Thursday morning Archie stood in it with his pockets on a plastic tray — keys, wallet, a deck of cards, two half-dollars, a roll of peppermints and a lock pick shaped like a hairpin — while a corrections officer named Doucette looked at the lock pick and then at him.

"It's a hairpin," said Archie.

"It's not a hairpin."

"It's a very ambitious hairpin."

Doucette put it in an envelope and wrote on the envelope.

"He's with me," said Margaret Okafor, behind him. "God help me."

She had driven up from Portland at six. She was in her fifties, compact, in a black wool coat and flat shoes, with close-cropped hair gone silver at the temples and a leather briefcase older than her client. The wedding cake, he had learned afterward, had been lemon; the hotel kitchen staff had eaten it, and she'd sent him a thank-you note on letterhead.

She walked him down a corridor of painted cinderblock to a door with a narrow window in it, and stopped.

"Ground rules," she said. "You are a witness for the State. You do not discuss your statement. You do not discuss the scarf. If she starts to, you stop her, and if you don't, I will, and I'm better at it." She looked at him over a pair of reading glasses she hadn't put on. "You're here because the State has a theory, my client has a confession that isn't one, and the most famous debunker in the world is paying my invoices. I want a third party, Mr. Fairweather. Somebody with a better reason than an inheritance. Find me one."

"You think there is one."

"I think the Attorney General filed a murder charge this morning on a scarf and a bad feeling." She opened the door. "Twenty minutes."

---

The attorney visiting room was the green of a hospital corridor, with a steel table bolted to the floor and three plastic chairs. Ivy Crane was already sitting in one of them, in an orange jail sweatshirt, with her hands folded on the table. Without the silk scarf her throat looked bare and young. Her hair was still scraped back. Somebody had taken her hair elastic and given her a rubber band instead.

She looked at him, and then at her lawyer.

"I said I didn't want to see him."

"And I said I decide who's useful," said Okafor, and sat down and uncapped a fountain pen.

Archie sat across from Ivy. He had driven twenty minutes down Route 1 rehearsing an opening, and when he opened his mouth it was gone.

"They charged you," he said.

"I know. I was there."

"With murder, Ivy."

"I know what they charged me with." Her voice was perfectly flat. "I told them it was my fault. It was."

"You told them that because you set out the horn."

She didn't answer.

"Every night," he said. "You lay it out. You put it in the middle of the table. And you think if you hadn't—"

"If there's no horn, there's nothing to put anything on." She said it to the table. "If I'd quit at twenty. If I'd quit at twelve. That's not a feeling, Mr. Fairweather. That's arithmetic."

"It's arithmetic with a hole in it."

"Then find the hole." She looked up. "Isn't that why she brought you?"

He wanted to argue. He had the argument ready; he could feel it arranging itself, patter and all. He looked at her folded hands, which were the hands of a woman who had spent seventeen years doing difficult things in the dark, and put the argument away.

"I taught my daughter a trick last night," he said.

Okafor's pen paused. Ivy said nothing.

"Gus's finale. The Empty Chair. She asked me yesterday to teach her all of it, so I did. In the carriage house, with a coat hanger and a sheet and my engineer sitting under a trapdoor on a sandbag." He shrugged. "She was terrible the first time. She nearly broke my ankle."

"All of it?" said Ivy.

"All of it."

"Including the part you didn't want to show her."

He looked at her. "Including that."

"How old?"

"Sixteen."

Ivy was quiet for a moment. Then she unfolded her hands and laid them flat on the steel.

"I was twelve," she said. "A motel in Nashua. She turned off the lights and held a hairbrush out in the dark and said, *Take my hand, baby.* Left hand under, right hand over. When she squeezes twice, you let go one finger at a time. You keep your other hand on Warren Gilley's, or whoever's, so he's sure. He's holding a medium's daughter. That's all he was ever promised." She said it like a recipe. "Then you've got a hand free, and so has she. The rod's under the cloth at my place. The glove on the end is wet. I do the bell and the touches. She does the horn."

"Ivy," said Okafor, not looking up.

"They have it on video. The fan's video. It isn't a secret anymore." Ivy didn't take her eyes off Archie. "The remote's up my left sleeve. When she lifts the horn I kill the cameras. Teddy calls it the static. Teddy has always known exactly what it is. When it's over and the lights come up, before anybody else can touch it, you wipe the horn. There's lipstick on the small end. Every séance since I was twelve. I've never once thought about it. You don't think about it. You just do it."

"It's a reset," said Archie.

"It's a reset." For the first time something moved in her face. "I wasn't hiding anything on Saturday night except her."

Okafor wrote that down, word for word, and underlined it.

Archie sat back in the plastic chair. In his head, for no reason he could have explained to her, Kev Duchesne was standing in the dark under the opera house stage with an EMF meter in one hand, telling him about the Trumpet Hiatus with the reverence of a man describing a moon landing. *Two thousand three to two thousand six, Mr. Fairweather. Sister Lori wouldn't use the trumpet in private sittings. Not once. The forums think Brother Amos told her to.*

"Your mother stopped using the horn," he said. "In the private sittings. For three years."

Ivy's eyes narrowed very slightly. "Kev Duchesne."

"Kev Duchesne."

"Brother Amos told her to put it away. That's the story."

"Brother Amos was your mother in a deeper voice."

"Brother Amos was a bathrobe and an accent she got off a record of Welsh hymns," said Ivy. "Yes."

"Then here's what I know about performers." He leaned forward. "You don't drop the best routine you've got for three years because a fan forum says so. You drop a routine for two reasons. It's stopped working, or it hurt somebody. That horn never stopped working. It's the best trumpet I ever sat with, and I've sat with forty." He kept his voice low. "What happened in 2003?"

The room was very quiet. Somewhere down the corridor a door buzzed and banged.

"I was seven," Ivy said.

Okafor's pen stopped.

"I don't remember much. I remember it was raining. We were parked outside a church. Here — in Rockland. She'd gone inside, which she never did unless she was working a church, and I was in the back seat with a coloring book. She came out with a newspaper under her coat and got into the driver's seat and didn't start the car." Ivy looked at a point on the green wall above his shoulder. "She just sat there with the wipers off and cried until the windows were all rain and you couldn't see out. I'd never seen her cry when it wasn't for a sitter."

"Did she say why?"

"She said a man had died." A small, terrible shrug. "I was seven. I wanted to know if we could still get McDonald's."

Archie didn't say anything.

"Years later," said Ivy, "in a hotel bar in Phoenix, after three of something, she told me she never did the horn on a private sitting again after the man in Maine. That's how she said it. *The man in Maine.* As if that was his name." She looked back at Archie. "That's all I know. I don't know who he was."

"Would she have written him down?"

"She wrote everybody down."

"Ivy," said Okafor again, differently.

"She kept appointment books," Ivy said. "Thirty years of them. Every sitter since the church halls — names, towns, what they wanted, what she charged them, what worked. In her own shorthand. Nobody can read it but her." She stopped. "Nobody could."

"Where are they?"

She didn't answer right away. He watched her decide.

"In a hatbox," she said. "In Pru Haskell's office safe at the inn. I moved them on Sunday. I told Pru they were Mother's private things." Her chin came up a fraction. "They are."

"Why move them?"

"Because those books are thirty years of people crying in rented rooms," said Ivy, "and I wasn't going to have them read out on a folding table by a lieutenant who hates his reading glasses."

Okafor had stopped writing. She looked at her client for a long moment, and then at Archie, and something in her face rearranged itself into the expression of a woman who had just been handed a third party with a ribbon on it.

"The estate isn't probated," she said, half to herself. "The papers belong to the estate. My client is the sole heir. And Mrs. Haskell is an innkeeper, not a probate judge." She tore a fresh sheet from her legal pad and began to write in a fast, upright hand. "Release of personal property. To Archibald Fairweather, one hatbox, contents, from the custody of Prudence Haskell. Ivy, read it before you sign it. Always read it."

Ivy read it. She signed it. Okafor signed under her, and dated it, and wrote her bar number beside her name, and tore the sheet off the pad and folded it once and held it out across the table.

Archie took it.

"Read them," Ivy said. She was looking at the paper in his hand, not at him. "Then you'll know what she was. All of it."

"I already liked her," Archie said. "That's the problem."

Ivy looked at him then. She didn't say anything. There was a knock on the door window — Doucette, holding up five fingers — and she stood, and put her hands behind her back because that was what you did here, and went out without looking back.

---

In the corridor, Okafor shook his hand and kept hold of it a second too long.

"Third party, Mr. Fairweather," she said. "Bring me one. And don't make me read about it in the *Press Herald* first."

She went off toward the interview rooms. Archie walked the other way, toward the lobby and his envelope with the hairpin in it, with Ivy Crane's signature folded in his hand.

Halfway down the corridor a door opened, and Tess Calloway came out of it with a trooper Archie didn't know and a thick file under her arm.

She was in the charcoal coat. Her braid was wet; it must have started raining again. She saw him, and her stride didn't change, and her eyes went once — quick, gray, exact — to the paper in his hand, where the fold had come open enough to show two signatures, and then back to his face.

He didn't say *Sergeant*. She didn't say anything at all.

They passed each other in the green corridor, close enough to touch, and kept walking, and behind him the door at the far end buzzed and opened and closed.


---

# Chapter 31

## Dress Rehearsal

Pru Haskell was in Bangor for her daughter's birthday, and she was the only person alive with the combination to the safe in the Spindrift Inn's office. She'd be back at nine. She had told him so twice on the phone that morning, kindly, and a third time at noon, less kindly.

By six o'clock on Thursday evening Archie had looked at his grandfather's watch eleven times.

The opera house didn't care. It was full of light and noise and fresh paint, the arched windows gone black over the harbor, the radiators knocking along under them. Ozzie and three of the Pinkham cousins had set out the folding chairs in rows that afternoon. The painted harbor drape was flown out, and the stage was cluttered with everything the show needed and some things it didn't: Lenny's cabinet, a rack of costumes, a stepladder, a bentwood chair with a white sheet folded over its back, and Chief Dale Pinkham, in the center aisle, holding a flashlight and a list.

"Archie," the chief called. "When folks come in. Do I tear the tickets or just look at 'em?"

"There aren't any tickets, Dale. It's free."

The chief considered this. "Then what do I tear?"

"Nothing. You smile and you point at chairs."

"Ayuh." Dale wrote something on the list. "Smile. Point."

They were running Act One in order, which meant they were on the Sparrows, which meant Lenny had flatly refused to release the doves. "They don't rehearse," he'd said. "They know it." So Archie stood alone in the gold wash at center stage producing four imaginary birds from an empty silk, and on the fourth he lifted his empty hands and said, "And then the stage goes blue and—"

The stage went blue. It went blue about two seconds after he'd stopped needing it to, and he stood there in it with his arms up, looking foolish.

"You said *blue* in the middle of a sentence," Hollis's voice came down from the booth at the back of the balcony, carrying the length of the hall without the slightest effort. "Cue lines go at the *end* of the sentence, Archie. Like a period. I'm not a mind reader. I taught English."

"You taught *drama*."

"I taught it to people who could finish a sentence."

At the stage-right end of the second row, Shannon Mayo was folding programs on her knee from a stack the height of a lobster crate. She didn't laugh. Beside her, Liam did, loudly, and was elbowed.

---

The hinge went at twenty past six.

Lenny's cabinet was eight feet tall, black lacquer, with a door on the front that opened on a hinge he'd machined himself, and that hinge had chosen tonight to scream. Lenny fetched an oil can, oiled it, and swung the door. It screamed.

"It's oiled," said Lenny.

"I can hear it's oiled."

Lenny swung it again, listening. Then he got down on his knees with a flashlight. "Different hinge," he said, and disappeared behind the cabinet.

In the hold, Archie sat down on the lip of the stage with his legs over the edge, and Liam Mayo came straight out of the second row.

"Do the coin," said Liam. "The walking one. Mattie says you do a walking one."

Archie took a half-dollar out of his waistcoat pocket and set it on the back of his first finger and let it walk — over the knuckle, under, over the next, a slow silver tumble from forefinger to little finger and back again by the thumb, without his looking at it. Liam stood with his chin on the edge of the boards and watched it go.

"You're not even looking," Liam said.

"That's the whole trick. If I look, you look."

Liam dug a nickel out of his jeans and tried. It went over his first knuckle, and sideways, and dropped through a gap between two stage boards with a small, final *tink*.

From somewhere behind the cabinet, Lenny said, "That's gone."

Liam looked at the gap. Then he looked at his mother. Shannon looked at Archie for the first time that evening, a long, level look with nothing in it he could name.

"Liam," she said. "Leave Mr. Fairweather alone."

But she didn't come and get him, and Archie gave Liam the half-dollar, and Liam spent the rest of the hold dropping it on his own knee.

---

After that things went wrong in the ordinary way, which Archie had always loved about rehearsals. His mother, who was supposed to be waiting in the wing, walked out to the edge of the stage and told the empty seats, unprompted, about the night in 1966 when Gus's Silver Moon had come loose and rolled into the lap of the Lieutenant Governor of Maine, and what the Lieutenant Governor had said.

"That's not in the show, Mother."

"It is now. They'll love it."

"There's nobody out there."

"There's Dale," said Vivienne, and in the center aisle the chief, who had been laughing, put his list over his face.

At a quarter to seven Dale came up the side steps holding a sheet of paper in the manner of a man who wished someone else had been handed it.

"Fire marshal's fella was in at noon," he said, to the stage generally and the booth in particular. "That door at the end of the balcony. The fire escape. Says it's got to be unlocked during the show. Code. Second way out of the balcony."

"That door," Hollis called down, "lets in the entire North Atlantic."

"I just read 'em, Hollis."

"I know you do, Dale." There was a great sigh from the booth, a clatter of keys, and Archie watched her come out along the balcony in her cardigan to the iron door at the far end, unlock it, push it open on a wedge of cold black air, and jam it a few inches ajar with a folded program. "There. Unlocked. If I freeze to the board, you can chip me off it."

She went back into the booth. A moment later her voice came down again, pitched for teaching. "Now, Mattie. Look here. The cue book lives in this drawer. *This* drawer, not that one, that one's full of my cough drops. Every cue's in pencil. If I get the flu, you open the drawer, you read the pencil, you push the slider when it says GO. That's all theater is."

"What if you get the flu in the *middle*?" said Mattie Pinkham, loudly and with great interest.

"Then you'll be very popular," said Hollis.

---

They did the Chair at seven, out of order, because Vivienne said that if she had to sit in a crawlspace in sequins she'd do it while her knees still worked.

Lenny took her down through the stage-right stairs. Archie heard them go along under the boards — his mother's voice saying *Leonard, if there is a mouse down here, I will know* and Lenny not answering — and then the sound of Lenny settling at the trap. Out in the house Hollis brought everything down but a single hard spot on the bentwood chair, standing on its leaf of the trap at center stage.

June came out into the light in her school cardigan, because the costume wasn't finished, and Archie came out beside her and stood by the chair and put the sheet on it. The hoop caught. The shape sat there.

He didn't look at it. He looked at the hall — Dale in the aisle with his flashlight off, Shannon with a program half-folded on her knee, Liam with the half-dollar in his fist — and listened to his daughter.

She started with Gus's line and then left it, the way she had in the carriage house, and told the house a little about the chair, and about waiting. She looked at them the whole time. He could feel her not looking at the sheet; it was a kind of heat. The count went long and slow and nobody in the hall noticed it was long. Under his hand on the corner of the sheet, nothing moved.

"Three," said June, and pulled.

Vivienne Fairweather sat in the bentwood chair in the black sequined gown she had last worn on this stage in 1988, with her hands folded in her lap and her chin up, and looked out at the house with the expression of a woman who had been sitting there all evening and couldn't think what the fuss was about.

Liam stood straight up on his chair and shouted, "*HOW.*" In the aisle, Dale Pinkham sat down on the floor. Shannon's program slid off her knee. And from the booth, high at the back, came Hollis's short, unmistakable bark of a laugh, and then the spot went soft and wide and warm, the way it was supposed to, exactly on time.

June didn't look at her father. She looked at the floor, with her mouth pressed tight, and he could see every bit of what it cost her not to grin.

---

At the break he went up to the booth with a question about that last cue. He wanted the spot to hold hard a half-second longer after the sheet came off, so the room could see Vivienne before it saw anything else.

The booth sat at the back of the balcony up three steep wooden steps. He was on the second step when he heard Rosie.

"Mumma says Daddy said it's warm," Rosie said, to nobody in particular. "Where he is. In the water. He said it's warm now."

Hollis's voice came back very low, so that he barely heard it.

"Oh, sweetheart. The water's never warm here." A rustle; somebody being shifted on a lap. "That's why your daddy had those big orange gloves. Now. Which one's the blue button? Show me."

Archie stood on the step for a moment. He thought of Shannon on the wharf with her arms wrapped around herself in a thin shirt. Then he went on up.

Hollis was in her chair at the dimmer board with Rosie Mayo on her knee and Mattie at her elbow with the cue book open. Rosie had both hands on a slider. Through the gap in the balcony door at the far end came a long thin draft that smelled of the harbor.

"Cue," said Hollis, without turning around. "Which one, and what's wrong with it."

"The Chair. After the sheet. Can you hold the spot a half-second longer before you warm it?"

"I can hold it till Christmas." She took a pencil from behind her ear and wrote in the cue book over Mattie's shoulder. "Half a second. There. Anything else, or are you going to stand there breathing on my gels?"

"That's all."

"Then go and tell your mother she was magnificent. She's waiting for somebody to."

He went down and told her.

---

At twenty minutes to nine he was sitting in the hearse in the dark lot behind the Spindrift Inn with the engine off and the release folded on the dashboard, watching the back door, and at four minutes past nine a pair of headlights swung in off Water Street, and Pru Haskell's little blue Subaru pulled into the space by the kitchen and stopped.


---

# Chapter 32

## The Hatbox

The hatbox had come from a milliner on Newbury Street in Boston, a long time ago. It was round and deep, covered in green-striped paper gone soft at the edges, with a cord handle and a lid that fit too tightly, and at half past nine on Thursday night it sat in the middle of the kitchen table at Fairweather House where the monkshood had been, with Archie in front of it and nobody else awake.

His mother had gone up at nine with a hot-water bottle and the satisfied silence of a woman who had brought the house down in sequins. Lenny's light was out in the carriage house. The fog had come in after supper, and every so often, from out past the point, the bell buoy said something low and irregular about the swell.

Archie took the lid off.

Inside, standing on end like files, were small notebooks: thirty of them, the cheap drugstore kind with cardboard covers, in faded reds and blues and blacks, each with a rubber band around it. Most of the rubber bands had died and stuck. He picked one at random and peeled it open.

The pages were full of tiny, cramped, slanting marks in blue ballpoint. Not words. Clusters of consonants, numbers, dashes, small circles, a little wavy line, a sort of T with a bar through it, check marks. Every page, top to bottom, both sides.

He opened two more, and sat back.

Thirty years of a woman's working life, in a language nobody could read but her.

He was still sitting there at ten when he heard June on the back stairs. She came into the kitchen in her pajama pants and his old Harvard Square sweatshirt, with her hair down, and stopped.

"I couldn't sleep," she said. "The count keeps going in my head." She looked at the hatbox. "Is that it?"

He thought about telling her to go to bed. She had school in the morning and a show at night.

He pulled out a chair for her instead.

---

"It's code," June said, after a minute with one of the books. "Isn't it."

"It's shorthand. Her own." Archie had the third book open in front of him, and he was looking at a cluster of letters near the top of a page — *Lwstn* — and something at the back of his mind had gone very quiet and attentive. "Lewiston," he said slowly. "That's Lewiston. No vowels."

He turned a page. A small circle beside a number. A wavy line. The T with the bar.

And then he knew where he'd seen it, and it was so obvious that he laughed out loud.

"It's Gus's," he said.

"What?"

"Chapter nine of *The Dead Don't Whisper*. 'The Billet and the Blue Book.' The old mediums used to pass notes on their sitters from town to town — who'd died, how, what the widow wanted to hear — in a shorthand the Spiritualist camps had been using since the eighteen-nineties. Gus printed the whole table so people would recognize it if they ever saw it in a medium's notebook. Consonants only. Numbers for relations. A circle means passed. The wavy line is water. The T is the trumpet. A check means it landed." He could see the page in his head, the fold-out table on the cheap paper, his own nine-year-old finger moving down the columns on the turret floor. "She told me herself, by the fire at the inn. Every medium of her generation learned the trade out of his book."

"You memorized a table out of a book when you were *nine*?"

"I memorized everything in that book when I was nine. I thought there'd be a test." He pulled the nearest notebook toward him. "Find me 2003."

"Which one's 2003?"

He looked at the covers. There were no years on them. He opened one and looked at the top of the first page — *Th 3/9* — and opened another — *Su 11/2* — and understood.

"She didn't write the year," June said, reading his face. "Just the day and the date."

"Then we guess."

"We don't *guess*." She was already pulling the hatbox toward her side of the table. "If she wrote the day of the week, the date only lands on that day in certain years. March ninth is a Thursday in—" she had her phone out, scrolling a calendar "—1995. 2000. 2006. 2017. 2023. And the towns change, and the prices go up. You read me what she wrote. I'll tell you when she wrote it."

So that was how they did it. June laid the notebooks across the farm table in a long row and worked down it with her phone, a pencil and the back of an envelope. Archie read her the top of each book — first date, first town, first price — and she wrote a year on a yellow sticky note and stuck it on the cover. She wouldn't let him skip ahead, and twice she was right not to.

At eleven she stopped with her pencil in the air.

"Read that one again."

"*Mrs. Albert P., Biddeford. Four, passed. Wants to know did he suffer.*"

"Four is what?"

"Wife."

"Mrs. Albert Pomeroy's wife died."

"Apparently."

"And the man in Saco two pages back — you said his husband."

He looked. He turned back. He turned forward again, and read three more, and sat with his finger on the page.

"She moved everything over one," June said. "Every relation. One place."

"Of course she did." The grin came up out of him before he could do anything about it. "Gus published the table. Any sitter who'd read the book could have read her notes over her shoulder. So she shifted it." He looked at his daughter across a table covered in a dead woman's notebooks. "That's very good, June."

"It's arithmetic," said June, and wrote on the next sticky note.

---

After that he could read it, and he read it to her.

He'd expected a ledger, and they were a ledger, but Lorelei had written down more than the money. *Mrs. Albert P., Biddeford. Husband passed. Wants to know did he suffer. Told her no. Cried — check. Forty dollars.* *Old man, Saco. Wife passed. Wants to know is she still mad about the dog. Told him she loves the dog. Twenty-five dollars. Gave him back ten. He paid in quarters.* *Man, Bangor. Mother passed. Wants the will. Mother says look in the freezer. (It's always the freezer.)*

June laughed at that one, and then looked as though she wished she hadn't.

On a page in what she'd marked 2000, between two sittings in Augusta, there was a single short line by itself.

"*I., four today,*" Archie read. "*Pancakes. No sittings.*"

June didn't say anything. She took the notebook from him and looked at the line for a while, and gave it back.

By half past twelve the row of notebooks had sticky notes from 1995 to last year, and June had moved one stack apart from the rest. Three books. The towns in those three changed less than in any of the others.

"Rockland," she said. "Thursdays. Over and over, for two years, starting in the fall of 2001. Always the same initials first." She counted, touching each entry with the pencil. It took her a while. "Forty-one times."

Archie took the first of the three books and found the first Thursday.

*Th 10/4. Rcklnd, Pk St. — P.L., Mrrw. Lbstr. 6○ D. 15. ~ qry, 8/01. W. H. dsnt kno — NO calls hse. $350.*

He read it twice in his head before he read it aloud.

"P.L. Merrow."

June's pencil stopped.

"Lobsterman. Son passed. D. Fifteen. Water — the quarry. August of 2001." He kept his voice level. "Wife, H. Doesn't know. Don't call the house. Three hundred and fifty dollars."

Neither of them said anything for a moment. The refrigerator hummed. Out past the point the bell buoy rang, twice, and stopped.

"There's a quarry," June said. "Two miles inland. Mattie says everybody swims there in August."

"Keep going," said Archie, and turned the page.

He read her the two years. He tried to read them quickly, and couldn't. Thursday after Thursday, the same initials. The first time the T with the bar appeared, in December: *Son spoke through T. "Dad." Cried — check.* The price going up in the spring to something she called *special sittings*, two thousand five hundred dollars each. June added it all on the back of the envelope without being asked, and turned the envelope around so he could see the number, and he looked at it and turned it face down.

The lines that worked had check marks.

*"It's warm here" — check.*

*"I'm not cold anymore" — check.*

In October of 2003, near the bottom of a page, in the same tiny hand: *"Come see me on the water" — check.*

Archie stopped reading aloud.

June leaned over and read it herself, and read the mark beside it, and he watched her remember what the mark meant.

He turned the page. There were two more Thursdays, and then a gap. Then, in late November, one entry, without a day of the week.

*11/20. P.L. lost at sea. Saw it in the paper.*

And under that — not in shorthand, not in any code at all, in plain, ordinary, slanting longhand, the only ordinary words in thirty years of notebooks:

*God forgive me.*

And under that, back in the code: *No more T for privates.*

---

The kitchen was very quiet.

"Lost at sea," June said finally. "In November 2003."

"Peter Langley," said Archie.

She looked up.

"It's on the memorial. The granite at the end of the wharf." He could see it the way he saw everything he'd ever read once — the cold pink morning, Shannon in his coat, the rows of names. "*Arthur Coombs, 1978. Robert and James Haskell, 1991. Peter Langley, 2003.*"

June's face went still. Then it went white, starting at the mouth.

"Langley," she said. "H. Langley."

"June—"

"On the card. In Gus's book. The sign-out card." She was looking at nothing. "W. Calloway, eleven, eighty-eight. M. Pinkham. And then H. Langley, twelve, oh-three. I've signed under it three times." Her voice was very small. "December 2003. A month after. Somebody named H. Langley went to the library and took out Gus's book about how mediums fool people."

The wife who didn't know. Who found out.

Archie didn't answer. He was somewhere else: in a small warm house on Pudding Lane on Monday night, with a fat gray cat on his knees and fish chowder in his stomach, looking at a photograph on a piano. A boy on a dock, holding up a mackerel. *Danny,* Hollis had said, already turning away to the costume rack. *My son.* And she had handed him a velvet waistcoat and talked about a Scrooge with shingles.

He didn't say it.

June was watching his face. He had never in his life been able to hide anything from her that mattered, and he watched her see it, and he watched her refuse it.

"You're thinking about Mrs. Tibbetts," she said.

"I didn't say anything."

"You didn't have to." She put the pencil down. "She's Tibbetts. Not Langley. There must be fifty women in this town whose name starts with H. Helen. Heather. Half of them are probably Coombses. And lots of people are named Danny, Dad." She was talking fast now, in her debate voice, building it. "Lots. It's a coincidence. It's a coincidence until it isn't, and you check it. The *Packet*'s on microfilm at the library. Deb opens at nine. There'll be an obituary. There'll be a — something. We check it."

He looked at his daughter across the table: sixteen, white to the lips, holding a dead woman's notebook in both hands.

"Lots of people are named Danny," he said.

"Right." She let out a breath. "Right."

"Go to bed, Junebug. You've got school. You've got a show."

She didn't move for a moment. Then she squared the three notebooks with a sharp tap against the table, the way she squared a deck, and set them by his elbow, and stood.

At the foot of the back stairs she stopped.

"We'll check it," she said. "In the morning. Both of us."

"Goodnight, June."

She looked at him a second longer than she needed to. Then she went up, and he heard her door close, and the house was quiet.

---

He didn't go up.

He sat at the farm table with the hatbox and the long row of notebooks with their yellow years, and the one book open at November. After a while he took off his grandfather's watch and wound it, slowly, all the way, the little crown clicking under his thumb, and put it back on. It was two o'clock. Then it was three.

The fog pressed white against the windows. Every six seconds, through it, Merrow Light went green and faded, green and faded, and the bell buoy off Shag Ledge kept ringing, low and uneven.

He kept his hand flat on the page, over the only plain words in thirty years, and sat there until it was time for the Galley to open.


---

# Chapter 33

## Blueberry or Apple

At ten minutes to five on Halloween morning the town wharf was black and wet and empty, and the fog was so thick that the lights of the Galley seemed to hang in it by themselves, with no building around them.

Archie knocked on the glass.

Bev Thibodeau was behind the counter with her back to him, sliding pies into the case. She turned, looked at him through the door, and pointed at the clock over the coffee urn. Then she went back to the pies.

He stood on the step in his buffalo-check coat with his hands in his pockets and waited. Out in the harbor, somewhere beyond the moorings, the bell buoy was ringing, slow and uneven, and a boat engine he couldn't see turned over and caught and went away toward it. At five o'clock exactly the bolt went back.

"Sit down," said Bev. "You're letting the fog in."

He sat on the stool at the end of the counter by the register. The Galley at five in the morning was a different room from the Galley at seven — chairs still upside down on the tables, the booths empty, the windows gray, the grill only beginning to tick as it heated. It smelled of coffee and hot sugar and the bleach she'd used on the floor last night. The radio on the shelf by the kitchen door was reading the marine forecast in the voice of a man who had read it ten thousand times: *fog, visibility under one mile, seas two to four feet.*

Bev took the chairs down off the liars' table, one at a time, and set them around it at the angles they liked. She filled the urn. She poured a mug of coffee and put it in front of him without being asked, and then she looked at him properly for the first time, over her reading glasses, with the pot still in her hand.

"You've been up all night."

"Yes."

"You look it." She put the pot back on the warmer. "Drink that."

He drank some of it. It was too hot and too strong and exactly what he needed, and it didn't help.

He had driven down Shore Road in the dark with the whole thing laid out in his head, and somewhere on the way he had decided what he wanted. He wanted Bev to laugh at him. He wanted her to say *No, that's some other Langley, there were Langleys all up and down this coast,* and put a piece of pie in front of him and tell him he looked like something left on a mooring, and send him home to bed. He'd driven down here to be told he was wrong by the one person in Merrow who'd never once pretended he was right.

"Bev," he said. "Settle something for me."

"I'm opening."

"It's a small thing. A name."

She was wiping down the counter with a gray cloth, in long strokes, the way she did every morning. She didn't stop. "Go on."

"Langley," said Archie. "Peter Langley. He's on the memorial."

The cloth stopped.

Bev stood there with her hand flat on the counter. Then she folded the cloth in half, and in half again, and set it down by the register.

"Langley?" she said. "That's Hollis."

He didn't say anything.

"Hollis Langley, she was, back when. Married Peter in — eighty-three, it would've been. Roland and I went." Bev picked the cloth up again and found nothing to do with it. "She kept Tibbetts at the school. Said she'd taught two hundred kids to spell it already and she wasn't starting over. Everybody else called her Langley. Then after Peter she went back to Tibbetts for all of it. Mail, bank, everything." She looked at him. "You didn't know that."

"No."

"No reason you would. It's twenty years."

"She had a boy."

"Danny." Bev said it the way you'd set down something heavy you'd carried a long way. "Up the quarry. August, oh-one. He was fifteen. Went off the high ledge with the other boys and didn't come up." She was quiet a moment. "Danny Langley ate a whole blueberry pie on that stool you're sitting on, when he was twelve, on a bet. Roland bet him he couldn't. Roland paid up, too. Five dollars."

Archie put both hands around the mug.

"And Peter—" he said.

"Two years after. Near enough. November." Bev's flat Down East voice didn't change at all, which was how he knew what it cost. "Took the *Hollis T.* out in a gale warning, middle of the afternoon, all by himself. Nobody knew why. Nobody ever did know why. She fetched up on Shag Ledge. They found him two days after." She looked past Archie, out the gray window, toward the place in the fog where the bell buoy was. "Hollis sat in that back booth every morning that winter. Wouldn't eat. I fed her anyhow."

The urn began to gurgle. Neither of them looked at it.

---

He had thought, at three in the morning at his own kitchen table, that it would feel like a trick coming clear — the moment in the second row when you finally see the thread. It didn't. It felt like a room he'd been living in for a week, filling up slowly with water, and everything in it was something he had already touched.

A letter to a television company in May, before he'd come back to Merrow. *Don't cancel her. Beat her. In Gus's house.* Page one-twelve, and three booths in this diner chanting the answer — Mrs. Tibbetts's séance unit, a rite of passage, every kid in Merrow. A fire permit in a cardigan pocket. Candles lit by one pair of hands, because nobody else was striking a match on her stage. Teddy's voice from the wing, *Hollis, sorry, can you slide the horn left*; a camera swinging away to the window for forty seconds; a woman in black stage gloves walking off with a tray among several other people in black stage gloves. A fall border cut to the ground in October and the whole bed turned over, in the porch light, while he stood with his arms full of costumes. *Hers are a foot taller. The deer won't touch it.* Shannon on the phone with a free reading in the spring, then three more at three hundred and fifty dollars apiece. *Hollis already gave me the speech.* A four-year-old on a lap in a plywood booth, saying that Daddy said it was warm.

*Oh, sweetheart. The water's never warm here.*

He had heard it. He had stood on the step and heard it and thought it was the kindest thing anybody had said in that building all week, and gone on up and asked about a light cue.

He looked up. Bev was watching him.

She hadn't asked a single question. She was a Maine woman of sixty-seven who had buried a husband and fed a widow through a winter, and she had been watching faces across this counter for forty years, and she had seen his, and she had heard the name he'd put next to it. That was all she had. It was enough. Something in her square, plain face had gone very still, and then, slowly, it gave way, and Archie watched Bev Thibodeau — who had never once sat down behind her own counter in his presence — reach for the stool by the register and sit on it.

"Archie," she said. "What is this."

He couldn't tell her. He found he physically could not make the words; they stopped somewhere in his chest.

Bev looked at him a long time. Then she nodded, once, the way she nodded when somebody told her they were paying cash.

"All right," she said. "I don't want to know. I don't." She took off her reading glasses and pressed her thumb and finger into the corners of her eyes, and put the glasses back on. "But whatever it is, it goes to Tess Calloway. Not to you. Not to Dale. To Tess."

"I know."

"Today."

"I have to talk to Hollis first."

Bev's eyes narrowed. "Why."

"Because I owe her that." He heard how it sounded. He said it anyway. "Because she gave me the keys, Bev. In August. She brought me a pie and the keys to the opera house and told me I looked like I needed both. I'm not going to let her find out from a cruiser in her dooryard."

Bev was silent for a while. Out on the wharf, a truck door slammed, and then another.

"Show's tonight," she said.

"Seven o'clock."

"Done by ten?"

"About ten."

"Then at ten o'clock," said Bev, "I'm calling Tess myself. I don't care what it is. I'll give Hollis her show, if that's what this is. I'm not carrying it one minute past ten." She stood up off the stool. "You hear me?"

"I hear you."

She looked at him a moment longer. Then she went to the pie case and slid the door open.

"Blueberry or apple."

"Bev, I'm not—"

"I didn't ask if you were hungry. There's two. Pick."

He looked at the pies. "Blueberry," he said.

She cut him a slice, a wide one, and put it on a plate in front of him with a fork, and went back to the urn. He sat looking at it. He didn't eat it.

---

The bell over the door rang at a quarter past five, and the cold came in, and the liars' table came in with it — Elwin Coombs first, then Dickie Thibodeau, then the long silent shape of Pickle, in their barn coats and their caps, stamping the fog off their boots. They went past the counter toward their round table by the kitchen door, the way they had gone past it every morning since August, and Bev was already pouring their coffee.

Elwin slowed beside the end stool. He looked at the man sitting there, at his gray face and his untouched pie, and he stopped.

"Morning, Archie," said Elwin Coombs.

It was the first time in sixty-six days that the liars' table had spoken to him. He had kept count of every one of them.

Archie was looking out the window, at the fog where the bell buoy was ringing, and he didn't hear it.


---

# Chapter 34

## The Trumpet Woman

Her father had kept everything on paper.

Tess Calloway had known that her whole life. When she was nine and her mother left, Walt Calloway had come home from the station that night and sat at the kitchen table and written it down — not in a diary, he didn't keep a diary, but on a yellow legal pad, in his square block capitals, the way he wrote up a fender-bender: DATE. TIME. WHAT WAS SAID. Then he'd torn off the sheet and folded it in thirds and put it in the drawer by the stove with the takeout menus and the warranty for the refrigerator, and as far as Tess knew it was still there, in the house on Pound Hill that she was renovating one weekend at a time, badly.

He'd run the Merrow Police Department the same way for twenty-nine years. Every call, every complaint, every lost dog and every drunk and every summer kid who'd taken a lobster boat for a joyride had gone onto paper, and the paper had gone into the green steel filing cabinets in the basement of the town hall, one drawer per year, filed alphabetically by the name of whoever had walked in the door to complain.

Not by the name of whoever they'd complained about. By complainant. That was the thing nobody at the State Police had known, and the thing it had taken her until three o'clock Thursday afternoon to think of.

"Two thousand three," said Dale Pinkham, and hauled the drawer open with a shriek of rusted runners.

The basement smelled of damp stone and old paper and the oil furnace that roared on the other side of the wall. A single bulb hung over the cabinets. Dale had brought down two folding chairs from the select board room and a box of doughnuts from the Galley, and he'd eaten two of the doughnuts on the stairs.

"You want to tell me what we're looking for?"

"I'll know it when I see it."

"Ayuh. That's what your dad used to say." He handed her the first fat handful of manila folders. "He'd have been at this an hour ago."

She'd been uneasy since Wednesday. Uneasy wasn't evidence, and she didn't take uneasy to her lieutenant, but she couldn't make it go away. She'd watched the footage of the controls segment forty times — the magician with the trumpet at his mouth, his cheeks puffing, that obscene sound — and every time she watched it she believed him, and every time she believed him she told herself that was precisely what he was paid to make people do. She'd read the lab report on the scarf: aconitine and lipstick, together, on a single fold, in a smear consistent with wiping. Consistent with wiping something off. Not putting something on. She had stood in the rain in the town hall parking lot on Wednesday and watched Archie Fairweather's face when Brannock said *your mother*, and it had not been the face of a man protecting himself.

And then on Thursday morning at the jail, in the corridor outside the attorney visiting room, she had passed him coming out with a sheet of paper in his hand, Ivy Crane's signature on it and Margaret Okafor's under that.

Okafor had been happy to tell her what it was. Okafor wanted a third party more than she wanted lunch. *A man in Maine, Sergeant. Two thousand three. My client's mother stopped using the trumpet in private sittings after him and didn't start again for years. Find him, and you'll find someone with a much better reason than my client had.*

A man in Maine. Two thousand three. Tess had driven back to Merrow from Rockland thinking about a drawer in her father's kitchen, and had stopped the car on the side of Route 1 and sat there for a minute with the engine running.

"Abbott," read Dale, from the folder in his hand. "Dog bite. Ayuh, that's the Abbotts' shepherd, that dog bit everybody." He set it on the pile. "Ames. Burglary. Ames — that's the summer place out on the point, they lost a television." Another folder. "Bickford. Noise complaint." He looked up. "Your dad wrote down *noise complaints*?"

"He wrote down everything."

"Ayuh, I can see that."

Dale had come to Merrow in 2010, from the sheriff's office in Rockland, the year her father retired. He knew the town's people now, but he didn't know its paper. He read every name aloud, and said who they were, and was right about most of them, and Tess let him, because it helped to have someone talking.

It was twenty minutes past noon when she found it.

It was a thin folder, near the back of the drawer, with a strip of masking tape on the tab and two words in her father's hand: LANGLEY, H.

She knew before she opened it. She knew the way you know a voice on the phone before it says its name. Hollis Langley. That was what her mother had called her, and the women at church, and the woman at the post office — *Hollis Langley, Peter's wife* — back when Tess was small and Mrs. Tibbetts was Mrs. Tibbetts only at the high school, where she'd taught Tess to diagram a sentence and told her, in the eleventh grade, that she wrote like a person giving testimony and that it wasn't a compliment.

And Danny. Danny Langley, a grade ahead of her at Penobscot Regional, with his mother's laugh and his father's ears, who had played Puck in the Merrow Players' summer show the year Tess was twelve and had swung down out of the flies on a rope and gotten stuck, and laughed so hard hanging there that the whole audience had laughed with him. Danny Langley, who drowned at the quarry the summer Tess turned fourteen.

She opened the folder.

There were three sheets of yellow legal paper, written on both sides, and a photocopy. Her father's square capitals marched down the pages, pressed so hard into the paper she could feel them with her fingertips from the back.

*DATE: 11/26/03. COMPLAINANT: HOLLIS LANGLEY, 14 PUDDING LANE. RE: HUSBAND PETER LANGLEY (DECEASED 11/18/03, LOST AT SEA, SHAG LEDGE).*

*COMPLAINANT STATES SHE HAS FOUND HUSBAND'S NOTEBOOK AND RECEIPTS SHOWING HE ATTENDED 41 PRIVATE SESSIONS 10/01–11/03 WITH A "SPIRITUALIST MEDIUM" CALLING HERSELF SISTER LORI CRANMER, PARK ST., ROCKLAND (OVER THE LAUNDROMAT). TOTAL PAID $61,500. SECOND MORTGAGE TAKEN ON HOUSE W/O COMPLAINANT'S KNOWLEDGE.*

*NOTEBOOK RECORDS "MESSAGES" FROM SON DANIEL (DECEASED 8/01) GIVEN THROUGH A "TRUMPET." MESSAGES INCLUDE: "DAD IT'S WARM HERE," "THE WATER'S WARM," "I'M NOT COLD ANYMORE," AND IN FINAL ENTRIES "COME SEE ME ON THE WATER."*

*COMPLAINANT BELIEVES THESE MESSAGES CAUSED HUSBAND TO TAKE BOAT OUT IN GALE.*

There was more. A call to the Rockland police: the room over the laundromat already empty, the landlord paid through December in cash. A call to an assistant attorney general in Augusta, whose name her father had written down and underlined twice. And at the bottom of the last page, in the same capitals, pressed so hard the pen had nearly gone through:

*NO STATUTE APPLIES PER AAG. SHE NEVER TOLD HIM TO GO OUT IN A STORM. NOT IN SO MANY WORDS. TOLD H.L. I AM SORRY. NOT FILED W/ STATE.*

The photocopy was of a flyer. It had been pinned to a corkboard once — there were holes in the corners — and copied badly, so the gray had gone to black in places. MESSAGES FROM LOVED ONES, it said across the top, in a font that was trying to look like handwriting. SISTER LORI CRANMER, SPIRITUALIST. PRIVATE SITTINGS. Underneath was a photograph of a woman in her thirties with dark hair and a dark mouth and steady, amused eyes.

There was no white streak yet. It didn't matter. Tess had looked at that face on the autopsy table on Sunday afternoon.

"Tess?" said Dale. He'd stopped reading names. "You all right?"

"Who's Hollis Langley," she said. It came out wrong — not a question.

"Couldn't tell you. Before my time."

"It's Hollis Tibbetts, Dale."

He looked at her. She watched him work it out, the way you'd watch a man realize the ice under his boots was thinner than he'd thought. His big face went slowly slack under the walrus mustache, and he set the folder in his hands down on the pile very carefully, as if it might break.

"Oh," he said. "Oh, no. Oh, Hollis."

---

She needed one more thing, and she needed it before she called Augusta, because Brannock was going to ask her how she knew the woman in a twenty-two-year-old photocopy was the woman in the morgue, and *I looked at her* was not an answer a lieutenant accepted.

Sea View was four minutes up the hill.

Marisol was at the nurses' station, and smiled when she saw her. "You just missed him," she said. "The magician. He was here at noon, same as always."

"Was he." Tess kept her voice even.

"He did a trick for Carol." Marisol nodded toward the common room. "She hasn't let go of it."

Carol Gilley was sitting in her armchair by the window in a cardigan that was, for once, buttoned right, holding a single playing card in both hands. It was the queen of hearts. Across the face of it, in black marker, in someone else's handwriting, was the word WARREN. Carol was looking at it and smiling, and every so often she turned it over to check the back, and then turned it face up again, as if to make sure the name was still there.

Tess stood in the doorway and looked at that for longer than she meant to.

Her father was by the piano in his wheelchair, humming. He was in a good way today; she could see it from across the room — the clear blue eyes, the big hands quiet on the arms of the chair. He smiled when he saw her. "Tessie."

"Hi, Dad." She pulled the footstool over and sat down by him and took the photocopy out of her coat, folded so only the photograph showed. "Dad, I need you to look at something for me. Do you know this woman?"

He took it. His hands shook and the paper shook with them. He held it a long way from his face, the way he did now, and looked at it for so long that she started to think he was gone again, somewhere in 1988, and wouldn't be coming back today.

"That's the trumpet woman," said Walt Calloway.

Tess didn't move.

"Hollis's woman." He tapped the photograph with one big finger. "Hollis came to see me about her. Sat in my office and cried. Hollis Langley never cried in her life, Tessie, not even at the boy's funeral. I couldn't do a thing." His face clouded. "Nothing to charge. I told her I was sorry. I told her—"

"I know, Dad. You wrote it down."

"I wrote it down." He nodded slowly, satisfied, and handed the paper back to her. Then he looked at her — properly, the lighthouse look — and smiled, and she saw him leave 2003 and go somewhere else, somewhere softer.

"You made me mail that letter," he said.

"What letter?"

"To the magic fella. On the TV. You were twelve. You wrote him how he did the lighthouse trick and you made me put two stamps on it, so it'd get there faster." He chuckled, deep in his chest. "Two stamps. I told you that's not how stamps work."

"Dad."

"He wrote you back, too. You remember? You kept it in your—"

"Dad," said Tess. "I remember."

She kissed the top of his head, and stood, and went out onto the long front porch of Sea View, where the rocking chairs sat empty in the cold and the whole bay lay spread out below in the hard bright afternoon light. She stood at the rail for a moment until her face was her own again. Then she called her lieutenant.

Brannock listened to all of it without interrupting, which was how she knew he was taking it seriously.

"A twenty-two-year-old complaint," he said when she'd finished, "a flyer, and an Alzheimer's patient."

"And the forty seconds, Lieutenant. She slid it. At the producer's request, in gloves, in the one gap in the footage. We've had her on our own list since Sunday. We just didn't have a reason."

There was a pause on the line. She could hear him breathing through his nose.

"Write me the affidavit," he said. "House and outbuildings. I'll walk it to the judge myself. You don't have probable cause to arrest her on this and you know it, so nobody goes near her until we've been through that shed. Where will she be tonight?"

"The opera house. She's running the lights for the show."

"Of course she is." Another pause. "Bissonnette in plain clothes in the hall from five. A car on Main Street. Quietly, Sergeant. If the search gives us anything, we take her at intermission, out of sight of three hundred people and a television crew. Not before." Then, in a different voice: "And Calloway. Your magician."

She didn't say anything.

"He's been to see the daughter. He had her sign something. Does he know?"

"I don't know what he knows."

"Find out," said Brannock, and hung up.

She drove down the hill at a quarter past three with the affidavit already half-written in her head. At the bottom she could have gone left, on Elm, straight to the town hall. She went right instead, onto Pudding Lane, without quite deciding to.

It was a short street of small old houses with picket fences. Halfway down it, in front of a weathered Cape with a blue door and a big front border cut down to the ground for the winter, a long black 1958 Cadillac hearse was parked at the curb, with THE GREAT FAIRWEATHER — MARVELS & MYSTERIES in gold letters on its side.

Tess didn't slow down. She didn't look at the house. She drove to the end of Pudding Lane and turned onto Water Street and pulled over by the inn and sat there with both hands on the wheel.

He knew.

Of course he knew. He'd had Ivy Crane's release in his hand on Thursday morning, and Archie Fairweather had never in his life been able to leave a locked thing locked. He knew, and he'd gone to her, alone, because that was what he was — a man who couldn't walk past a closed door without trying the handle, and couldn't walk past a friend in trouble without trying to save her, and didn't seem to understand that sometimes those were the same mistake.

He hadn't called.

She sat there for a long time with the engine running and the heater on. Out on the harbor the afternoon light was going long and gold, and the bell buoy off Shag Ledge was ringing, faint and irregular, the way it did when there was a swell coming in.

At twenty minutes to four, her phone buzzed on the seat beside her.

It was a text, from a number she'd never saved and knew anyway. Two words.

*Intermission. Please.*


---

# Chapter 35

## Pudding Lane

He had not been able to ask her all morning, because all morning she had been surrounded.

By eight o'clock on Halloween the opera house had been full of people — Mattie Pinkham and three of her cousins on ladders hanging the new lights, Deb Coombs hemming a curtain, the liars' table in a row of folding chairs in the back, offering opinions about the ladders. And in the middle of it, everywhere at once, with her clipboard and her ring of keys and her reading glasses swinging on their beaded chain, Hollis Tibbetts: calling cues up to the booth, sending Corey from the Galley to the hardware store for gaffer tape, telling Archie his tails needed pressing and his hair needed cutting and that she'd do the first and wouldn't touch the second for a hundred dollars. She'd been cheerful. She'd been brisk and funny and completely herself. He had stood on a ladder beside her for an hour, handing up cable, and every time she laughed it went into him like a splinter.

He couldn't ask her there. Not in front of Mattie on a ladder, and Deb with her pins, and Elwin Coombs telling everybody he'd known Gus Fairweather personally and Gus had owed his father three dollars.

At noon he'd gone up to Sea View, because it was Friday and he always went on Friday, and because he could not think of one other thing to do with his hands. He'd done a trick for Carol Gilley. He'd sat with Walt Calloway and listened to the story about Gus and the police cruiser again, and laughed in the right place.

At half past two Hollis had gone home to finish June's costume.

At three o'clock he parked the hearse on Pudding Lane.

---

She was in the garden.

The front of the house was what it always was: a weathered gray Cape with a blue door and a picket fence, books in every window, a dress form wearing half a Victorian bodice visible through the glass of the front room. The big front border had been cut to the ground and turned over for the winter and was bare brown earth now, except for a row of dahlias along the fence, their stalks blackened by frost. Hollis was on her knees beside them on a folded feed sack, in her men's cardigan and an old felt hat, with a garden fork in her hands, lifting the tubers out of the cold ground one clump at a time and laying them on newspaper to dry.

Malvolio, enormous and gray, was sitting on the roof of the potting shed in the last of the sun, watching her do it.

She looked up when the gate creaked. She smiled.

"You're early," she said. "June's jacket's on the dress form. I've only the hem to finish. Did you want tea?"

"No. Thank you."

Something in his voice made her look at him a second longer. Then she went back to the dahlias. She worked the fork in under the next clump and levered it up, and the tubers came out of the earth in a fat knot, brown and pale and muddy, and she shook the dirt off them and laid them on the paper.

"You have to get them up before the ground freezes," she said, "or they rot. Everybody thinks dahlias are so tough. They're not. They're the most delicate things in the garden." She reached for the next one. "Peter used to say I fussed over them more than over him."

The fork went in. The tubers came up.

Archie stood at the edge of the bare brown bed, where all those tall blue flowers had been in September, with his hands in the pockets of his buffalo-check coat. He had thought, driving over, about all the clever ways he could do this. He knew a hundred. He could come at it sideways, the way you come at an audience volunteer — a little talk, a little warmth, a question she wouldn't see the point of until it had already landed. He'd been doing it to people for thirty years.

He didn't want to do it to her.

"Hollis," he said. "What was your husband's name?"

She stopped with the fork in the ground.

She didn't look up right away. She looked at the dahlias, and at her own gloved hands on the handle of the fork, and then she sat back on her heels on the feed sack and looked at him for a long time, the way she might have looked at a student who'd finally, at the end of the year, read the book.

"Peter," she said.

"Peter Langley."

"Peter Langley." She nodded slightly. "I was Hollis Langley for twenty years, everywhere but at the school. People forget that. It's been a long time."

"Did Peter go to Rockland on Thursday nights, Hollis?"

She didn't answer that.

She pulled the fork out of the ground and laid it across the newspaper, carefully, so its tines wouldn't touch the tubers. She took off her garden gloves and laid them on top of the fork. Then she got up off the feed sack, which took her two tries, and Archie didn't help her because she wouldn't have wanted him to. She brushed the dirt from the knees of her trousers.

"How did you know?" she said. Not as if she were denying anything. As if she were curious.

"The library card. In Gus's book. *H. Langley, December 2003.*"

For a second something almost like a laugh went across her face.

"Deb and her cards," said Hollis. "Forty years she's been told to throw those out." She shook her head. "Wait here."

She went into the house. The blue door closed behind her. Archie stood in the garden with the bare bed and the black dahlia stalks and the cat watching him from the shed roof, and the sun went behind the roofs across the lane, and the cold came up out of the ground all at once, the way it does in Maine at the end of October, as if somebody had opened a door.

She came back out with an envelope.

It was a long white business envelope, sealed. Across the front, in a teacher's upright, unmistakable hand, it said *Det. Sgt. Theresa Calloway — by hand*.

"I wrote it last night," Hollis said. "When they charged that girl with murder." She held it out to him. "It's all in there. Everything Tess needs. What I did and how, and where the rest of it is, and that Ivy Crane didn't know a thing about it. I'd have brought it to her myself tonight, after." She looked at the envelope in her hand. "I'd have liked to tell Vivienne first. I don't suppose that's possible now."

Archie didn't take it.

"Why?" he said. "Hollis. *Why?*"

"It's in there."

"I don't want to read it in a letter."

"No," she agreed. "I don't suppose you do." She was quiet a moment. "You'll get it, Archie. All of it. I'll tell you myself, I promise you that — not like this, in a garden, with the dahlias half up. Afterward."

"After what?"

"After the show."

He stared at her.

"Gus's show." Hollis's voice was perfectly steady. "Tonight. His night. I've run the lights in that building for thirty years, Archie. I ran them for him, at the end, when old Mr. Thibodeau's hands got too bad for the board — I was twenty-nine years old and he let me run the board for the Empty Chair. I've never once missed a show. Not one. Not when Danny—" She stopped. "Not ever."

"Hollis—"

"I'm not going anywhere. Where would I go? I'm sixty-six years old and I've got a cat." She was still holding out the envelope. "Take it. If I don't come down out of that booth tonight, it goes straight to Tess. And if I do, it goes to her anyway, and I'll go with it. Either way. Tonight. You have my word."

He thought about Tess. He thought about Bev at five in the morning in the empty Galley, saying *Archie* in that voice. He thought about Ivy Crane in Knox County Jail, who couldn't get out on a Friday night whatever anybody did, and who would walk out tomorrow morning either way if this envelope said what Hollis said it did. He thought about the opera house tonight, three hundred people, his grandfather's name on the poster, two hundred and eighty light cues that lived in this woman's head and in a coffee-stained cue book in a drawer.

He thought — and hated himself for thinking it — that she had never once in nine weeks lied to him. Not about anything.

He took the envelope.

Hollis let out a breath. It was the only sign she'd given, the whole time, that she hadn't been sure he would.

"Thank you," she said.

"I'm going to tell her," Archie said. "Tess. That I know. I'm not going to—" He stopped. "I'm not going to hide it from her."

"No. You shouldn't." She looked at him, and her face softened. "You're a terrible liar, you know. To people you're fond of. Vivienne says you always have been. It's the only thing about you that isn't a trick."

She went back into the house again, and this time when she came out she was carrying a jacket over her arm — dark blue velvet, cut down small, with silver braid frogging across the front and a high collar, the kind of jacket a magician's assistant would have worn on the stage of the Merrow Opera House in 1960. It had been one of Gus's. Archie recognized it from a photograph in the turret.

"For June," said Hollis. "The hem's done. I finished it while you were standing there trying to work out how to ask me." She put it into his arms, on top of the envelope. "She'll look like a queen in it. Tell her to stand up straight."

He held the jacket. It was heavy, and warm from being indoors, and smelled faintly of cedar and of Hollis's house.

Hollis bent and picked up her garden fork and her gloves, and looked down at the last of the dahlias still in the ground, and decided, visibly, to leave them.

"Go on," she said. "You've got a show to do, and so have I." She touched his arm once, lightly, the way she'd touched it on the ladder that morning, and then she turned and went toward the house with the fork over her shoulder.

At the blue door she looked back.

"Break a leg, Archie," said Hollis Tibbetts, and went inside.

---

He sat in the hearse at the curb with the velvet jacket on the passenger seat and the envelope in the inside pocket of his coat, over his heart, where he could feel its edge every time he breathed.

He took out his phone.

He wrote *Tess, I know who* and deleted it. He wrote *Tess, please come to the show* and deleted that too. He sat looking at the empty screen. On the roof of the potting shed across the fence, Malvolio stood up, stretched, and jumped down out of sight.

Archie typed two words, and looked at them for a long moment, and sent them before he could take them back.

Then he started the hearse and drove to the opera house, where the lights were already on.


---

# Chapter 36

## The Great Fairweather

By a quarter to seven there were three hundred and eleven people in the Merrow Opera House, which was eleven more than the fire marshal allowed and roughly a fifth of everybody who lived in the town.

Archie watched them come in through a gap in the stage-left curtain, the way Gus had taught him never to do and every magician in history had done anyway. The black drapes were gone from the windows now. The tall arched panes were full of the dark, and in the dark, far down, the lights of the moorings and the green wink of Merrow Light out at the end of the point. The hall was warm for once — three hundred bodies and the new radiator valves — and loud, and it smelled of wet wool and popcorn and the cider Bev had brought up in two enormous coffee urns and was selling at the top of the stairs for a dollar a cup, for the roof.

Half the children were in costume. There were three ghosts, a pirate, two Harry Potters, a very small hot dog, and Rosie Mayo, aged four, dressed as a lobster in a red felt suit with claws that went past her hands, sitting on her mother's lap in the fourth row. Shannon had her arm around Rosie and her other hand on Liam's knee. Liam was wearing a black cape and a mustache drawn on with eyeliner, and Archie understood, with a feeling in his chest he didn't have time for, that Liam had come as him.

In the front row, by their own insistence, sat the liars' table. Elwin Coombs, Dickie Thibodeau and Pickle sat side by side in their good coats with their arms folded, in the attitude of men who had come to see a lobster boat launched and would believe it floated when it floated.

Warren Gilley was in the sixth row on the aisle, in his bow tie, and next to him, in a wheelchair with a blanket over her knees and a pass from Sea View pinned to her coat, was Carol. She was holding a playing card in both hands. Warren kept looking at her instead of the stage.

Kev Duchesne was in the second balcony row with a camera on a little tripod and a lanyard that said, in Hollis's handwriting, PRESS — APPROVED — BEHAVE. Deb Coombs was beside him, watching the camera as if it might steal a book.

At the very back of the hall, standing against the wall by the stairs in a gray parka with her hands in her pockets, was Kayla Bissonnette, who was twenty-four, and not in uniform, and not watching the stage.

And above her, up in the horseshoe of the balcony, in the little plywood booth with its window full of soft amber light, a small square shape in a cardigan was bent over the dimmer board.

Archie let the curtain fall closed.

The envelope was in the inside pocket of his tailcoat, under the opera cloak, against his chest. He could feel the edge of it when he breathed. He had been feeling it for four hours.

"Places," said Lenny behind him.

Archie turned. Lenny was in his fourth sweater and a headset, holding a clipboard he didn't need. Behind him, in the wing, June stood in Gus's dark blue velvet jacket with the silver frogging, her hair up, her face very white and very set. She looked, Hollis had been exactly right, like a queen. Beside her, Vivienne, in black with diamonds that might even have been real, was doing breathing exercises from 1962.

"Junebug," Archie said.

"Don't," said June. "I'll throw up."

"You look—"

"*Dad.*"

He went out.

---

The house went dark. The talk stopped the way it does in a theater, not all at once but in a wave from the back, people hushing each other, a child saying *is it starting* and being shushed.

Then a single light — a soft, narrow spot from high at the back of the hall, from the booth — found the center of the painted harbor curtain, and the curtain rose, and there, alone on the bare stage in the circle of light, a silver ball the size of a grapefruit was hanging in the empty air.

Nobody had seen it arrive. It was simply there, shining, turning very slowly, about four feet above the boards.

The hall made the sound Archie had spent his life working for. It isn't a gasp. It's quieter than that — three hundred people breathing in at the same time.

He walked out of the dark at the side of the stage in Gus's cloak, and the spot widened to take him in, and he did not look at the audience. He looked at the ball.

"My grandfather opened every show on this stage with this," Archie said. "From 1949 to 1988. He called it the Silver Moon."

He lifted a square of white silk from his cloak and held it up in front of the ball by two corners, like a man screening a candle from the wind, and the ball rose behind the silk and settled against it, glowing through the fabric.
