When he walked, it came with him. He turned in a slow circle and the ball went around him, under his arm, over his shoulder, along the outstretched line of his other hand like a cat along a fence. He went down to the front of the stage, and it went ahead of him, out over the footlights, out over the front row, and hung there above the liars' table.

Elwin Coombs looked up at it with his mouth slightly open. Dickie Thibodeau leaned back in his chair to get away from it. Pickle, who had not spoken one word in Archie's hearing in nine weeks, slowly raised one finger and pointed at it, as though to make sure everybody else could see it too.

Archie drew the silk away. The ball hung in the air above Pickle's head by itself.

Then it drifted back to him across the footlights, slowly, like something tired coming home, and settled in his open palm, and was a ball. He held it up to show them. He tossed it once in the air and caught it.

The applause began at the back and rolled forward and broke over the stage, and Archie Fairweather — who had stood in front of thirty thousand people at a time and heard every kind of applause there was — found, to his complete surprise, that this one went straight through him.

For the next forty minutes he forgot about the envelope.

He didn't mean to. It just happened, the way it always had, since he was four years old in his grandfather's kitchen with a silver dollar between two fingers. The room took him. Three hundred people from one small harbor town, in their good coats and their children's costumes, wanting to be amazed — and he amazed them, because it was the one thing in the world he was sure he could do.

He did the linking rings with Deb Coombs, who refused to be impressed and then gasped out loud when the ring in her own hands linked through his. He did the needle trick, swallowing a packet of needles and a length of thread and drawing them out threaded, which made Bev, at the top of the stairs with her cider, say "Oh, for God's sake" loudly enough to be heard on the stage. He borrowed Warren Gilley's wristwatch — asked for it, with a bow; no lifting tonight — and smashed it to pieces in a cloth bag with a hammer, and Warren went red to the tips of his ears, and Carol laughed out loud in her wheelchair, the clear delighted laugh of a girl at a show. Then Archie had Carol open the playing card she'd been holding in both hands all evening — the queen of hearts with WARREN across it — and Warren's watch was taped to the back of it, ticking.

Warren looked at the watch for a long moment. Then he looked at his wife, who was holding the card up and showing it to the people around her, beaming. He put his hand over his eyes.

And then Archie went down to the front row and stood in front of the liars' table.

"Gentlemen," he said. "I'm going to need one of you."

Elwin and Dickie both looked at Pickle. Pickle looked at the ceiling.

"Sir." Archie held out a deck of cards to him, face down, fanned. "You don't have to touch them. You don't have to take one. All you have to do is look at the backs and think of any card in the deck. Don't tell me. Any card at all."

Pickle looked at the cards for a long time. Then he looked at Archie. Then, slowly, with the whole hall watching, he nodded.

"Now," said Archie, "for the first time in my presence, and I'm told for the first time in public since about 1994 — would you please stand up and tell everyone which card you're thinking of?"

The hall went absolutely still. Several people in the back stood up to see better.

Pickle unfolded himself from his chair. He was very tall standing up. He turned around and looked at the three hundred people of Merrow, Maine, every one of whom had known him all his life.

"Seven of hearts," he said, in a deep, rusty voice. And then, after a moment, as if it had been building up for thirty years: "And my name's Lionel."

The opera house came apart.

It took Archie a full minute to get them quiet enough to tell them to look under their seats. Every seat in the house — all three hundred and eleven of them, and the folding chairs in the balcony, and Bev's stool by the cider urns — had a playing card taped underneath it. Every one of them was the seven of hearts.

Lionel looked at the card under his own seat, and at Archie, and sat back down, and folded his arms, and Archie could have sworn — though he'd never have said so out loud — that under the gray stubble the man was very slightly smiling.

That was when Archie saw Tess.

She'd come in at some point and was standing at the back by the stair door, a little away from Kayla, in the charcoal coat with her braid over one shoulder and her hands in her pockets. She wasn't laughing. She wasn't clapping. She was just watching him — not the cards, not the crowd, but him, the way she'd watched him across her father's wheelchair at Sea View on the first day. As if she were trying to work out how he did it.

He held her eyes for a second over three hundred heads. She didn't look away.

Then he turned and went back up the steps to the stage, because there was one more number before the intermission, and it was Gus's, and the envelope was suddenly back against his chest as if it had never left.

---

The last number of the first act was the Sparrows.

It was the simplest thing in the show. Gus had done it to close his first half every year for forty years: a bare stage, a single light, the magician in his cloak, and out of the empty air of his hands, one by one, white doves. The cue was written into the cue book in Hollis's handwriting in three places, underlined: on the fourth bird, the stage goes to deep blue, and a single pin spot finds the magician's hands, and the doves fly up out of the light into the dark of the hall and settle on the balcony rail.

Archie produced the first dove from an empty silk, and the house sighed. The second from the collar of the cloak. The third from the air, bare-handed, in a flurry of white that drew a cry from somewhere in the back.

He produced the fourth, and lifted his hands, and waited for the blue.

The blue didn't come.

The stage stayed in its warm gold wash. The pin spot didn't find his hands. The light just held — steady, ordinary, wrong — the way stage lights hold when nobody is touching the board.

The four doves went up out of his hands anyway, because doves don't wait for cues. They flew up over the heads of the audience in a white clatter of wings and out across the dark hall, and the people of Merrow turned in their seats to watch them go, and three of them landed on the balcony rail just as they'd been taught.

The fourth flew higher, and landed on the roof of the light booth.

The booth window was dark.

Archie stood at the center of the stage in the wrong light with his empty hands still raised. Out in the hall the audience was applauding — they didn't know; there was no reason for them to know; it had been beautiful anyway — and he lowered his hands and bowed, because his body knew how to do that without him, and smiled, and heard himself say something about a short intermission and Bev's cider and the roof.

At the edge of his vision, in the stage-left wing, Lenny had already taken off his headset. Archie watched him go — moving faster than Archie had seen him move in thirty years — through the side door to the balcony stairs.

Somebody out front found the switch for the house lights and threw it. The hall came up bright and loud and full of people standing and stretching and talking about the seven of hearts.

Archie walked off into the wing. He stood there in the dimness by the rope rail in his grandfather's cloak, with the envelope against his chest, and waited, and did not let himself think anything at all.

It was a minute and a half. He knew, because he counted.

Then Lenny came back through the side door, breathing hard, his reading glasses pushed up on his bald head, and crossed the wing to him, and held out a small folded square of paper torn from the back of a cue book.

"It's for you," said Lenny.


---

# Chapter 37

## Intermission

The note was four lines in pencil, on the back of a page torn from the cue book, in the same upright hand as the envelope in his pocket.

*Archie —*
*Thank you for the lights. I'm sorry about the second half.*
*Ivy Crane didn't do it. You know that.*
*— H.*

He read it twice. Then he turned it over. On the other side, in Hollis's writing from some earlier year, faded, it said *Q.142 — blue to pin on 4th bird — GO*.

"The booth's empty," said Lenny. "Board's on the act-one preset. Her hat's gone. The fire-escape door at the end of the balcony's propped open." He paused. "The one the fire marshal made her unlock."

Archie looked up from the note.

The stage-left wing was a narrow dim space between the back wall and the edge of the curtain, crowded with Lenny's rope rail and a prop table and the Empty Chair itself — a plain bentwood chair with a white sheet folded over its back, waiting for the finale. Through the gap in the curtain he could hear the hall, loud and happy, three hundred people standing in the aisles with their cider. Somebody was laughing about Lionel.

"Archie." It was June, at his elbow, in the blue velvet jacket. Her eyes went from his face to the note in his hand. "What's wrong? What's going on?"

He didn't answer, and he saw her see that he didn't.

The side door to the balcony stairs banged open.

Tess Calloway came through it fast, with Kayla Bissonnette behind her, and she had a folded legal document in one hand and her phone in the other, and her face was the face from the hospital waiting room — the whole room in a second and a half — except that this time, when it reached Archie, it stopped.

"Where is she?"

"She's gone." He held out the note. "Out the fire escape. I don't know where."

Tess took the note and read it. Kayla, behind her, had gone white to the lips. "I was on the booth door, Sarge," Kayla said. "The whole time. She never came out the booth door, I swear to—"

"I know. It's not on you." Tess didn't look away from Archie. "The balcony fire door. It's supposed to be locked."

"Fire marshal made her unlock it on Thursday," said Lenny. "For the show. She grumbled."

Tess closed her eyes for exactly one second.

"We executed the warrant at six-forty," she said, very quietly. "Her house and her shed. There's a blue glass jar on the second shelf of that potting shed, Archie, with about four ounces of something in it, and a pair of black cotton gloves rolled up beside it like a pair of socks, and the evidence tech wouldn't let any of us in the door until he'd put his own gloves on." Her voice was perfectly level. "We were going to take her at intermission. Quietly. Out the back. Not in front of three hundred people." She looked at the note in her hand. "You knew. When you sent me that text, you knew."

"Yes."

"*Intermission. Please.*" She said it flatly. "You gave me a riddle."

"I gave you the time. She asked me for the first half. Gus's half. She said she'd come down out of that booth and go with you after—"

"She *asked* you."

"She gave me this." He took the envelope out of his tailcoat and held it out to her. *Det. Sgt. Theresa Calloway — by hand.* "She wrote it last night. When they charged Ivy. She said it was everything. She said it went to you tonight, either way."

Tess looked at the envelope for a long moment without taking it. Then she took it. She didn't open it. She put it inside her coat.

June was standing very still beside him.

"Dad," she said. Her voice was very small and very clear. "Who's *she*?"

He turned and looked at his daughter in her grandfather's velvet jacket, with her hair up and her face white, and understood that he had kept her in the audience all day. At breakfast, at seven, she'd asked him, *Did you check the Danny thing?* and he'd said *I'm looking into it*, and handed her a piece of toast, and driven her to school in the hearse, which she had for once allowed. He had kept it from her exactly the way he'd kept every method from her for sixteen years, and told himself it was a gift.

"Hollis," he said.

June's face didn't change. She'd been at the kitchen table half the night with him; she'd read the same tiny handwriting in the same small books. She had known, he realized. Somewhere under *lots of people are named Danny*, she had known as well as he had, and she'd let him put her to bed and keep it from her, because he was her father and she'd thought he'd tell her.

"You knew this morning," June said.

"Yes."

"And you didn't—" She stopped.

Vivienne's phone rang.

His mother was in the wing behind June, in her black and her diamonds, and she'd heard all of it; he saw that in her face. She looked at the screen and answered it. "Ozzie," she said, and listened, and her free hand went to her throat.

"Ozzie's on the wharf," she said. "For the children and their silly dare. He says somebody just took Elwin's skiff off the town float. No lights. Heading out." She swallowed. "Toward the bell."

Nobody said anything.

Out past the end of the point, in the cold black water off Shag Ledge, where Gus Fairweather's skiff had been found drifting in 1988 with his cloak folded on the seat. Where a lobsterman named Peter Langley had put his boat on the rocks in a November gale, and drowned, because a voice in the dark had told him the water was warm.

Tess already had her phone to her ear. "This is Calloway. I need Marine Patrol and Coast Guard Station Rockland to Merrow Harbor, a fourteen-foot skiff, one person aboard, no lights, heading for the bell off Shag Ledge—" She was already moving toward the stairs. She stopped at the side door and turned and pointed at Archie.

"You stay here," she said.

"Tess—"

"You *stay here.* This is mine now. This is my job, and it's my town, and she was my teacher, and you've done enough." She looked past him at June, and at the empty chair with the sheet over its back, and her voice changed very slightly. "You've got a show."

She went down the stairs. Kayla went with her.

Archie stood in the wing.

He could hear the house through the curtain, the cheerful roar of it. In about twelve minutes, Lenny would flash the house lights and they would all go back to their seats, and the second half would start, and at the end of the second half, forty minutes from now, the stage would go dark, and a single chair would stand in a single spotlight, and June would come out in Gus's jacket and say the words he'd taught her on Wednesday night in Lenny's workshop, and pull the sheet, and her grandmother would be sitting there, and he would be standing beside the chair looking surprised. He had promised her that. He'd promised her, in a hotel room in Boston, a real year in a real place, and in the opera house on Wednesday he'd promised he'd be beside her on the stage when she did it, and before either of those, last spring, without saying it out loud, he had promised her the one thing that mattered — that he would never again walk off a stage and leave her sitting in the audience wondering where he'd gone.

And out there in the fog, a woman he loved was sitting in a skiff with no lights at the place where her husband had drowned.

He couldn't do both. There wasn't a trick for it. He'd been looking for one for the last ninety seconds, the way he'd looked at every locked thing his whole life, and there wasn't one.

"Go," said June.

He turned.

She was standing in front of the Empty Chair in the blue velvet jacket with both her hands in fists at her sides, and her eyes were wet, and furious, and absolutely steady.

"June—"

"I'm still mad at you. I'm going to be mad at you for a really long time." Her voice shook and she pushed straight through it, the way Shannon had on the wharf. "But you know what she's going to do. You know better than anybody. So go."

"The Chair—"

"I know how to do the Chair. You taught me. All of it." She lifted her chin. "Grandma's in the chair anyway. You were only going to stand there and look surprised."

"Darling," said Vivienne, from behind her, in her stage voice — and then, in her real one: "I can do the second half in my sleep. I did it with your grandfather for twelve years. I know every word of his patter — I wrote half of it. And Lenny built everything else."

"Mattie has the cue book," said Lenny. "Hollis showed her the board. Thursday." He looked at Archie under his enormous white eyebrows. "Go."

Archie looked at the three of them — his mother in her diamonds, his oldest friend in his fourth sweater, his daughter in his grandfather's jacket with her fists clenched — standing in the narrow wing in front of the chair.

He had spent his whole life giving people a show. He had never once in his life let anybody give him one.

"Okay," he said.

He let June see it. He let her see his face while he chose, with nothing over it at all — no patter, no grin, no trick. Then he bent down and kissed the top of her head, the way he had when she was small enough to let him, and she let him, and he turned and went through the side door and down the back stairs two at a time, with his grandfather's opera cloak flying out behind him over his tails.

Tess was at the bottom of the stairs, at the stage door, on her phone. She looked up at him coming down.

She opened her mouth.

Then she shut it again, and turned, and shoved the stage door open into the alley, and held it for him.

The cold came in off the harbor like a wall. The fog had come up while they were inside. It hung in the alley and in the street beyond, thick and gray and glowing under the streetlights, and it smelled of salt and kelp and woodsmoke, and through it, from somewhere far out past the end of the point, came the sound of the bell buoy off Shag Ledge, ringing and ringing.

They ran.


---

# Chapter 38

## The Bell Buoy

Ozzie Lund had the engine running.

The harbormaster's boat was a twenty-one-foot aluminum workboat with a little wheelhouse, a blue light on a mast, and HARBORMASTER — MERROW in white stenciled letters down the side, and Ozzie was standing at the wheel at the bottom of the fuel-dock ladder in a float coat and a watch cap, with the spotlight already lit and swinging. He was seventy, and slow-talking, and had courted Archie's mother since September with a thermos of coffee and a seriousness that Archie had found hilarious right up until this moment, when he came down the ladder behind Tess and Ozzie put out one big hand and steadied him onto the deck without a word, the way you'd steady a child.

"Vivienne called," Ozzie said, and slammed the throttle forward.

The boat came off the dock with its bow up and its stern dug in, and the wharf lights went away behind them into the fog in about four seconds.

Then there was nothing.

Archie had been out on this harbor at dawn on Monday in Dale Pinkham's boat, with the light coming up gold over the water and Merrow Light standing white on its point and every mooring visible for a mile. It was a different place now. The fog had closed down over everything. The spotlight on the wheelhouse roof made a white cone in front of them, full of swirling wet gray, and showed them nothing but more of it. Beyond the cone, there was no harbor. There was no town. There was the throb of the engine and the slap of the hull and, out ahead somewhere, the bell.

*Clang.*

The swell had come up while they were inside the opera house. The boat climbed it and fell off the back, climbed and fell. Archie held onto the edge of the wheelhouse with both hands and felt the old familiar seasickness rise up from his stomach and decided, firmly, that he did not have time for it.

Tess was on her radio. "Marine Patrol, Calloway. We're outbound past the moorings, heading for the bell off Shag. Visibility's maybe fifty feet." She listened. "Copy. Twenty minutes." She lowered the radio and looked at Ozzie. "Coast Guard's coming out of Rockland. Twenty minutes in this."

"Ayuh," said Ozzie. "Won't need twenty."

*Clang.* Closer.

Archie stood braced in the stern in Gus's opera cloak and his tailcoat and his good shoes, with salt water coming over the side in sheets and soaking him to the knees, and found that he was thinking about a skiff — an old wooden skiff, thirty-seven years ago, drifting near Merrow Light at dawn with nobody in it and a black opera cloak folded neatly on the seat.

"There," said Ozzie, and swung the spotlight.

The bell buoy loomed out of the fog: a big rust-red iron cage on a float, taller than a man, rolling heavily in the swell, its bell clanging inside it. Beyond it, so close that Archie's stomach turned over for a different reason entirely, a long black hump of rock rose out of the water with white foam breaking over it, and the swell sucking back off it with a sound like a breath. Shag Ledge.

And between the bell and the ledge, rising and falling, a small open skiff with its outboard tipped up out of the water.

Hollis was sitting in the stern.

She was in her men's cardigan and the old felt hat she'd worn in the garden, and she had no life jacket on, and she was sitting perfectly upright with her hands folded in her lap, facing the ledge. When the spotlight hit her she didn't turn around. She lifted one hand against the glare, the way you'd shade your eyes from the sun at a picnic, and then put it back in her lap.

Ozzie throttled down and brought the workboat around in a wide, careful arc until it was lying off the skiff's side, between her and the open water, about twenty feet away, rolling. He kept the engine just ticking over. He knew this ledge. He'd been pulling people off it for forty years.

"Hollis," said Archie.

She turned her head then, and looked at him across the dark water, and he saw her see the cloak.

"Oh, Archie," she said. "You were supposed to be doing the second half."

"June's doing it."

Something went over her face in the hard white light. "Is she," said Hollis softly. "Good for June."

The swell lifted the skiff and set it down. Every time, it came down a little closer to the rocks.

"I don't have a trick," Archie said. He had to raise his voice over the bell and the engine and the sea, and he hated that; he wanted to say this quietly. "I want you to know that. I've been trying to think of one the whole way out here and I don't have one. I've just got me."

"I know, dear. You always have." She looked away from him, back toward the ledge. "That's the trouble."

"Hollis, come on board."

"Do you know what she said to me?" Hollis was still looking at the rocks. "On Thursday. The first day. I took her around the building — the stairs, the green room, the booth. I showed her Gus's trapdoor. And at the end of it she put her hand on my arm, the way she did with everybody, and she smiled at me, and she said, *Honey, where's the ladies' room?*" Hollis's voice didn't change at all. "She'd sat across a table from my husband forty-one times. She'd talked to him in my son's voice. She'd taken our house. And she stood in Gus's theater and looked me in the face and asked me where the ladies' room was." She was quiet a moment. "She didn't remember the town, Archie. She didn't remember *any* of us."

Archie held onto the wheelhouse. The boat rolled. Behind him he could hear Tess on the radio, low, giving their position.

He thought about a hatbox on a kitchen table at two o'clock in the morning. He thought about thirty small books in tiny, cramped, private shorthand, and his daughter's finger moving down a page.

"She remembered Peter," he said.

Hollis turned her head.

"She wrote him down," Archie said. "In her book. She kept a book for every year — every sitter, every Thursday, every dollar. Ivy kept them all. I read them last night." He made himself keep his voice steady. "November 2003. There's an entry. *P.L. lost at sea. Saw it in the paper.* And under that, in the same handwriting, it says, *God forgive me.*" He swallowed salt. "And then it says she put the trumpet away. She didn't use it for private sittings again for three years. Her fans have a name for it. She remembered him, Hollis. She remembered Peter."

For a long moment Hollis didn't move at all.

Then something went out of her — he saw it, even in that terrible flat light, even across twenty feet of black water. Her shoulders came down. Her folded hands came apart in her lap. She looked at him with a face he had never seen on her, not in nine weeks, not once — a face without any teacher in it at all.

"Oh," said Hollis Tibbetts. "Oh, Peter."

She stood up in the skiff.

"Hollis, *sit down*—" That was Ozzie, sharp, the first time Archie had ever heard him raise his voice. He had the orange ring buoy off its bracket in one hand, and he threw it — a good throw, a flat hard throw across the water that landed right against the skiff's side, its line paying out from the cleat.

Hollis looked down at it. She didn't reach for it.

And then the swell came.

It was bigger than the others. Archie saw it coming out of the fog behind the skiff, a long black shoulder of water rising up, and the skiff rose with it, and rose, and Hollis was standing up in the stern with no hands on anything, and at the top of it the skiff swung broadside and the sea dropped out from under it and put it down hard on the edge of the ledge with a crack like a gunshot.

The skiff went over on its side. Hollis went into the water.

She went under at once. Archie saw the felt hat floating, and the white wash breaking over the rock, and nothing else.

He didn't decide anything.

His hands were already doing it. His hands had the silver clasp of the opera cloak open and the cloak off his shoulders and folded — lining out, shoulders in, thirds, collar on top, the clasp centered — and laid down on the seat beside the wheel, without his mind anywhere near them. He kicked off one good shoe and then the other.

"Archie—" Tess, behind him.

"Line," he said to Ozzie. "Around my waist. I don't go in without a line. Never have."

Ozzie looked at him for one second. Then he had the end of a coil of rope around Archie's middle and knotted in a bowline in about the time it took to say it, and he braced his boots against the gunwale and took the strain.

Archie went over the side.

The cold was the worst thing he had ever felt.

He'd known it would be. He'd done ice-water tanks for television — the year of the Arctic special — and he'd learned then that the first thing cold water does is try to make you breathe. It hits your chest like a fist and your whole body gasps without asking you, and if your head is under when it happens you breathe in the sea. He'd trained for that for months. Nine hundred water-tank escapes, a breath-hold of four minutes on a good day, a body that knew how to go still in the water instead of fighting it.

None of that was forty-seven years old in fifty-two-degree water at night in a swell.

He came up gasping anyway, the cold like a band of iron around his ribs, and got his breath under control by main force — *slow, slow, slow* — and kicked toward the place where the hat was.

She came up beside the rock, an arm's length from him, her white hair plastered to her head.

He got hold of her cardigan. He got an arm around her.

And for ten seconds — he counted them; he always counted — she fought him.

It wasn't much. She was sixty-six years old and the cold had her too. But she twisted in his arm and pushed at him, with her hands flat against his chest, pushing him away, toward the boat — and he understood, with the sea in his mouth and the ledge looming, that she was not fighting to stay. She was trying to make *him* go. She was trying to push him back to the line, and the boat, and his daughter in a blue velvet jacket on a stage, and she was going to keep pushing until one of them let go.

"*Hollis,*" he said, into her ear, with what breath he had. "Hold on. I'm not letting go. So you have to."

The swell lifted them both.

Five more seconds. He felt her stop.

Then her hands, which had been pushing against his chest, closed into fists in the wet front of his tailcoat, and held on.

"Pull!" Archie shouted, to nobody, to the fog, and the line around his waist went bar-tight and dragged them both back across the black water toward the light, and a hand came down out of the glare and closed hard on the collar of his coat, and held, and did not let go — and he looked up through salt and fog and saw Tess Calloway leaning half out over the gunwale with her braid in the sea and her teeth gritted, hauling.


---

# Chapter 39

## Harbor Blanket

By the time Ozzie brought the workboat in against the fuel dock, half of Merrow was on the wharf.

They'd come down from the opera house at intermission — word had gone through the hall the way word went through Merrow, faster than the people carrying it — and they stood in their good coats and their children's costumes under the floodlights at the head of the wharf, in the fog, three deep along the railing by the Galley: Bev in her apron with a coffee urn's worth of cider still in a pitcher in her hand; Elwin and Dickie and Lionel; Warren Gilley without Carol; Deb Coombs; Kev with his camera held down at his side, not filming. Dale Pinkham had strung a line of yellow tape across the top of the fuel-dock ramp and was standing in front of it with his arms spread like a man holding back a tide.

The ambulance was already there, its back doors open, its lights turning red and white in the fog.

Archie didn't remember climbing the ladder. He remembered being on the dock, on his knees, with somebody's foil blanket around his shoulders crackling every time he breathed, and his whole body shaking so hard his teeth clacked. He was in his socks. One of them was gone. Somebody — an EMT, a young man with a beard — was holding his wrist and looking at his watch and saying things Archie didn't listen to.

Hollis was on a stretcher three feet away, wrapped in a gray wool harbor blanket with another foil one over it, her face blue-white under the floodlights and her white hair flat and dark with seawater. Two more EMTs were bent over her. Tess was crouched at the head of the stretcher, soaked to the shoulder on one side, her braid dripping.

Hollis turned her head and found Archie.

"It was me," she said.

Her voice was very small and very clear. The EMTs went still.

"Not the girl. Not Ivy. Me. I put it on the horn, at the table, when Teddy asked me to move it. Wolfsbane. From my own garden." Her teeth were chattering, and she spoke through them, carefully, like a woman reading aloud. "For Peter. And for Danny. And for Shannon, because she'd started on Shannon—"

"Hollis." Tess's hand came down on the gray blanket over Hollis's shoulder. "Stop. Stop talking." Her voice was rough. "Hollis Tibbetts, you have the right to remain silent. Anything you say can be used against you in a court of law. You have the right to an attorney—"

"I know my rights, Theresa." A ghost of the old classroom voice, through blue lips. "I taught you to spell them."

"—and if you cannot afford an attorney one will be appointed for you. Do you understand these rights as I've read them to you?"

"Yes."

"Then I'm not going to ask you any questions. Not one. Not here." Tess's jaw was set. "You're going to the hospital, and you're going to get warm, and tomorrow when a doctor says you can, you and I will sit down with a lawyer in a room with the door shut, and you can tell me all of it then. Not on a dock. Do you hear me? Not here, Hollis."

Hollis looked up at her for a long moment.

"You were always my best one at procedure," she said.

Then her hand came out from under the blankets, shaking, wet. It went into the pocket of the soaked cardigan, which they hadn't gotten off her yet, and came out holding something that clanked: an iron ring the size of a dinner plate, with what looked like a hundred keys on it — brass and steel, long and short, every lock in the Merrow Opera House and half the doors on Main Street. Seawater ran off it onto the dock.

She held it out across the gap. Not to Tess. To Archie.

"Somebody has to run the lights," said Hollis.

He took it. It was so heavy his shaking arm dipped under it.

The EMTs moved then, all at once, and the stretcher came up, and they were wheeling her up the ramp past Dale's yellow tape, past the people of Merrow standing silent along the railing — and Archie saw Bev put her hand over her mouth, and Elwin Coombs take off his cap, and Shannon Mayo, at the very end of the railing, with Rosie in her lobster suit asleep on her shoulder, standing perfectly still with tears running down her face and not wiping them away.

The ambulance doors closed. The lights turned away up Main Street into the fog.

The young EMT with the beard was trying to get Archie onto a second stretcher.

"No," said Archie.

"Sir, you were in fifty-degree water for—"

"Three minutes and forty seconds. I know. I counted." He was shaking so hard he could barely get the words out. "I'm all right. I've done worse. I did the Arctic special." He got one foot under him. "I have a show."

"Sir—"

"I have a *show.*"

Tess was standing over him. She had Hollis's envelope in one hand, sealed, dark with seawater at one corner, and her other hand was pressed flat against her own chest, and she was looking down at him with an expression he had never seen on her, and couldn't read, and didn't have time to.

"You're out of your mind," she said.

"Probably." He held out his hand. "Phone. Please. Mine's in the harbor."

She looked at him a second longer. Then she gave him her phone.

He dialed with a thumb that didn't want to work. Lenny answered on the first ring. Through the phone, faint and tinny, Archie could hear an audience laughing, and a piano, and his mother's voice, enormous, doing a bit of Gus's patter he hadn't heard since he was nine.

"She's alive," Archie said. "She's going to the hospital. Lenny, where are we?"

"Your mother's doing the Floating Lady with Corey from the Galley." Lenny's voice was perfectly flat. "He keeps waving to his mother."

"How long to the Chair?"

"Seven minutes. Maybe eight. Your mother's milking it."

"Put the chair on the trap. Keep Mother by it. I'm coming in from the alley."

There was a short silence on the line.

"You'll never make it," said Lenny.

"I've made it from worse. Seven minutes. Don't tell June."

He handed the phone back to Tess. Ozzie was already there, at his elbow, with his truck keys in his hand and the folded opera cloak over his arm, and his float coat still on.

"Truck's at the head of the wharf," said Ozzie. "I'll take you to the alley." He paused. "You'll want this."

He held out the cloak.

Archie looked at it, folded square over Ozzie's arm, and had no time to wonder who had folded it.

"Keep it for me," he said. "Give it to my mother."

Then he got up off the dock in one sock and a foil blanket and a soaking-wet tailcoat, with a ring of a hundred keys clutched against his chest, and he ran — staggered, mostly, but forward — up the ramp past Dale's tape and through the people of Merrow, who stood aside for him without a word, every one of them, and closed in behind him and watched him go.

At the top of the ramp, Bev Thibodeau stepped out in front of him and put the pitcher of hot cider into his free hand.

"Drink that," she said. "Then go."

He drank it. It burned all the way down. He gave her back the pitcher, and she took it, and pulled his foil blanket closed at the neck with one hard tug like a mother at a school bus, and stepped aside.

Ozzie's truck was idling at the curb. Archie fell into the passenger seat. The truck pulled out into the fog, and through the open window, as it went, he heard the bell buoy off Shag Ledge still ringing, out in the dark, for nobody.


---

# Chapter 40

## The Empty Chair

Gus Fairweather had built the crawlspace in 1951, and he had built it for a man who was five foot eight.

The hatch was in the alley behind the opera house, under the iron back stairs: a square wooden door set into the stone foundation, painted the same gray as the stone so that nobody had noticed it in seventy years. Archie had found it again in August with a flashlight and Lenny, and they'd crawled the length of it together on their elbows, cursing, and come up through the trap in the middle of the stage covered in dust, and Archie had laughed so hard he'd had to lie on the boards for a minute, and Lenny had said, *Your grandfather was a short man with no regard for anybody's knees.*

Ozzie's truck stopped at the mouth of the alley. Archie got out. He didn't say thank you. He'd say it later, every day for a year.

The hatch was open. Somebody had propped it with a brick, and there was a flashlight on the ground beside it, switched on, pointing in.

He got down on his knees on the frozen gravel in his one sock and his foil blanket and his ruined tailcoat, with Hollis's keys still clutched against his chest, and went in.

It was forty feet. He'd measured it in August. Forty feet of packed dirt and old stone, eighteen inches high in the low places, under the joists of the stage, with water pipes and electrical conduit running overhead and the dust of a hundred years on everything. The flashlight made a yellow tunnel ahead of him full of cobwebs. He went on his elbows and his knees, dragging himself, the keys clanking against the stones every time he moved.

He was so cold. He'd stopped feeling his feet somewhere on Main Street. The shaking had gotten into his jaw and his hands and, worst of all, his elbows, which kept going out from under him. Twice he put his face in the dirt.

*Get up, Sparrow,* he thought, from nowhere, in a voice that wasn't his. *Watch my hands.*

He got up.

Over his head, through the floorboards of the stage, he could hear the show.

It came down to him in pieces through the old pine: a piano playing something slow and minor — the Empty Chair music, the music Gus had used, that Lenny had found on a wax cylinder in the turret in September — and under the piano, the enormous waiting silence of three hundred people in the dark. And then, clear and steady and very close, a voice he knew better than his own.

"My great-grandfather," said June, "used to say that every trick is a promise."

He stopped crawling.

"He said that when a magician asks you to watch a chair — an ordinary chair, a chair you could buy at any yard sale in Maine — what he's really asking you is to believe him. That something that isn't there can be there. That someone who's gone can come back." June's voice went out over the hall, and it was steady, and it was his own patter, the words he'd given her on Wednesday night in Lenny's workshop, but she'd changed them. She'd made them hers. "He did this trick on this stage every Halloween for forty years. The last time anyone saw him do it was thirty-seven years ago tonight."

Archie started crawling again, faster. The flashlight beam jerked ahead of him. There was light in front of him now, a thin bright square of it outlining the trapdoor overhead, and below it, crouched in the dirt in black velvet and diamonds with a second flashlight in her hand, his mother.

Vivienne turned and saw him coming, and put her hand over her mouth.

Lenny was beside her, on his knees under the trap, one hand on the iron release lever Gus had built in 1951 and oiled every October since. He looked at Archie in the flashlight — the foil blanket, the one sock, the dirt, the keys — and his face didn't change at all.

"Twelve minutes," said Lenny.

"I said seven."

"You said seven. You took twelve. June's been stalling for five." He jerked his head upward. "She's good at it."

Above them, through the boards: "Would you all help me count?"

Vivienne was already moving out of the way. She'd been crouched directly under the trap, ready — it was her place, her cue, her chair; she'd been coming up through Gus's trapdoors since she was twelve years old. She shifted aside in the dirt without a word, in her diamonds, and took Archie's face in her two cold hands for one second, and looked at him, and let him go.

"Go on, darling," she whispered. "It's your chair."

"*One,*" said three hundred people, overhead.

Archie got onto the short iron ladder under the back leaf of the trap. There was barely room to crouch. His knees were on the rungs, his head bent against the underside of the stage. The chair stood on the front leaf, a foot from his face. Through the crack along the back one he could see a hard white line of light — the spot, on the sheet.

"*Two.*"

Lenny's hand was on the lever. His other hand came out, and gripped Archie's arm above the elbow, hard, through the wet sleeve of the tailcoat — the way you'd grip a man to keep him from falling, or to make sure he was really there.

"You came back," said Lenny.

It was the only thing he said. His voice did something on the last word. He let go of Archie's arm and pulled the lever.

The back leaf dropped open on its counterweight, silent, the way Gus had built it to. Above Archie, falling to the boards behind the chair, was the long tail of the white sheet, glowing with the spotlight beyond it like a lampshade. He went up into it the way he'd gone up through a hundred traps — one smooth motion, hands on the rim, feet on the rungs — and turned, inside the cloth, under the hoop, and sat, in the chair, in the hot white light, dripping. Below him the leaf swung shut without a sound.

"*Three—*"

Through the sheet he could see June's shape, standing beside the chair. He could see her hand come up and take hold of the cloth.

She pulled.

The sheet went up and back off him in one long white sweep, hoop and all — he'd taught her that, the way Gus did it — and the light hit him full in the face, and he sat there in it.

He must have been quite a sight. He knew that. A tall, wet, filthy man in a dripping tailcoat with one sock, gray dust in his hair and down one side of his face, wrapped in a crackling silver foil blanket that caught the spotlight and threw it back in a hundred pieces, like a cloak made of mirrors — with an iron ring of a hundred keys in his lap.

The audience didn't know. How could they? They'd seen a chair, and a sheet, and a count of three; and where a girl in a blue velvet jacket had told them they were going to see her grandmother, there was, impossibly, a soaking-wet magician in a silver cape, as if he'd come straight up out of the sea. It was the best trick they'd ever seen. It was the best trick anyone in Merrow had seen since 1988.

The sound began somewhere in the back and came forward like weather. Then they were on their feet, all of them, the whole hall, the roar of it going up into the pressed-tin ceiling and out through the tall dark windows into the fog.

June didn't move.

She stood beside the chair with the sheet hanging from her hand, in her great-grandfather's jacket, with her hair coming down, and she looked at her father, and her face was not the face of a girl who'd just done a trick. It was white, and wet, and absolutely still, and she knew — he could see her knowing — exactly what it had taken to get him into that chair, and exactly where he'd been, and exactly what it meant that he was here.

The audience thought it was the trick. June knew it wasn't.

"You're late," said June.

Her voice cracked right down the middle.

"I know," said Archie. "I'm sorry. I came the long way."

She dropped the sheet. She put both arms around his neck, foil blanket and seawater and dust and all, and held on — in front of three hundred people, the girl who wouldn't be dropped off at school in the hearse — and Archie Fairweather sat in his grandfather's empty chair in the middle of the Merrow Opera House and held his daughter, and did not vanish.

---

Afterward, when the curtain was down and the house lights were up and the noise out front had gone on for so long that Lenny had finally just turned off the stage lights to make it stop, Archie found his mother in the stage-left wing.

She was standing by the rope rail in her black velvet, with dirt on the knees of it and her diamonds slightly askew. Ozzie Lund was beside her in his float coat, saying nothing, with one hand at the small of her back.

Over her arm was Gus's opera cloak.

Vivienne held it out to him. She didn't say anything at first. She just looked at it — at the black wool and the wine-red silk, folded lining out, shoulders tucked in, in thirds, the collar laid on top and the silver clasp precisely centered — and then she looked up at her son.

"Ozzie says you did this," she said. "On the boat. Before you went in."

"I don't remember doing it."

"No," said his mother. "I don't suppose you do." She laid the cloak across his arms, carefully, the way you'd hand someone a sleeping child. Her voice was very quiet. "It's folded exactly the way my father used to fold it, Archibald. Exactly."


---

# Chapter 41

## Daylight

On Saturday morning the fog burned off by nine, and by eleven the Galley was so full that people were eating standing up at the pie case.

Archie had slept for nine hours in his clothes on top of the covers, with a cat he didn't own lying on his chest — Hollis's Malvolio, who had been brought up from Pudding Lane at midnight by Dale Pinkham in a cardboard box, on Hollis's instructions relayed by Tess from the hospital, and who had walked into Fairweather House as if he'd been expected. Archie had woken with a cough, a fever of a hundred, every muscle in his body informing him of its existence, and a mild but unmistakable conviction that he'd been run over by a riverboat.

He'd come to the Galley anyway, because there were people he owed.

When he came in, the bell over the door rang, and the room went quiet the way it had on Friday a week ago — heads lifting, forks slowing. Then somebody at the counter started clapping, and somebody else did, and Bev Thibodeau said "*Oh*, for heaven's sake, let the man sit down," and it stopped, and they let him sit down.

The liars' table had a chair pulled out.

Archie looked at it. He looked at Elwin Coombs, and Dickie Thibodeau, and Lionel, who was eating eggs and did not look up. Elwin pushed the chair another three inches out from the table with his boot.

Archie sat in it. Nobody said anything. Bev put a coffee in front of him and a piece of blueberry pie, at eleven in the morning, without being asked.

"I didn't order pie."

"You're eating pie," said Bev.

He ate the pie.

---

Ivy Crane came in at half past eleven, with Tess Calloway one step behind her.

Ivy was in the same oversized gray sweater she'd been arrested in, and she was paler than ever, and she had a manila envelope from the Knox County Jail property office under one arm. A bail commissioner had come in at eight and released her on her own recognizance on the remaining charge, the one about the scarf. The attorney general's office would move on Monday to dismiss the rest. Tess had driven her up from Rockland herself, and was standing just inside the door now with her hands in her coat pockets, the way she'd stood at the back of the opera house.

Ivy looked around the full diner. Her eyes found Archie at the liars' table and went past him, and found Shannon Mayo behind the counter with a coffee pot, and stopped.

She walked down the length of the counter. The room watched her do it.

"Mrs. Mayo," said Ivy. "I'm Ivy Crane."

Shannon put the coffee pot down on the warmer.

Ivy took a check out of the pocket of her sweater and laid it on the counter between them. It was for one thousand and fifty dollars. "That's what you paid," she said. "For the readings. I did the research for them. I found Tommy's obituary, and the boat's name, and your Facebook page, and the pictures of your kids." She didn't look away. "Everything my mother told you about your husband, I gave her. None of it came from him. I'm sorry."

Shannon looked at the check. She didn't pick it up.

"I'm going to write to everyone," Ivy said. "Everyone who ever sat with her. There are thirty books. I'm going to give them all back their money out of hers and tell them the truth. It's going to take a long time." She took a breath. "I wanted you to be first. Because of the — because it was here."

Shannon still didn't pick up the check. When she spoke, her voice was very quiet.

"Hollis did it for me," she said. "That's what they're saying. All of it. Because of what that woman told me on the phone."

Ivy didn't answer. There wasn't an answer.

Shannon looked down at the check for a long time. Then she folded it in half and put it in the pocket of her apron, and picked up the coffee pot again, and went down the counter to refill the man at the end, who didn't need it. Her hand was shaking. She didn't spill a drop.

Ivy turned around. For the first time since Thursday in the jail, she looked straight at Archie.

"Margaret Okafor says you're paying her," she said.

"I am."

"I told you not to help me."

"You did," Archie agreed.

Ivy looked at him a moment longer. Then something happened to her face that he had never seen it do: it moved toward a smile, and didn't quite get there, and stayed, halfway, as if it had forgotten the rest of the way.

"Vermont," she said. "In January. Vet tech school. I'm going to clean out kennels and get bitten by Labradors." She shrugged. "Nobody's going to hold anybody's hand in the dark."

"Sounds wonderful," said Archie, and meant it so much that she looked faintly startled, and went back to Tess at the door.

---

Shannon came to the liars' table twenty minutes later with the coffee pot, and refilled his cup, and stood there.

He didn't know what to say to her. He'd promised he wouldn't humiliate her, and then he'd sat in the dark and held hands while her husband's voice came out of a tin horn, and then he'd turned on the lights on all of it, and her children's godmother was in a hospital room in Rockport with a trooper at the door.

"I'm not going to forgive you," Shannon said. "I want you to know that. Not for a while."

"That's fair."

"I'm not saying it's fair. I'm saying it's how it is." She looked out the window at the harbor, where the moorings sat bright in the cold sun. "Liam wants to know if you'll teach him the card one. The one you did for him on Sunday."

Archie looked at her.

"I said I'd ask," said Shannon, and went back to the counter.

---

Warren Gilley came in at noon, alone, in his camel coat and his bow tie, and stopped dead in the doorway when he saw Archie at the liars' table.

Archie got up. It hurt. He went over to him before Warren could leave, and said, low, so that only Warren and possibly Bev could hear it, that he was sorry — not for the Galley, which he could never take back, but for every word of it, and that there was no excuse, and he wasn't going to offer one.

Warren listened to all of it with his face set like plaster.

"Noted," he said.

Archie held out the cardboard tube he'd carried in under his arm. It had come by overnight courier on Thursday, before any of the rest of it. Inside, rolled in tissue, were two posters, seventy years old, in red and black and gold: THE GREAT FAIRWEATHER WALKS THROUGH WALLS! and RADIO'S MASTER OF MYSTERY. A man in Philadelphia and a woman in Connecticut had bought them in September on the internet. They'd been very reasonable, in the end.

"These are from my mother," said Archie. "And from me. They were Gus's, and they're hers to give, and she wants Carol to have them. For her room. My mother says Carol used to sit in the front row every Halloween when she was a girl, with her hair in braids. She says she remembers her."

Warren looked at the tube in Archie's hands for a long time. He didn't take it.

"I won't take anything from you," he said.

"They're not for you," said Archie. "They're for Carol."

Something went across Warren Gilley's face. He reached out and took the tube. He held it in both hands against the front of his good coat, carefully, the way Carol had held the queen of hearts.

"For Carol," he said. Then he turned and went out without his coffee, and the bell rang behind him.

---

At half past twelve the liars' table rose as one, the way it always did, and put on its caps.

Elwin Coombs stopped beside Archie's chair. He looked down at him, and over at Dickie, and at Lionel, who nodded, very slightly.

"That trick," said Elwin. "The chair. Last night."

"Yes?"

"How'd you do it?"

The whole Galley went quiet again. Archie looked up at the three of them — at Elwin's weathered red face, at Dickie's suspenders, at Lionel, who had stood up in front of the whole town last night and told them his name — and he thought about a crawlspace forty feet long and eighteen inches high, and a girl's voice coming through the floorboards, and an old man's hand gripping his arm in the dark.

"I'll never tell you," Archie said. "Not as long as I live."

Elwin considered that for a moment. Then he nodded, once, slowly, with deep satisfaction.

"Correct answer," said Elwin Coombs, and the liars' table went out into the sunshine.

Archie was still looking at the door when Tess Calloway sat down in Elwin's empty chair.

"Hollis is asking for you," she said. "She's at Pen Bay. She's made her statement. Her lawyer's furious and she doesn't care." Tess looked at him across the table, at the pie plate and the fever in his face. "Can you stand up?"

"Probably."

"Then come on," said Tess. "I'll drive."


---

# Chapter 42

## Keys

There was a state trooper on a chair outside the door of room 214, reading a paperback, and he stood up when he saw Tess and looked at Archie for slightly too long, the way people did.

"Twenty minutes," Tess said to Archie. "I'll be right here." She sat down in the trooper's chair. "And if her lawyer comes up those stairs, I wasn't."

The room was small and warm and very bright. It had a window over the parking lot and, beyond the parking lot, a long gray stripe of Penobscot Bay going silver in the early afternoon. Hollis was sitting up in the bed in a hospital gown and somebody's blue cardigan, with an IV in the back of one hand and her reading glasses on their beaded chain around her neck. Somebody had brought her the glasses. Somebody had brushed her hair.

She looked at him over the top of the glasses as he came in.

"Sit up straight," she said. "You look like you were dragged through a hedge backward."

"I was dragged through a harbor."

"That's no excuse for slouching." She pointed at the chair by the bed with the hand that didn't have the IV in it. "Sit. You've got a fever. I can see it from here."

He sat.

For a moment neither of them said anything. Out in the hall, someone's cart rattled past. The heating ticked.

"How's June?" said Hollis.

"Mad at me."

"Good. She should be." Hollis nodded, satisfied. "Was she good? Last night? Before you came up through the floor like a drowned rat?"

"She was wonderful."

"I knew she would be. She has your hands and her own head." Hollis looked out the window at the bay. "I'd have liked to see it."

He didn't know what to say to that, so he didn't say anything.

"You want to know," said Hollis. "Go on. Ask me. I've told Tess and a man from the attorney general's office and a lawyer who says I'm making a terrible mistake, and I'll tell you, too, and then I'm done telling it."

"You don't have to."

"I know I don't." She folded her hands on the blanket, IV line and all. "That's why I'm going to."

She told him.

She told it plainly, the way she'd taught a thousand teenagers to tell a thing: beginning, middle, end, no adjectives you couldn't defend. Danny at the quarry, the summer he was fifteen, on a hot afternoon in August, showing off for a girl. Peter after — how he'd stopped talking, and stopped eating, and then one day in October started going to Rockland on Thursday nights and wouldn't say why, and came back lighter, and she'd been so grateful for the lightness that she hadn't asked. The notebook she found after he drowned, in his oilskin pocket in the shed: forty-one Thursdays in Peter's careful lobsterman's printing, every word the trumpet had said in their son's voice. *Dad, it's warm here. The water's warm. I'm not cold anymore. Come see me on the water.* The second mortgage. The flyer from the Grange with the woman's face on it.

"I took it all to Walt Calloway," Hollis said. "He was so kind. He sat with me for two hours. He called Augusta in front of me. There wasn't anything anybody could do." She looked at her hands. "So I went to the library."

Gus's book. Page one-twelve. She'd sat in the reading room at the long oak table and read how a woman in the dark with one hand free could lift a tin horn to her lips and become your dead child, and she had understood exactly how it had been done to her husband, forty-one times, and she had put her head down on the table and cried until Deb Coombs came out from behind the desk and put a hand on her back.

"After that I taught it," she said. "For sixteen years. Every senior who came through my room. The Fox sisters and Houdini and Gus and page one-twelve. *How the Grieving Are Fooled*, five pages, typed." She almost smiled. "I thought if I put it in enough heads, it would never happen in Merrow again. Nobody ever asked me why I'd added it. They just thought I liked ghost stories."

Then the woman on the television, eleven years of her. Then Tommy Mayo, in February. Then Shannon, in April, at Hollis's kitchen table, telling her about a message on Facebook.

"I wrote to that producer to *expose* her," Hollis said. "I want you to know that. In May I didn't mean anything else. I was going to stand up in Gus's theater on her own camera with Peter's notebook in my hand and read it out. Every word. I'd have done it, too. And then you came to town, and I thought — oh, *better*. Archie Fairweather. He'll catch her red-handed in front of the whole country, and I'll read the notebook after, and that will be that."

"What changed?"

"Shannon came for coffee. In September." Hollis's voice didn't change at all. "She'd had another reading. She sat at my kitchen table and told me, so happy, that Tommy had said he missed her on the water." She looked at Archie. "*Missed her on the water.* That's how it starts. That's exactly how it started with Peter. And I gave her the speech, Archie. I gave her the whole unit. I gave her Gus's book. She said, *Mrs. Tibbetts, I know it's probably not real, but it's the only time I get to hear his voice.* And she put Gus's book on her shelf and paid that woman another three hundred and fifty dollars." Hollis looked out at the bay. "You can't debunk a widow. I'd been trying for twenty years. You can't. Exposing her wouldn't have stopped Shannon. It wouldn't have stopped Peter. It would only have made her famous for a week, and then she'd have been back on the telephone."

"So you dug up the garden."

"So I dug up the garden." Hollis nodded. "I thought it would look like her heart. That's what it does, you know — it stops your heart. A woman her age, in the dark, at a séance. Her fans would have said the spirits took her. Some of them still will." She looked at him. "I didn't think anybody in Merrow would know what a numb mouth meant. And then on Sunday morning I was unlocking the radiators and I heard Tess say *wolfsbane* to you, and I went down to the green room and was sick in the sink."

"And Ivy?"

Hollis's face changed then, for the first time.

"I told myself they'd let her go," she said. "On Tuesday. I told myself it was the scarf — tampering, a fine, a night in jail, she'd be back in California by the weekend. I told myself that for two days. And then on Thursday they said *murder*, and I came home from your rehearsal and sat down at my kitchen table and wrote it all out, and sealed it, and I've never in my life been so ashamed of anything as I was of those two days." She was quiet a moment. "Tell her that. Will you? I'd like her to hear it from somebody."

"I'll tell her."

The heating ticked. Out on the bay, a lobster boat was coming in, very small, leaving a white line behind it on the gray.

"Would you do it again?" Archie said.

Hollis didn't answer for a long time.

"I'd like to tell you I wouldn't," she said finally. "I'm a schoolteacher. I know what the right answer is. I've graded it." She turned her head on the pillow and looked at him over her glasses. "Ask me in a year."

He nodded.

"I watched you do it," he said. "That's the thing I can't get past. On Saturday night I was standing at the back of the hall with Dale, talking about a riverboat, and I watched you walk around that table with a tray, and set out the glasses, and slide the horn when Teddy asked you to. I *watched* you. I was looking right at you, Hollis. It was the best trick I've ever seen in my life, and I never saw it."

"Nobody looks at the stagehand, dear," said Hollis. "Gus taught me that. In that very building. I was twenty-nine." She smiled a little, tiredly. "And everybody said nobody fools Archie Fairweather. It was on the posters for your challenge. I used to like that."

"Well," said Archie. "You did."

"I did." She didn't sound proud. She didn't sound sorry, either. She sounded like a woman stating the date. "I'd rather it had been anybody else."

She reached over with the hand that had the IV in it, slowly, and patted his wrist, twice.

"Now listen," she said, in a different voice — the clipboard voice, the general's. "I have three things for you, and then you're going to go home and take something for that fever. One. The Players. Vivienne runs the costume shop already; she can run the rest. *Our Town* in March, and don't let them talk her into a musical. Two. Rosie. I'm her godmother. Somebody's got to do it properly now, from closer than Knox County. I'd like it to be your mother, if Shannon will have her. Ask Shannon, not Vivienne; Vivienne will say yes before she's finished hearing the question."

"And three?"

"Malvolio."

"Hollis, I don't—"

"He's already decided. Dale said he went in your front door like he owned it." Hollis looked at him. "He likes the radiator in the kitchen. He'll eat anything except fish. He'll sleep on your chest if you let him, and on your face if you don't. Keep him, Archie."

"I'll keep him."

"Good." She let her head go back against the pillow. She looked, suddenly, very tired, and every one of her sixty-six years. Her eyes closed.

He thought she'd fallen asleep. He stood up, carefully, so the chair wouldn't scrape.

"Archie."

Her eyes were open again, looking at the ceiling.

"There's one more thing. It's nothing. It's just something I've wondered about for thirty-seven years, and I'd like to give it to somebody who'll wonder about it properly." She turned her head and looked at him. "That last night. Gus's last show. I was in the booth. I ran the board for the Chair. And afterward, when he came off, he went up the center aisle in that cloak, the way he always did, shaking hands, and at the back door Walt Calloway was standing where he always stood, in his uniform, in case." Hollis paused. "And Gus stopped in front of him. And looked at him. And winked."

Archie stood very still.

"Just winked," said Hollis. "And went out. And walked down to the harbor, and we never saw him again." She closed her eyes. "I always wondered what that was about."

At the door, the trooper was standing, and Tess was standing, and the twenty minutes were up.

Archie walked out to Tess's car in the cold bright afternoon with his hands in the pockets of his buffalo-check coat. In the right-hand pocket, where he'd put it that morning without thinking — the way you'd carry anything you'd promised somebody you'd look after — was an iron ring the size of a dinner plate, with a hundred keys on it. It knocked against his hip every step he took. It was heavier than anything he'd ever carried, and he carried it all the way home.


---

# Chapter 43

## The Memorial

At five o'clock the sun went down behind the town, and the harbor went from gold to pewter to slate, and Archie Fairweather was standing at the end of the town wharf in front of a block of granite with his hands in his pockets.

He hadn't meant to come here. He'd meant to go home — Tess had dropped him at Fairweather House at three, and he'd gone in and stood in the kitchen with the cat winding around his ankles and his mother asleep upstairs and June out somewhere with Mattie, and the house had been so quiet that he'd turned around and gotten back in the hearse. He'd driven to the wharf without deciding to. He'd walked past the Galley, closed now, and the bait shed and the stacked lobster crates and the fuel dock, where there was still a scrap of yellow police tape fluttering on the ramp rail, and he'd kept walking until there wasn't any more wharf.

The memorial was cold to the touch. He'd touched it.

*Peter Langley 2003.* It was about two-thirds of the way down, in letters a little worn but still sharp. He'd walked past it a dozen times since August. He'd read it at least twice — once on the first Friday with Shannon, once on Tuesday morning with Tess — and he hadn't seen it, either time, because there'd been nothing to see. It was just a name on a stone, in a town that had been carving names on this stone since 1847.

At the bottom, alone, the newest name was still pale inside the letters.

He heard her coming before he saw her: boots on the planks, unhurried, all the way down the wharf from the parking lot. He didn't turn around. She stopped beside him, a little to one side, and looked at the granite, and didn't say anything for long enough that the bell buoy rang twice, out in the dusk.

"They found Peter's notebook," Tess said finally. "In her piano bench. Under the sheet music. Forty-one Thursdays." She was quiet a moment. "I read it this afternoon. I'm not going to read it again."

Archie nodded.

"I knew Danny," she said. "Danny Langley. He was a grade ahead of me. He played Puck in the summer show at the opera house when I was twelve, and he got stuck on the rope coming down out of the flies, and he just hung there in the air laughing until everybody in the whole building was laughing with him." She looked at the name. "I'd forgotten that. I'd completely forgotten it until yesterday."

"I'm sorry."

"I'm not telling you so you'll be sorry." She turned and looked at him then, and her gray eyes were very steady, and very angry. "I'm telling you so you'll understand what I'm about to say."

"Okay."

"You had a sealed confession to murder in your coat for five hours yesterday." Her voice was level and low, and every word landed. "You knew who'd done it at five o'clock in the morning. You knew, and you went to her house alone, and you let her walk into a building full of three hundred people, and you sent me two words." She didn't raise her voice. She didn't need to. "Ivy Crane walked out of Knox County Jail at ten o'clock this morning. Nobody gets released on a Friday night. Nothing you did or didn't do changed one minute of that. So don't tell yourself you risked Ivy, because you didn't. That's not what you risked."

"I know."

"You risked *Hollis.* She was out on that ledge with no life jacket, Archie, an hour before we'd have had her. You risked that jar in her shed. She had the rest of the afternoon alone in that house after you drove away. She could have poured it down the sink. And you risked *you.*" Her voice went, for the first time, very slightly unsteady. "You went into that water at night with a sixty-six-year-old woman who was trying to push you away, and if Ozzie Lund hadn't put a line on you, you'd be on that stone. I had my hand on your collar. I couldn't feel my fingers. I couldn't feel anything."

The bell buoy rang. A gull came down onto a piling at the end of the wharf and folded itself up and looked at them.

Archie didn't make a joke. He thought of three. He didn't make any of them.

"You're right," he said. "About all of it. I should have called you at five in the morning from the Galley. Bev told me to. I didn't, because I wanted to hear it from Hollis first, and then because she asked me for the show and I wanted her to have it. I wanted her to have Gus's lights." He looked at the granite. "That was for me. Not for her. I'm sorry, Tess."

She looked at him for a long moment.

Something went out of her shoulders. Not all of it. Some.

"Brannock wants to know if you'd consult," she said.

He stared at her.

"Officially. On — he called them *the weird ones.*" Her mouth did something very small at the corner. "He says if this is going to keep happening in his jurisdiction, he'd rather have you inside the tent than outside it, setting things on fire. He said it this morning, at the command post, standing in front of your space heater, which he has not sent back to Portland." She shrugged. "I told him I'd ask."

"What do you think I should say?"

"I think you should think about it," said Tess, "for longer than it takes you to say yes."

"Yes," said Archie.

She closed her eyes briefly. When she opened them, though, she wasn't angry anymore. She was looking at him the way she had from the back of the opera house the night before — the way she'd watched him across her father's wheelchair on the first day — as if she were trying to work out how he did it.

"Hold out your hand," she said.

"Why?"

"Just hold it out."

He took his left hand out of his coat pocket and held it out. She took it by the wrist and turned it over, palm down. Then she reached into her own coat pocket and took out a watch.

It was his. It was Gus's — an old steel Hamilton with a cracked crystal and a worn leather strap, which had been on his grandfather's wrist at every show for forty years and on Archie's since he was nineteen. He hadn't noticed it was gone. He looked at his bare wrist and then at the watch in her hand, and felt his face do something it hadn't done since he was a boy at a birthday party watching a man pull a dove out of an empty hat.

"You were in the water," said Tess. "When I got hold of you. You had one arm around Hollis and the other one on the gunwale, and the strap was half off your wrist. It was going to go to the bottom." She was buckling it onto his wrist as she talked, carefully, her cold fingers working the old leather through the buckle. "So I took it."

"You *lifted* it."

"I took it."

"Off a drowning man. In the middle of a rescue. With one hand." He was grinning now; he couldn't help it. "Sergeant Calloway. That's a *lift*."

"I've watched every one of your specials," said Tess. "Some of them twice." She pulled the strap through the keeper and settled the watch on his wrist and, for a second, didn't let go. Her fingers stayed where they were, cold, on the inside of his wrist, over his pulse, which he was fairly sure she could feel. She was very close. The dusk had gone blue around them, and the first lights were coming on along the moorings, and he could see each separate eyelash and the gray of her eyes and the place at the corner of her mouth that had almost smiled.

She let go.

"Don't tell anyone," said Tess.

And something went through Archie Fairweather that he couldn't account for at all.

It wasn't the words. They were ordinary words. He'd said them himself a thousand times, to a thousand volunteers, with a wink, at the end of a trick. But the way she said them — quietly, looking straight at him, as though she were handing them back to him after a long time — stopped him where he stood, the way a name had stopped him in the dark a week ago, the way his hands had stopped in the straitjacket. As if there was something he'd known once and put down somewhere and couldn't, for the life of him, remember where.

"Tess—"

But she'd already turned. She walked away from him up the wharf, unhurried, her braid swinging, her hands in her coat pockets, past the fuel dock and the crates and the dark windows of the Galley, toward the lights of the town. She didn't look back. He was almost certain she was smiling.

Archie stood alone at the end of the wharf with his grandfather's watch ticking on his wrist and his own pulse going under it where her fingers had been.

Out past the point, in the gathering dark, Merrow Light flashed green, and went out, and flashed again. And the bell buoy off Shag Ledge rang, once, and then again, the way it did on a calm night when there was nothing out there at all — just a little swell coming in, slow, from very far away.


---

# Chapter 44

## Sparrow

Supper on Saturday night was just supper, which at Fairweather House had never once happened before.

Nobody tried to cook anything clever. Vivienne made a pot of the soup she'd been making since 1971, which had no name and no recipe and was different every time. Ozzie Lund brought bread from the bakery in Camden and stood in the kitchen doorway holding it until somebody told him to sit down. Lenny came in from the carriage house in three sweaters instead of four, which in Lenny was the equivalent of a party hat. And at seven o'clock, the back door opened without a knock and Bev Thibodeau came in out of the dark with a pie in each hand and set them on the counter.

"Blueberry," she said, "and apple."

"You brought both," said Archie.

"There's no third pie," said Bev, and sat down, and let June take her coat.

They ate in the kitchen at the scarred farm table, with the windows black and Merrow Light sweeping its green flash across the water every six seconds out past the point, and the jug of blue flowers gone from the middle of the table, and nobody mentioned why. They talked about the show. They talked about Lionel. Ozzie told a long, slow story about a summer person who'd once tried to moor a forty-foot yacht to the bell buoy, and Vivienne laughed until she had to put her spoon down, and Ozzie looked at her laughing as if she'd invented it.

Malvolio sat on the table.

He'd arrived at midnight the night before in a cardboard box, and in eighteen hours he had established that the radiator under the kitchen window was his, that Archie's chest was a bed, and that the farm table was a place where a large gray cat might reasonably sit, among the bread and the butter dish, with his paws folded under him, watching the soup.

"There is a cat," said Lenny, "on the table."

"He's not on the table," said Archie. "He's adjacent to the table."

"He's on the table."

"He's visiting."

"Hollis said he'll eat anything except fish," said June.

"Then he's the only sensible creature in this house," said Lenny, and gave Malvolio a piece of bread, without looking at him, as if it had happened by accident.

June was sitting across from Archie. She hadn't said much. She'd said almost nothing to him all day — she'd slept until noon and gone out with Mattie and come back at six with her hair blown everywhere and her cheeks red from the wind, and when he'd said *Hi, Junebug* she'd said *Hi* and gone upstairs. He'd let her. He hadn't known what else to do. He'd had a lot of practice, this week, at not knowing what else to do.

But she was watching him now, across the soup, in the way she watched an opponent across a debate table in the minute before the round began.

When the pie was gone and Bev had gone and Ozzie was drying dishes he hadn't been asked to dry, June put down her fork.

"I'm still mad at you," she said.

"I know."

"I'm going to be mad at you for a really long time."

"I know. You said."

"Okay." She looked at him a moment longer. Then she stood up. "Can I try something?"

---

The turret was cold. It always was. Archie turned on the green-shaded lamp on Gus's rolltop desk, and the round room came up out of the dark around them — the shelves of conjuring books, the framed posters of the Great Fairweather sawing ladies in half and holding doves with tremendous dignity, the black windows on every side with the harbor in them and the town's lights scattered on the water like spilled sequins.

And the trunk, under the north window, with its faded gold stars and its seven brass dials and the yellowed card on the lid. *To be opened when I'm gone. Not before.*

June knelt down in front of it.

Archie knew what she was going to do before her hand went out. He'd known since Tuesday night, when he'd sat on this floor himself with his fingers on the dials and spelled out three letters — S, P, A — and stopped, and taken his hands away, because he would not let a woman who'd lied to a grieving town for twenty years open his grandfather's trunk with a word she'd found in an old newspaper.

"June," he said. "That's her word."

June looked up at him.

"No, it isn't," she said. "It was never her word. She found it in the *Packet*. In 1988. A reporter wrote it down because Grandpa Gus said it to him." She turned back to the trunk. "It was always his. She just said it out loud. That's all she did." She put her fingers on the first brass dial. "Somebody has to say it out loud, Dad. You weren't going to."

He stood in the lamplight and didn't stop her.

She turned the dials one at a time. Each one clicked under her thumb as it went past each letter, a tiny, precise sound, like a watch being wound.

S.

P.

A.

R.

R.

O.

W.

She took hold of the brass hasp, and looked up at him once, and pulled.

The lock opened.

It opened so easily — so quietly, with such a small and ordinary *clack* — that for a second neither of them moved. Then the door at the top of the stairs creaked, and Archie turned, and his mother was standing in it with one hand on the frame, in her kaftan and her tweed coat, very still.

June lifted the lid.

The trunk smelled of cedar and mothballs and, faintly, impossibly, of pipe tobacco and Brylcreem. Inside lay a pair of white kid opera gloves, yellowed, folded neatly one on the other. Beside them was a deck of cards in a red case, worn soft at the corners. Beside that, a square of heavy cream paper folded once, with one word on the outside in a big, looping, theatrical hand that Archie had last seen on a birthday card when he was ten.

*Sparrow.*

His hands weren't steady. He let June unfold it.

*Sparrow —*
*You remembered.*
*That's the whole trick.*
*— G.*

That was all it said. June read it out loud, and then read it again silently, and then handed it to him without a word, and he stood holding it in the lamplight and found that he could not, for the moment, see it very well.

"There's something else," said June.

It had been lying on top of everything else — on top of the gloves and the cards, the last thing put in, face down. A postcard. June turned it over.

It was a picture postcard of a sunset, the kind they sell in racks on boardwalks: an orange sun sinking into a flat turquoise sea between two palm trees, and across the top in yellow cartoon letters, GREETINGS FROM KEY WEST. The colors had faded. One corner was soft, as if it had spent some time in somebody's pocket.

June turned it over again.

It had been mailed. There was a stamp — a thirty-two-cent stamp with a flag on it — and across the stamp a round black postmark, slightly smudged but perfectly legible: KEY WEST FL. MAR 14 1996.

It was addressed in the same big, looping, theatrical hand: *Master Archie Fairweather, Fairweather House, Gull Point, Merrow, Maine.*

And on the left side, in the same hand, four lines:

*Sparrow —*
*Still working on the trick.*
*Don't let your mother sell the house.*
*— G.*

Nobody in the turret said anything at all.

Archie read the postmark three times. *1996.* He'd been eighteen in 1996, a year away from his folding table in Harvard Square and the cups and balls for tourists outside the subway. Gus Fairweather had been gone eight years. The whole town had stood on the wharf in the rain for the memorial service. Walt Calloway had closed the case.

And somebody — somebody who knew the word — had come into this house, sometime after the fourteenth of March, 1996, and opened this trunk, and put a postcard in it, and closed it again.

"That's his hand," said Vivienne, from the doorway. Her voice was very quiet and very steady. "That's my father's hand."

She came into the room and took the postcard from June, and held it under the lamp, and looked at it for a long time. Her face did several things in a row, each of them very fast. Archie had seen her play Lady Macbeth and Mother Courage and, once, unforgettably, a talking teapot, and he had never in his life seen her face do what it did now.

"That old *showman,*" said Vivienne Fairweather.

She sat down very suddenly on the edge of Gus's desk chair.

"I told you," she said, to nobody, to the room. "I told you all. Nobody had opened it. I told you to draw your own conclusions."

Archie looked down at the cream-colored note in his own hand. *You remembered. That's the whole trick.*

And he did remember — not something from 1988, not something from a kitchen with a screen door, but something from the night before. The boat. The fog. Hollis going into the black water off Shag Ledge. His own hands, without any instruction from him at all, unclasping his grandfather's opera cloak and folding it — lining out, shoulders tucked in, in thirds, the collar on top, the silver clasp centered — and laying it on the seat beside the wheel before he went over the side.

He'd done it without thinking. Because that was what you did, if you'd grown up at Gus Fairweather's knee. That was what you did with a good cloak before you went to work. You didn't leave it in a heap.

Thirty-seven years ago, at dawn, Walt Calloway's men had found Gus's skiff drifting near Merrow Light with nobody in it. And on the seat, they said — it was in every story anyone ever told about that night, it was the detail that made people shiver — his black opera cloak, folded.

Folded.

A man who goes over the side by accident in the dark doesn't stop to fold his cloak.

Archie looked up from the note. His mother was holding the postcard against her chest with both hands. June was kneeling by the open trunk in the lamplight, looking up at him — and her face wasn't mad at him at all just then. It was lit up from the inside like a struck match, exactly the way his own must have been when he was four years old and a big man with silver hair crouched down in front of him in a summer kitchen and said *Watch closely, Sparrow.*

He felt the grin start somewhere around his ribs, and he didn't try to stop it.

"Junebug," said Archie Fairweather. "Get your coat."

June was already on her feet.

"Where are we going?"

"Sea View," said Archie, reaching for the opera cloak. "There's a man there I need to see about a boat."
